INTRODUCTORY STATEMENT TO THE READER: 

This book has grown out of my experience in trying cases, in appealing and defending them on appeal, and in lecturing to students in a law school on appellate practice. My purpose in preparing it is to state clearly, accurately and concisely that practice, as it now is, in the State of Indiana, and to state it in such a way that the student and the beginner as well as the experienced lawyer may know what it is, where to find it, and how to use it. 

Indianapolis, Indiana 

September 15, 1934

ISAAC CARTER

INDIANA APPELLATE PRACTICE FOUNDATION FOR AN APPEAL. 

The foundation for a successful appeal to either the Supreme or Appellate Court of this State is laid in the trial court, because if the trial court has jurisdiction of the subject matter and the parties to the suit, the case will not be reversed unless the party appealing presents to the court, to which the appeal is taken, a record made in the trial court, showing that he excepted to and complains of the decision of that court on a question of law. 

For these reasons it is absolutely necessary, in order that one may be able properly to try a case and present it on appeal, that he should understand the fundamental law on which a successful appeal depends. That law will first be considered, and then the law of appeal proper. 

Unless otherwise stated, the law so herein set forth does not refer to criminal actions, and all references to the Appellate Court are to the Indiana Appellate Court. 

AN APPEAL. 

There is no right of appeal unless it is given by statute. 

Brown v. Brown, 168 Ind. 654, 80 N. E. 535.

If the court has jurisdiction of the parties and the subject matter of the suit, no appeal to either the Supreme 1

THE LAW 

or Appellate Court can be successfully maintained unless the record shows an exception taken by the party appealing, and except in certain interlocutory orders specified in the statute, it shows a final judgment. An exception is an objection taken to a decision of the court on a matter of law. Burns 1933, 2—3101; Burns 1926, 681; Baldwin, 454. A final judgment is one that disposes of all of the issues as to all the parties so far as the court has power so to do. Keller v. Jordan, 147 Ind. 113, 46 N. E. 343; 

Emmeier v. Blaize, 203 Ind. 303. An interlocutory order is an order made by the court concerning some matter involved in the case before the final judgment is rendered. 

Bouvier. The record is the legal history of the case prepared by the clerk, and all appeals are tried by the record. Watson v. Finch, 150 Ind. 183, 48 N. E. 245.

THE LAW. 

Both civil and criminal law is a rule of conduct prescribed by the supreme power in a state, commanding what is right and prohibiting what is wrong. Blackstone, Vol. 1, p. 44; Burns 1933, 9-2401; Burns 1926, 2400; 

Baldwin, 2.

The supreme power in a state is vested in the people and the laws prescribed by them are co-extensive with the territory of the state, but have no extra-territorial force. Burns 1933, Ind. Const. Art. 1, § 1; Burns 1926, 53; Baldwin Ind. Const. Art. 1, §1; Burns 1933, Ind. 

Const. Art. 14, § 2; Burns 1926, 229; Baldwin, Ind. Const. 

Art. 14, § 2; Carnahan v. Western Union, 89 Ind. 526. 

The boundaries of the state are specifically set forth in 

Burns 1933, Ind. Const. Art. 14, §1; Burns 1926, 228;

A COURT 

3 

Baldwin Ind. Const. Art. 14, § 1; Burns 1933, Ind. Const. 

Art. 14, § 2; Burns 1926, 229; Baldwin Ind. Const. Art. 14, 

§ 2. 

The people have prescribed the following as the law of this state:

First. The Constitution of the United States and of this state.

Second. All statutes of the General Assembly of this state in force, and not inconsistent with such constitutions. 

Third. All statutes of the United States in force, and relating to subjects over which Congress has power to legislate for the states, and not inconsistent with the constitution of the United States.

Fourth. The common law of England, and the Statutes of the British Parliament made in aid thereof prior to the fourth year of the reign of James the First (1607) (except certain designated sections) and which are of a general nature, not local to that Kingdom and not inconsistent with the first, second and third specifications of this section, Burns 1933, 1–101; Burns 1926, 244; Baldwin, 1.

Fifth. The Constitution of the United States, Section 

23 makes treaties entered into by the United States a part of the law of this state. 

A COURT. 

A court is a place where justice as declared by the people, in the law they have enacted, is judicially administered by men and women acting as officers, viz., judges and jurors, assisted by attorneys, a clerk and a sheriff.

A COURT 

Blackstone, Vol. 3, p. 23; Burns 1933, Ind. Const. Art. 7, 

§ 2; Burns 1926, 169; Baldwin Ind. Const. Art. 7, § 2; 

Burns 1933, Ind. Const. Art. 7, §8; Burns 1926, 175; 

Baldwin Ind. Const. Art. 7, § 8; Burns 1933, Ind. Const. 

Art. 1, § 19; Burns 1926, 71; Baldwin Ind. Const. Art. 1, § 19; Burns 1933, Ind. Const. Art. 1, § 20; Burns 1926, 72; 

Baldwin Ind. Const. Art. 1, § 72; Burns 1933, Ind. Const. 

Art. 7, § 11; Burns 1926, 178; Baldwin Ind. Const. Art. 7, § 11; Burns 1933, Ind. Const. Art. 7, § 21, Burns 1926, 188, Baldwin Ind. Const. Art. 7, § 21; Burns 1933, Ind. Const. 

Art. 7, §7; Burns 1926, 174; Baldwin Ind. Const. Art. 7, § 7; Burns 1933, Ind. Const. Art. 6, § 2; Burns 1926, 159; 

Baldwin Ind. Const. Art. 6, § 2. 

The judicial power of the state is vested in a supreme court, in circuit courts and in such other courts as the General Assembly may establish. Burns 1933, Ind. 

Const. Art. 7, § 1; Burns 1926, 168; Baldwin Ind. Const. 

Art. 7, § 1. 

The duties of the judges are to take the official oath, to open and adjourn court, to regulate the order of business and preside during the transaction of business, to conserve the peace, to decide all questions of law and fact properly presented to them, and to sign the record. 

Burns 1933, Ind. Const. Art. 15, § 4; Burns 1926, 233; 

Baldwin, Ind Const. Art. 15, § 4; Burns 1933, 49-101; 

Burns 1926, 11556; Baldwin, 13054; Burns 1933, 2—1101; 

Burns 1926, 428; Baldwin, 177; Burns 1933, 2—1102; 

Burns 1926, 429; Baldwin, 178; Burns 1933, 2—1106; 

Burns 1926, 433; Baldwin, 182; Burns 1933, 2-1204; 

Burns 1926, 437; Baldwin, 186; Burns 1933, Ind Const.

Art. 7, § 15; Burns 1926, 182; Baldwin Ind. Const. Art. 7, 

§ 15; Burns 1933, 2-2102; Burns 1926, 603; Baldwin, 360; Burns 1933, 4-324; Burns 1926, 1397; Baldwin, 1413. 

The duties of jurors are to attend the court and to "well and truly try the matter in issue between the parties and a true verdict give according to the law and the evidence." Burns 1933, 2-2007; Burns 1926, 583; Baldwin, 338.

A COURT 

5 The duties of an attorney as an officer of the court, are that he "will support the constitution of the United States and of this state and will faithfully and honestly discharge the duties of an attorney-at-law." Burns 1933, 4-3606; Burns 1926, 1036; Baldwin, 828. These duties are specifically set forth in Burns 1933, 4-3608; Burns 

1926, 1038; Baldwin 830.

The duties of the Clerk of the Supreme Court are to reside at the seat of government and keep his office open in a building provided for that purpose by the state from the hour of 9 a. m. until 4 p. m. of every day in the year, Sundays and Fourth of July excepted and the days named in Burns 1933, 19—1916; Burns 1926, 11357, Baldwin, 9259, and except also Saturdays from twelve o'clock noon; procure and preserve in such office all records and other books and stationery required by the court; attend in person or by deputy the terms of such court; administer all oaths authorized by law; sign and seal with his seal and issue all process required to be issued from such court under his hand; indorse the time of filing any books, records or writings required to be filed, or deposited in his office; and to make a complete record of all causes finally determined in such court, except the transcript of the court below. Burns 1933, 49-2102; Burns 1926, 11745; Baldwin, 1330; Burns 1933, 49-728; Burns 1926, 12101; Baldwin, 9258.

The duties of the clerk of the Circuit Court are to keep his office open at the county seat every day in the year, Sundays and the Fourth of July excepted and the days named in Burns 1933, above referred to, between the hours of 9 a. m. and 4 p. m., and to be there in person or by deputy; to indorse on all writings required to be filed in his office, the time of filing thereof; carefully preserve in such office all records and writings appertaining to his official duties; procure at the expense of the county, all necessary judges', appearance, bar, judgment and execution dockets, and order and final record books; attend in person or by deputy the circuit, municipal, criminal, superior and probate courts of such county; and enter in 2

JURISDICTION 

the proper record book all orders, judgments and decrees of such court and has power to administer oaths; Burns 

1933, 49-2705; Burns 1926, 11844; Baldwin, 1432; Burns 

1933, 49-2706; Burns 1926, 11845; Baldwin, 1433; 

Burns 1933, 4-2508; Burns 1926, 1731; Baldwin, 1723; 

Burns 1933, 4-2904; Burns 1926, 1756; Baldwin, 1780; Burns 1933, Ind. Const. Art. 1, § 19; Burns 1926, 

71; Baldwin, Ind. Const. Art. 1, § 19; Burns 1933, 4—1710; 

Burns 1926, 1589; Baldwin, 1629; Burns 1933, 4-2302; 

Burns 1926, 1683; Baldwin, 1745; Burns 1926, 11847.

The duties of the sheriff of the Supreme Court are by himself, or deputy, to "attend such court in term time, execute all the orders thereof, preserve order therein, and execute, by himself or deputy all process issued out of such court." Burns 1933, 49-2202; Burns 1926, 11753; Baldwin, 1338.

The duties of the county sheriff are to conserve the peace, arrest, without process, all persons who, within his view, shall commit any crime or misdemeanor, execute all process directed to him by legal authority, either in person or by deputy, attend upon and preserve order in all courts of record in his county, except justices' courts, and serve all process directed to him from the circuit court. Burns 1933, 49–2802; Burns 1926, 11859; 

Baldwin, 5494.

JURISDICTION. 

The jurisdiction of a court, or tribunal, is the authority it has to hear and determine a cause, and it is given either by constitutional or legislative enactment. Robertson v. The State, 109 Ind. 79, 10 N. E. 582; Burns 1933,

JURISDICTION 

7 

Ind. Const. Art. 7, § 1; Burns 1926, 168; Baldwin, Ind. 

Const. Art. 7, § 1. 

That a court may have jurisdiction of a cause it is necessary for it to have jurisdiction of the subject matter, parties, territory and amount involved. If it has not such jurisdiction it has no power to act in the cause and everything it does is void. Jackson v. Smith, 120 Ind. 

520, 22 N. E. 431; Pease v. State, 74 App. 572, 129 N. E. 

337. Courts established by the constitution cannot be abolished and the jurisdiction so given them cannot be taken away by statute. The following are the courts so established and the jurisdiction so given:

The supreme court and its jurisdiction is co-extensive with the limits of the state in appeals. It has such original jurisdiction as the general assembly may confer. 

Burns 1933, Ind. Const. Art. 7, §4; Burns 1926, 171; 

Baldwin, Ind. Const. Art. 7, § 4. The circuit court and its jurisdiction in both civil and criminal cases is such as may be prescribed by law. Burns 1933, Ind. Const. 

Art. 7, 8; Burns 1926, 175; Baldwin, Ind. Const. Art. 7, 

§ 8; Burns 1933, 4-303; Burns 1926, 1376; Baldwin, 1397.

The court of the justice of the peace and its jurisdiction, duties and powers are such as may be prescribed by 

Statute, Burns 1933, Ind. Const. Art. 7, § 14; Burns 1926, 181; Baldwin, Ind. Const. Art. 7, § 14; Houk v. Board, 14 

App. 662, 21 N. E. 1068; The State v. Noble, 118 Ind. 350, 21 N. E. 244. All other courts are of legislative origin. * * "Where the tribunal has jurisdiction of a general class of cases, every case of that class is within the subject of the tribunal's jurisdiction, for by the term 'subject' can only be meant cases of a general class. 

There is therefore a difference between the subject of the court's jurisdiction and the subject matter of the particular action. For the subject of the court's jurisdiction is a class, but the subject matter of a particular case is a single thing. *

Where it appears that the case is one of a general class over which the tribunal has jurisdiction * the judgment is not absolutely void if the particular subject was within the territorial jurisdiction of the court, and there was juris- 

---

JURISDICTION 

diction of the person." "Jackson v. Smith, 120 Ind. 520-2-3, 22 N. E. 431; State v. Wolever, 127 Ind. 306-15, 26 N. E. 762.

The territorial jurisdiction of the Supreme Court is defined by the constitution, but the territorial jurisdiction of all the other courts in this state is defined by the legislature. Such jurisdiction of the appellate court seems to be the same as that of the supreme court, Burns 

1933, 4-209; Burns 1926, 1351; Baldwin, 1364, but such jurisdiction of all the other courts is limited to the county in which the court is established, including such causes as come to them on change of venue from other counties. 

Sherry v. Winton, 1 Ind. 96; Huber v. Robinson, 23 Ind. 

137; Loeb v. Mathias, 37 Ind. 306.

If the action is in rem the territorial jurisdiction is in the county in which the property is situated. Burns 1933, 2-701; Burns 1926, 323; Baldwin, 75; Quarl v. Abbett, 102 Ind. 233, 1 N. E. 476; if in personam it is in the county in which the defendant resides, or if more than one, where one of them resides. Burns 1933, 2-707; 

Burns 1926, 329; Baldwin, 81. The statute provides where particular cases must be brought. Burns 1933, 2-702; Burns 1926, 324; Baldwin, 76; Burns 1933, 2-703; Burns 1926, 325; Baldwin, 77; Burns 1933, 2-704; Burns 1926, 326; Baldwin, 78; Burns 1933, 2-705; Burns 1926, 327; Baldwin, 79; Burns 1933, 2-706; Burns 1926, 328; Baldwin, 80; Burns 1933, 2-707; Burns 1926, 329; Baldwin, 81; Burns 1933, 2-708; Burns 1926, 330; Baldwin, 82. Jurisdiction of the subject matter can not be given by consent, but if there is jurisdiction of the general class of cases to which the particular case belongs, jurisdiction of the territory or of the person may be given by consent, or waived by failing to make timely and specific objections. Center Twp. v. The Board, 110 Ind. 579, 10 N. E. 291; Tucker Twp. v. Board, 130 Ind. 514, 30 

N. E. 531; Mankin v. Pennsylvania Co., 160 Ind. 447, 67 N. E. 229; Cox v. Pruitt, 25 Ind. 90; American Life 

Ins. Co. v. Mason, 159 Ind. 15, 64 N. E. 525.

JURISDICTION 

9 Want of jurisdiction of the subject matter and of the person, if it appears on the face of the complaint, is raised by demurrer. Burns 1933, 2—1007; Burns 1926, 362; Baldwin, 111. If it does not so appear, it is raised by answer, and if not so raised, it is waived except as to the jurisdiction of the subject matter. If the action is brought in the wrong county, the question is waived unless raised by demurrer, or answer. 

Burns 1933, 2-1011; Burns 1926, 366; Baldwin, 115. Pittsburgh v. 

Home Ins. Co., 73 App. 226, 125 N. E. 427.

If the defect is in the process, the question is raised by a written motion specifically setting forth the defects.

In no case is it necessary for the party questioning the jurisdiction to say he enters a special appearance. The paper he files shows what it is and it must contain only such facts as show that there is no jurisdiction. Whitesides v. Drage, 56 App. 679, 106 N. E. 382. 

Unless waived, jurisdiction of the party or parties. defendant is secured by summons or publication. The summons is issued by the clerk to the sheriff and must be served by him or his deputy, personally on the defendant or by leaving a copy of it at his usual or last place of residence. Burns 1933, 2-802; Burns 1926, 332; Baldwin, 83; Burns 1933, 2-803; Burns 1926, 333; 

Baldwin, 84. If a defendant is a non-resident, or his residence is unknown, jurisdiction of him may be secured by publication when the statute so provides. Burns 1933, 2-807; Burns 1926, 338; Baldwin, 88.

Process may be waived by the party entering an appearance but this must be by some formal entry, or plea, or motion, or official act. Eaton Tr. v. Union Co. National Bank, 141 Ind. 159-163, 40 N. E. 693, and which constitutes a part of the record in the cause. McCormack v. The First Natl. Bank, 53 Ind. 466. Acknowledgment of service entered on the back of the summons gives jurisdiction. McCormack supra. No personal judgment can be rendered against a defendant where the only service is constructive. Quarl v. Abbett, 102 

Ind. 233, 1 N. E. 476.

THE RECORD 

If a court has a judge, a clerk and a seal it is presumed to be a court of general jurisdiction; if it has not these, it is a court of limited jurisdiction. Everything is presumed in favor of the jurisdiction of the former and nothing in favor of the jurisdiction of the latter. For there to be no jurisdiction in the former, the record must so show; in the latter, for there to be jurisdiction, the record must show every fact necessary to jurisdiction. 

Eel River v. State, 143 Ind. 231, 42 N. E. 617; Cobb v. 

The State, 27 Ind. 133. If the court has no jurisdiction of either the person or subject matter, the question may be raised at any time, by court or counsel. Huber v. 

Beck, 6 App. 47, 32 N. E. 1025.

THE RECORD. 

All appeals to either the Supreme or Appellate Court are tried by the record. Campbell v. State, 148 Ind. 527, 47 N. E. 221. The record is a chronological statement, made by the clerk of the court in which the cause is pending, of all that is done in it and of which he obtains information from the court, or by being filed in his office, written in books authorized and directed by law to be kept in his office in his custody and verified by the signature of the judge. Burns 1933, 2-1103; Burns 

1926, 430; Baldwin, 179; Burns 1933, 4—324; Burns 1926, 1397; Baldwin, 1413; Burns 1933, 49-2706; Burns 1926, 11845; Baldwin, 1433. It includes every pleading, motion in writing, report, deposition, or other paper filed or offered to be filed in any cause, or proceeding, whether received by the court, refused, or stricken out, any order

THE RECORD 

11 or action of the court in respect to any such pleading, motion in writing, report, deposition, or other paper, and every exception thereto taken by any party. The same shall be entered on the minutes of the court and when so entered, shall be a part of the record without any bill of exceptions. Every oral motion and the ruling of the court thereon and the exception taken thereto, shall be entered upon the record or minutes of the court and shall be a part of the record without any bill of exceptions. Burns 1933, 2—3104; Burns 1926, 684; Baldwin, 462.

Every motion to insert new matter, or to strike out any part or parts of any pleading, deposition, report or paper in a cause, shall be made in writing and shall set forth the words sought to be inserted or struck out. 

Burns 1933, 2-1069; Burns 1926, 424; Baldwin, 461.

This provision is mandatory. Lehman v. City of Goshen, 178 Ind. 54, 98 N. E. 1. Nothing stated above shall be construed to prevent the bringing into or putting into the record by proper bill or bills of exceptions any matter which under the common law would not be a part of the record without a bill of exceptions. Burns 1933, 2—3104; 

Burns 1926, 684; Baldwin, 462. As to what is a part of the record at common law see Blackstone Vol. 1, 69; 

Vol. 3, 24, 317. The above does not refer to criminal actions. Papers not a part of the record and which are used as evidence, on the trial are not part of the record without a bill of exceptions, Blackstone Book 3, p. 372; but if they are a part of the record and are used as evidence it is sufficient to refer to them in the bill of exceptions and state that they were admitted in evidence. 

Henry v. Thomas, Ex., 118 Ind. 23, 20 N. E. 519; Miller v. Wayne, etc., 32 App. 480-486, 70 N. E. 180.

It is provided in Acts 1933, p. 1039 as follows concerning criminal cases :- "The bill of exceptions must contain so much of the evidence as is necessary to present the questions of law upon which the exceptions were taken; but it shall not be necessary for the bill to contain all of the evidence given in

THE RECORD 

the cause or proceeding, unless the decision of the court, or verdict of the jury, shall be called in question as being contrary to law, or not sustained by sufficient evidence. When any matter may be brought into the record by a bill of exceptions, the original bill of exceptions, whether containing the evidence or any other matter, may be incorporated into the transcript of the record of such cause on appeal to the supreme court or the appellate court, and such original bill of exceptions shall, in every case, constitute and be considered as a part of such transcript, the same as if copied therein by the clerk, whether such original bill or a copy thereof be specified in the praecipe, or otherwise directed to be incorporated into such transcript; PROVIDED, HOWEVER, That if the original written instructions as given and/or refused by the court shall be incorporated into any bill of exceptions, the court shall certify in his certificate to such bill of exceptions that such original written instructions are incorporated therein and the bill containing such original written instructions shall be copied into the transcript by the clerk; PROVIDED, That every pleading, motion in writing, report, deposition or other paper, filed or offered to be filed, in any cause or proceeding, whether received by the court, refused or stricken out, shall be a part of the record from the time of such filing or offer to file; and any order or action of the court in respect to any such pleading, motion in writing, report, deposition or other paper, and every exception thereto taken by any party shall be entered by the clerk on the minutes or record of the court, and the same when so entered shall be a part of the record without any bill of exceptions. Every oral motion and the ruling of the court thereon and the exceptions taken thereto shall be entered upon the minutes or record of the court and shall be a part of the record without any bill of exception; PRO- 

VIDED, FURTHER, That a motion to strike out or insert any matter, and the ruling thereon, shall not be a part of the record without a bill of exceptions, unless the matter to be stricken out or inserted is set out in full in the motion and, PROVIDED FURTHER, That nothing herein shall be con-

THE RECORD 

13 strued to prevent the bringing into or putting into the record by a proper bill of exceptions any matter, ruling or exception, except the indictment or affidavit, the motion to quash, the pleading in answer to such indictment or affidavit, the verdict, the motion and reasons for a new trial and the final judgment."

So far as the above act is the same as sections 684 and 424 Burns 1926 concerning the record in civil cases, the decisions of the courts on said sections presumably will be the same on the corresponding portions of said act. 

On appeal the record consists of a transcript-copyof the record in the trial court and the record of the proceedings in the court to which the appeal is taken, except that the evidence included in the bill of exceptions may be certified without copying, and possibly also the instructions, if a copy of the same is included in a bill. Acts 1933, p. 918; Burns 1933, 2-3112; Burns 1926, 692; Baldwin, 466; Burns 1933, 2-3204; Burns 1926, 698; Baldwin, 480; Burns 1933, 2—3205; Burns 1926, 699; 

Baldwin, 481; Burns 1933, 2-3206; Burns 1926, 700; 

Baldwin, 482; Burns 1933, 2-3222; Burns 1926, 716; 

Baldwin, 496; Burns 1933, 2-3223; Burns 1926, 717; 

Baldwin, 497.

It is settled that in order to make the instructions a part of the record in a civil case, without a bill of exceptions, they must be filed in open Court and the record must affirmatively show they were so filed. The filing in open Court of any paper in a case as answer, demurrer, or reply, which by such filing becomes a part of the record proper, must be shown by an order book entry, made as a part of the proceedings in the cause. A thing done in open Court which must be proved by an order book entry as a part of the proceedings in a cause, cannot be shown by a recital in the certificate of the clerk. Thompson v. Thompson, 156 Ind. 276, 59 N. E. 845. 

That which is not a part of the record by virtue of the law cannot be made a part of the record by agreement.

THE RECORD Davis v. Union Trust Co., 150 Ind. 46, 49 N. E. 817. No original paper, document or record embodied in a transcript will be considered by the court, except the original bill of exceptions containing the evidence, but under the Act of 1933 the instructions possibly may be certified, without copying if a copy of the same is included in the bill. If the original instructions are included in the bill, the clerk must copy them. Mitchell v. Beissenherz, 192 

Ind. 587, 135 N. E. 885. Every court takes judicial knowledge of the record of a case pending in it until it is finally disposed of. Graves v. Kelly, 62 App. 164-170, 112 N. E. 899.

Correcting the Record. The power of a court over its judgments, during the entire term at which they are rendered is unlimited and the proceedings in a cause remain in fieri until the end of the term at which the motion for a new trial, though filed after judgment, is ruled upon, and in the meantime the court may alter, amend, or set aside its former rulings, orders and judgment without notice to the parties. Livingston v. Livingston, 190 Ind. 223, 130 N. E. 122; Shaw v. Union Trust 

Company, 79 App. 277, 137 N. E. 895; Moore v. Moore, 81 App. 169, 135 N. E. 362.

Courts have the inherent power to make their records speak the truth and they may do so after the end of the term at which the cause ceased to be in fieri. But the correction can be only to show what the court did and which by mistake the clerk failed to record, and the correction cannot be made on oral testimony, but must be made on some memorandum, memorial paper, record or other minute of the transaction to amend by, of a date prior or at least of equal date as the record sought to be amended. Taylor v. State, 191 Ind. 200, 132 N. E. 

294; Holcomb v. Norman, 43 App. 506, 87 N. E. 1057.

Whatever is used as a memorandum to correct by must be a part of the record. Miller v. Royse, 60 Ind. 189; 

Chicago, etc. v. Smith, 74 App. 336, 129 N. E. 49. However, the Supreme Court of this state, February 12,

MOTION TO CORRECT RECORD 

15 1934 in the case of Illinois Pipe Line Company v. Charles 

Fitzpatrick, 188 N. E. Rep., No. 8 p. 771 said, "the decisions of this court construed in the light of learned and respected authority may be properly interpreted as establishing the rule that where the record fails to show a judgment was rendered, order made, or that action was taken, the fact that such action was taken can only be established nunc pro tunc after the term by evidence consisting of some written memorial or record but that when the fact that a judgment was rendered or some other action taken is so established parol proof may be resorted to for the purpose of showing the character, terms and conditions of the action taken."

The proceeding to correct the record is by motion and all parties against whom the order was made should be parties, but if any of them refuse to join such should be served with notice of the motion. Pritchard v. Mines. 

56 App. 671, 106 N. E. 411.

AN AGREED CASE. 

"Parties shall have the right in all cases, either with or without process, by agreement to that effect, to submit any matter of controversy between them to any Court that would otherwise have jurisdiction of such cause, upon an agreed statement of the facts, to be made out and signed by the parties; but it must appear by affidavit that the controversy is real and the proceedings in good faith to determine the rights of the parties; whereupon the Court shall proceed to try the same and render judgment as in other cases. Burns 

1933, 2-2201; Burns 1926, 604; Baldwin, 362.

The statement of the case, the submission, and the judgment shall constitute the record. Burns 1933, 2-2202; 

Burns 1926, 605; Baldwin, 363.

The judgment may be enforced in the same manner as if it had been rendered in an action, and, in like manner, shall be with costs, subject to an appeal, unless otherwise provided in the submission." Burns 1933, 2-2203; Burns 

1926, 606; Baldwin, 364.

AN AGREED CASE 

17 The following form for presentation to the court of an agreed case seems to meet the requirements of the statute:

STATE OF INDIANA MARION COUNTY 

A.B. V. C.D. 

Agreed Case

MARION CIRCUIT COURT MAY TERM 1933. 

In the above entitled cause we make known to the court that we have a controversy over our rights growing out of the following facts: (here set out the facts)

And we ask the court to determine our rights on such facts: 

STATE OF INDIANA 

A. B. 

C. D.

MARION COUNTY 

A. B. (or C. D. or A. B. & C. D.) on his oath says he is the plaintiff in the above cause; that the controversy between him and C. D., as above set forth is real and the proceedings in this case as above stated are in good faith. 

Subscribed and sworn before me this 1933. 

A. B. day of 

Notary Public.

There should be a caption to the statement, showing who is plaintiff and who is defendant. Smith v. Junction, etc., 29 Ind. 546. In order that the plaintiff may win, the statement must show a cause of action in his favor. Templeton v. Board, 173 Ind. 226, 89 N. E. 880; and the agreement must be sworn to by one or both of the parties unless the party in whose behalf the affidavit

THE PLEADINGS 

is made is a corporation. Booth v. Cottingham, 126 Ind. 

431, 26 N. E. 84; State v. Coughlen, 86 Ind. 404. Pleadings are not required and if filed should be disregarded, Day v. Day, 100 Ind. 460, and if they are filed with an agreed statement of the facts it will not be regarded as an agreed case, Penn. Co. v. Niblack, 99 Ind. 149, but if both sides treat it as an agreed case, the Court will so treat it. Booth v. Cottingham, 126 Ind. 431, 26 N. E. 

84. It is not necessary to move for a new trial, but there must be an exception to the decision. Warrick Co. v. Houghlan, 90 Ind. 115. On appeal the case will be decided on the agreed statement without considering the findings of the Court or its action and no presumptions will be indulged in its favor. Jennings v. Hembree, 71 

App. 370, 124 N. E. 876.

THE PLEADINGS. 

The pleadings are the formal written allegations of the parties of their claims and defenses made for the judgment of the court. Burns 1933, 2-1001; Burns 1926, 356; Baldwin, 106. All must be signed by the parties or their attorneys. Burns 1933, 2—1027; Burns 1926, 382; 

Baldwin, 131. The signature may be typewritten. Boes v. Grand Rapids R. Co., 59 App. 271, 108 N. E. 174.

The pleadings consist of the complaint by the plaintiff; the demurrer and answer by the defendant and the demurrer and reply by the plaintiff. Burns 1933, 2—1003; 

Burns 1926, 358; Baldwin, 108.

A civil action is commenced by filing in the office of the clerk a complaint and causing summons to issue

THE PLEADINGS 

19 thereon or publication to be made. Burns 1933, 2-802; 

Burns 1926, 332; Baldwin, 83. 

The complaint shall contain: Burns 1933, 2—1004; 

Burns 1926, 359; Baldwin, 110: "First. The title of the cause, specifying the name of the court, the county in which the action is brought and the names of the parties to the action, plaintiff and defendant.' 

The party complaining is the plaintiff and the adverse party is the defendant. Burns 1933, 2-102; 

Burns 1926, 257; Baldwin, 15. The following complies with the statute: "In the Marion Circuit Court 

Marion County, Indiana 

John Smith V. 

John Jones."

If the plaintiff is ignorant of the true name of the defendant, he may be designated in any pleading or proceeding by any name, and when the true name is discovered, the pleading or proceeding may be amended accordingly, either before or after service. Burns 1933, 2-1070; Burns 1926, 425; Baldwin, 174.

The proper method of objecting that the defendant has been sued by the wrong name is by a plea in abatement giving the right name. Adams Express Co. v. State, 161 

Ind. 328, 67 N. E. 1033. If no objection is made that the defendant is sued by the wrong name and the defendant pleads by his true name, the error is waived. Board v. 

Huffman, 134 Ind. 1, 31 N. E. 570. If a party sued by a wrong name is served and appears to the action, he may on motion at any time during the trial have the proper name inserted and there is then no cause for abating the action. Boland v. Caudell, 181 Ind. 295, 104 

N. E. 577. If the court or county is not stated and if the pleadings are not signed, these are matters of form, can be reached only by motion to strike out, may be cured by amendment and if not amended will be disregarded

THE PLEADINGS 

on appeal. Smith v. Flack, 95 Ind. 116; Brandies v. 

Grisson, 26 App. 661, 60 N. E. 455; Lentz v. Martin, 75 

Ind. 228; Plew v. Jones, 165 Ind. 21, 74 N. E. 618. If the names of the parties, or court, or state, are given in the title it is not necessary to repeat them in the body of the pleading. Voyles v. Hinds, 186 Ind. 38, 114 N. E. 

865; nor in the several paragraphs of a pleading, Chicago, etc. v. Thomas Adm., 147 Ind. 35, 46 N. E. 73; and failure to state the name of the court is immaterial. 

Citizens etc. Co. v. Shepherd, 30 App. 193, 65 N. E. 765.

A name consists of the Christian and surname. Schofield v. Jennings, 68 Ind. 232. Suit by partners can not be in the partnership name. The partners must sue in their individual names and by such names they must be sued. Karges Furn. Co. v. Amalgamated, etc., 165 Ind. 

421, 75 N. E. 877. A corporation must sue in its corporate name and need not aver in the complaint that it is such or how it became so. 

Co., 9 App. 282, 36 N. E. 455. not appear in the complaint, the by a demurrer for want of facts. Bascomb v. Toner, 5 App. 229, 31 N. E. 856.

Shearer v. R. S. Peale 

If the legal name does question may be raised "Second. A statement of the facts constituting the cause of action, in plain and concise language, without repetition, and in such manner as to enable a person of common understanding to know what is intended."

This part of the complaint can be questioned only in one or more of three ways, as follows: 

1. By a motion to strike out and it is never error to overrule such a motion but it may be error to sustain it. Holland v. Holland, 131 Ind. 196, 30 N. E. 1075; 

Faylor v. Brice, 7 App. 551, 34 N. E. 833. 

2. By a motion to make more specific. "When the allegations of a pleading are so indefinite or uncertain that the precise nature of the charge or defense is not apparent, the court may require the pleading to be made definite and certain by amendment." Burns 1933, 2-1048; Burns 1926, 403; Baldwin, 152,

THE PLEADINGS 

21 3 It is error to overrule a motion to make a pleading more specific, if the pleading is so indefinite and uncertain that the precise nature of the charge or defense is not apparent. Tipton Light Co. v. Newcomer, 156 Ind. 

348, 58 N. E. 842; A. J. Yawger Co. v. Joseph, 184 Ind. 

228, 108 N. E. 774. 

3. By demurrer. The defendant may demur to the complaint when it appears upon the face thereof either "First. That the court has no jurisdiction of the person of the defendant, or the subject of the action." 

Jurisdiction is the power given to a court by law to hear and determine a cause, Leathers v. Hogan, 17 Ind. 

242, and is of two kinds of the person and of the subject matter. Robertson v. State, 109 Ind. 79, 10 N. E. 

582. If there is no jurisdiction of either the person or subject matter, the court has no power to proceed and anything it may attempt to do is a nullity and if the lack of jurisdiction appears on the record, the question may be raised at any time by motion or by demurrer, and the court may on its own motion dismiss the case. Robertson v. The State, 109 Ind. 79, 10 N. E. 582; Louisville, etc. v. Johnson, 67 Ind. 546; Huber v. Beck, 6 App. 47, 32 N. E. 1025. Jurisdiction over the person may be given by consent, and is if the parties enter their appearance in the case without objection by filing a paper in the case, or by being silent when they should speak. Jelly v. Gaff, 56 Ind. 331. If there is no legal process by which the defendant is brought into court and no appearance, and judgment is taken by default, an appeal may be taken by the defaulted party without any proceeding in the trial court. Odell v. Carpenter, 71 Ind. 463; and if in such a case an appeal is taken and the record does not show a legal summons properly served, the cause will be reversed. Cole v. Allen, 51 Ind. 122. 

The question of jurisdiction over the person in the trial court is raised by a special appearance and moving to quash the process for reasons stated. If the process

22 

is defective, such motion must be made before entering a general appearance. Pittsburgh, etc. v. Hodge, 175 

Ind. 669, 94 N. E. 324.

MOTION TO QUASH PROCESS. 

STATE OF INDIANA JOHNSON COUNTY 

A.B. V. C.D.

JOHNSON CIRCUIT COURT MARCH TERM, 1933. 

In the above entitled cause the defendant enters this special appearance and moves to quash the summons in the same for the following reasons: 

1. The return of the Sheriff shows that the defendant was served by leaving a copy of the summons at the office of the defendant.. 

2. The return of the Sheriff shows the summons was served by reading the same to the defendant on the 10th day of March, 1933. The summons shows it was returnable on the 15th day of 

March, 1933. (State any other reasons.) 

E. F. 

Attorney for Defendant.

If a judgment is taken by default, the court on appeal cannot assume that anything was proved beyond what was alleged in the complaint, nor can the complaint be considered to have been amended to meet the proof. Old v. Mohler, 122 Ind. 594, 23 N. E. 967.

Jurisdiction of the subject matter is given to every court by statute and by it "is meant jurisdiction of the class of cases to which the particular case belongs." 

McCoy v. Able, 131 Ind. 417, 30 N. E. 528. If there is no jurisdiction the objection may be interposed at any time and requires for its exhibition neither formal motion or bill. McCoy v. Able, 131 Ind. 417, 30 N. E. 528.

If the court has jurisdiction of the subject matter, as

above defined, but by statute the exercise of such jurisdiction is confined to a certain county, and the parties agree that the cause may be transferred to the court of another county having such jurisdiction, this latter court has jurisdiction; or if the court announces that he will transfer it to the other court, the parties must at once state their objection to it being so transferred. Center 

Township v. The Board, 110 Ind. 579, 10 N. E. 291; 

Daniels v. Bruce, 176 Ind. 151, 95 N. E. 569. 

"Second. That the plaintiff has no legal capacity to sue.

Want of legal capacity means want of legal ability, as infancy, coverture, idiocy. Cuddington v. Canady, 157 

Ind. 243, 61 N. E. 567. However, the want of such capacity is also raised by a demurrer for want of facts. Hammond v. Cline, 170 Ind. 452, 84 N. E. 827. "Third. That there is another action pending between the same parties for the same cause. 

This question will not be raised by demurrer for want of facts and it refers only to actions pending in this state. Bayse v. Bayse, 152 Ind. 172; Catherwood v. Catherwood, 78 App. 332, 127 N. E. 816. "Fourth. That there is a defect of parties, plaintiff or defendant."

A defect of parties plaintiff means too few and not too many, is not raised by a demurrer for want of facts, is waived if not raised by demurrer, and the demurrer must name the parties. Carskaddon v. Rine, 154 Ind. 

410, 56 N. E. 844; The State v. McClelland, 138 Ind. 395, 37 N. E. 799. "Fifth. That the complaint does not state facts sufficient to constitute a cause of action."

The language of this specification should be used but if the equivalent of it is used it will be sufficient. Ross v. Menefee, 125 Ind. 432, 25 N. E. 545. It is not sufficient to say that the pleading does not state facts sufficient to constitute "a complaint"; Grubb v. King, 117 Ind. 243, 20 N. E. 142; nor "is not sufficient in law"; Gordon v.

Swift, 39 Ind. 213; nor "does not entitle the party to the relief demanded;" Kemp v. Mitchell, 29 Ind. 163. A demurrer for the above cause raises the question of the right of the plaintiff to maintain the action, Kinsley v. 

Kinsley, 150 Ind. 67, 49 N. E. 819; whether the plaintiff sues in his individual or representative capacity, Toner v. Wagner, 158 Ind. 447, 63 N. E. 859; that the action is prematurely brought, Middaugh v. Wilson, 30 App. 112, 65 N. E. 555; whether the plaintiffs have the right to sue jointly, Frankel v. Garrard, 160 Ind. 209, 66 N. E. 687; of the absence of a bill of particulars, or exhibit, Miller v. Bottenberg, 144 Ind. 312, 41 N. E. 804; of the failure to state the full name of one or more of the parties, Bascomb v. Toner, 5 App. 229, 31 N. E. 856.

A demurrer for this cause must be accompanied by a memorandum stating specifically wherein the pleading is insufficient for want of facts, and thereafter no other question can be raised by the demurring party. Sixth par. of Burns 1933, 2-1007; Burns 1926, 362; Baldwin, 111. The memorandum should state the reasons why the pleading is insufficient, without argument or repetition, and should follow the demurrer and be signed by the attorneys. State v. Bartholomew, 176 Ind. 182, 95 N. E. 417; State ex rel. v. Morris, 199 Ind. 78, 155 

N. E. 198. If there is no memorandum, or if there is, no question as to the sufficiency of the pleading can be raised on demurrer except the reasons stated in the memorandum, but if the pleading does not state a cause of action, the question can be raised on the proof, and if the proof does not make a cause of action, the plaintiff will be beaten. Prudential Ins. Co. v. Ritchey, 188 Ind. 

157, 119 N. E. 369. A party may recover on an insufficient complaint to which no demurrer has been filed, if the evidence tends to establish all the material averments thereof and in addition thereto such other facts as were necessary to its sufficiency. Angell v. Arnett, 81 App. 614, 142 N. E. 720. This requirement applies to demurrers addressed to affirmative answers, set-offs, counter-claims and replies. Quality Clothes Shop v.

Keeney, 57 App. 500, 106 N. E. 541; Pittsburgh, etc. v. 

Howe Ins. Co., 183 Ind. 355–70, 108 N. E. 525. "Sixth. That several causes of action have been improperly joined and for no other cause shall a demurrer be sustained."

Misjoinder is uniting of causes of action of different classes in the same complaint. These classes are defined in Burns 1933, 2—301; Burns 1926, 286; Baldwin, 45. "363. Where a demurrer is sustained on the ground of several causes of action being improperly joined in the same complaint the court shall order the misjoinder to be noted on the order-book and cause as many separate actions to be docketed between the parties as there are causes decided by the court to be improperly joined, and each shall stand as a separate action, and the plaintiff shall thereupon file a complaint in each of the above cases, to which the defendant shall enter his appearance and plead and go to trial or suffer a default, in the same manner as in the original action." "No judgment shall ever be reversed for any error committed in sustaining or overruling a demurrer for misjoinder of causes of action." Burns 1933, 2-1009; Burns 

1926, 364; Baldwin, 113.

If there are both a misjoinder of parties and of causes of action, the cause must be reversed. Tobin v. Connery, 13 Ind. 65. If a demurrer is sustained or overruled by the court, the party affected by such ruling may plead over or amend upon such terms as the court may direct and on payment of the costs occasioned by the demurrer. 

Burns 1933, 2-1010; Burns 1926, 365; Baldwin, 114.

The filing of an amended complaint or pleading after a ruling on demurrer, waives all precedent error on that pleading. Hedrick v. Whitehouse, 145 Ind. 642, 43 N. E. 

942; but for it so to do, the pleading to which the demurrer has been sustained must be amended. 

The complaint, or answer, or reply may contain more than one cause of action, or answer, or reply, but each must be distinctly stated in a separate paragraph and

numbered. Burns 1933, 2—1004; Burns 1926, 359; Baldwin, 110. Every paragraph of a pleading must proceed on a single definite theory. By this theory it must be judged and on this theory it must stand or fall. Bills v. Chicago etc. Co., 104 Ind. 13, 3 N. E. 611. This principle does not apply to claims against an estate, Taber v. Zehner, 47 App. 165, 93 N. E. 1035. 

Each paragraph must be good within itself, but exhibits filed with one pleading may be made a part of another pleading by reference. Farr v. Back, 13 App. 125, 41 N. E. 393; 

Brower v. Nellis, 6 App. 323, 33 N. E. 672. But one paragraph is necessary in a suit to foreclose a mortgage though many notes are secured by it. Mansfield v. 

Shipp, 128 Ind. 55, 27 N. E. 427; and but one alleging many breaches of a contract, Smiley v. Deweese, 1 App. 

211, 27 N. E. 505; and but one to recover a penalty for a continuous offense fixed at so much a day; Toledo etc. v. Stephensen, Trustee, 131 Ind. 203, 30 N. E. 1082.

Separate and distinct penalties should not be included in one paragraph. State v. Halter, 149 Ind. 292, 47 N. E. 

665. It is not reversible error to overrule a motion to separate causes of action stated in one paragraph of complaint. Aetna Ins. Co. v. Indiana etc. Co., 191 Ind. 

554, 133 N. E. 4.

In order to avoid needless repetitions in the pleadings, the pleader may sub-divide each paragraph of his pleading whether complaint, answer, or reply into distinct clauses and number them consecutively, and in any subsequent paragraph of such pleading, the pleader may incorporate in it by proper identification and reference, any clause or clauses found in any former paragraph without copying therein the same. Burns 1933, 2—1006; 

Burns 1926, 361; Baldwin, 109. "Hereafter, in all pleadings, papers or writings which are filed in or before any Court in any civil or criminal case, or in any proceeding of any kind, where the sufficiency of the same can, may be or is called in question, all recitals therein and all statements contained in any participial expression, or following the words 'having' or 'being,' shall be con-

sidered and held to be allegations of fact whenever necessary to the sufficiency thereof; and all conclusions stated therein shall be considered and held to be the allegation of all the facts required to sustain said conclusion when the same is necessary to the sufficiency of such pleading, paper or writing;

PROVIDED, that as against such conclusions, only the following remedy is given, that a motion may be made to require the party filing such pleading, paper or writing to state the facts necessary to sustain the conclusion alleged, said motion setting out wherein such pleading, paper or writing, is insufficient. If no such motion is made and ruled upon, all objections on account thereof are waived." Burns 

1933, 2—1005; Burns 1926, 360; Baldwin, 155.

This section refers to conclusions of fact and not of law. Greathouse v. Board, 198 Ind. 95, 151 N. E. 411; and allegations of conclusions of fact are sufficient if there is no motion to make them specific by stating the facts on which they are drawn; State v. Farris, 197 Ind. 

128, 150 N. E. 18; and the way to reach a pleading containing such conclusions is by a motion to state the facts instead of by demurrer. Fauvre Coal Co. v. Kushner, 188 Ind. 314, 123 N. E. 409.

The complaint shall contain "Fourth, A demand of the relief to which the plaintiff may suppose himself entitled," and "if the recovery of money be demanded, the amount thereof shall be stated." Burns 1933, 2-1004; Burns 1926, 359; Baldwin, 110.

If there is a default, the judgment cannot exceed the demand, nor can the amount of the demand be increased after default. Bash v. Van Osdol, 75 Ind. 186. If there is an answer and the damages awarded are greater than demanded, the complaint will be deemed to be amended. 

Southern R. Co. v. Bulleit, 40 App. 457, 82 N. E. 474.

Defect in the prayer for relief is not ground for demurrer, but is reached by a motion to make more specific. 

Goodall v. Mopley, 45 Ind. 355; Burns 1933, 2-1057; 

Burns 1926, 412; Baldwin, 162; Burns 1933, 2-1058;

EXCEPTION 

Burns 1926, 413; Baldwin, 163; Burns 1933, 2-1059; 

Burns 1926, 414; Baldwin, 164; Burns 1933, 2-1060; 

Burns 1926, 415; Baldwin, 165; Burns 1933, 2-1061; 

Burns 1926, 416; Baldwin, 166; Burns 1933, 2—1062; 

Burns 1926, 417; Baldwin, 167.

EXCEPTION. 

"An exception is an objection taken to a decision of the court upon a matter of law." Burns 1933, 2-—3101; Burns 

1926, 681; Baldwin, 454. "The party objecting to the decision must except at the time the decision is made. Burns 

1933, 2-3105; Burns 1926, 685; Baldwin, 455. 

In adversary proceedings, the law contemplates the parties litigant are before the court, or at least are afforded an opportunity to be present when the decision is made and the exception must then be taken and not afterwards. But in ex parte proceedings it is sufficient if the exception is taken at the earliest opportunity. The 

Wabash etc. Co. v. Dykeman, 133 Ind. 56, 32 N. E. 823; 

Rooker v. Fidelity Trust Co., 193 Ind. 450, 141 N. E. 41.

A motion, or a demurrer is an objection and an exception may be taken to the decision of the court on the same, Houk v. Barthold, 73 Ind. 21-29; but in order that the exception may avail, the motion or demurrer must state the reasons on which it is based. Public Service Commission v. Lake Erie, 191 Ind. 436, 133 N. E. 492; Burns 1933, 2-1007; Burns 1926, 362; Baldwin, 111, par. 6. An objection must precede the decision of the court and the exception must follow the decision. The objection must state specifically the grounds upon which it is based in order that the court may be aided in its decision. If the objection is general-if no specific grounds are

EXCEPTION 

29 stated-and the court overrules it, no error can be based on the ruling. An exception is necessary to present on appeal the decision of the court on an objection. If there is no objection, there is no question and if there is an objection, on overruling it, only such reasons will be considered on appeal as were stated to the trial court in support of the objection; but if the objection is sustained, the court to which the appeal is taken will consider any reason that supports the ruling of the trial court, whether stated in support of the objection or not. Elliott on 

Appellate Procedure, §§ 769, 770; O. and M. R. Co. v. 

Walker, 113 Ind. 196, 15 N. E. 234; Rapp v. Kester, 125 

Ind. 79, 25 N. E. 141.

An objection to the introduction of evidence must be made to the question when it is asked, or the evidence offered if documentary, and the reasons for the objection. must then be stated. When the court decides the question, the party against whom the decision is made must then except. Clanin v. Fagan, 124 Ind. 304, 24 N. E. 242.

If objectionable evidence is volunteered by a witness, or given in an answer that is not responsive to the question asked, before objection can reasonably be made, a motion should be made to strike out that which is deemed objectionable. Vickery v. McCormick, 117 Ind. 594, 20 

N. E. 495. No question on appeal can be presented on an objection without an exception, nor on an exception without an objection. Falley v. Gribling, 128 Ind. 110, 26 N. E. 794; Wilcox v. Monday, 83 Ind. 335. If evidence is admitted without objection a motion to strike it out comes too late. Brown v. Owen, 94 Ind. 31. If part of an answer to a question is proper and part improper, a motion to strike out the entire answer should be overruled. The motion should be to strike out the improper part. Vickey v. McCormick, 117 Ind. 594, 20 N. E. 495.

On appeal, no objections can be urged except those stated to the trial court. Shafer v. Ferguson, 103 Ind. 90, 2 

N. E. 302. Objection to the process is tested by a motion to quash the process or by plea in abatement made before a general appearance to the action. Pittsburgh v. Hodge,

EXCEPTION 

175 Ind. 669–71, 94 N. E. 324; to the pleadings by demurrer, or motion; A. J. Yawger and Co. v. Joseph, 184 Ind. 

228, 108 N. E. 774; Burns 1933, 2—1005; Burns 1926, 360; 

Baldwin, 155; Burns 1933, 2-1007; Burns 1926, 362; 

Baldwin, 111; Burns 1933, 2-1048; Burns 1926, 403;

Baldwin, 152; to the evidence in chief by a pertinent question by the one offering the evidence, objection by the opposing party, a statement of what the testimony will be in answer thereto by the one offering the evidence, a ruling of the court and an exception to the ruling at the time it is made. Kern v. Bridwell, 119 Ind. 226, 21 N. E. 

664. On cross-examination, the objection should be that the question does not ask for that which is proper on cross-examination and it is not necessary then for the party asking the question to state what he proposes to prove. Harness v. The State, 57 Ind. 1 and the same is true on rebuttal. One in whose favor the ruling is made cannot except to it. Scheible v. Slagle, 89 Ind. 323.

If the court had no jurisdiction of the subject matter, or over the person of the defendant, and the record so shows, an appeal may be taken without an exception. 

Burns 1933, 2-1011; Burns 1926, 366; Baldwin, 115; 

Boys v. Simmons, 72 Ind. 593; Kyle v. Kyle, 55 Ind. 387.

To be good, an exception to the admission of evidence must be based upon an objection which shows a legal reason why it should not be admitted. Lake Erie, etc. v. 

Parker, 94 Ind. 91. Jenny Electric Co. v. Branham, 145 

Ind. 314, 41 N. E. 448. An objection that it does not support any issue in the case is proper. Boil v. Simms, 60

Ind. 162, and if the objection is overruled and the question answered, it is not necessary to move to strike the answer out. Glenn v. Close, 42 Ind. 60. 

The objection may be made by counsel saying, "I object," followed by the reasons and no particular form of exception is required. Burns 1933, 2-3102; Burns 1926, 682; Baldwin, 457; but it should show that the party against whom the ruling is made excepts and is usually in the following form: "To which decision of the court the plaintiff (defendant) at the time excepts." There

EXCEPTION 

31 must be an exception to each decision of the court believed to be wrong, if the question of such decision is to be tested on appeal. Noonan v. Bell, 159 Ind. 329, 64 

N. E. 909. 1 Every exception must be reduced to writing and in order to be available on appeal the exception must be made a part of the record, because all appeals are tried by the record. Campbell v. State, 148 Ind. 527; Burns 

1933, 2-3105; Burns 1926, 685; Baldwin, 455. "Every pleading, motion in writing, report, deposition or other paper filed or offered to be filed in any cause or proceeding, whether received by the court, refused or stricken out, shall be a part of the record from the time of such filing or offer to file. Any order or action of the court in respect to any such pleading, motion in writing, deposition or other paper, and every exception taken thereto by any party shall be entered by the clerk on the minutes or record of the court, and the same when so entered shall be a part of the record without a bill of exceptions. 

And the same is true as to every oral motion, but anything may be made a part of the record by a bill of exceptions which at common law was not a part of the record. Burns 1933, 2-3104; Burns 1926, 684; Baldwin, 462. This statute does not make the evidence nor any motion directed to it while it is being offered during that trial a part of the record, but if the evidence is a part of a report filed in a case, it is a part of the record. Harrah v. State, 38 App. 495, 76 N. E. 443; and so are pleadings offered and not allowed to be filed. Home Ins. Co. v. 

Overturf, 35 App. 361, 74 N. E. 47; and so are the verified complaints and affidavit used as evidence on an application for the appointment of a receiver, and so are affidavits filed in a cause. Humrichous v. Thomas, 177 Ind. 

593, 98 N. E. 419; but such papers must be papers proper to be filed in the trial court. National Surety Co. v. Button, 41 App. 301, 83 N. E. 644. 

"Time may be given to reduce the exception to writing, but not beyond the term unless by special leave of court. 

Burns 1933, 2-3105; Burns 1926, 685; Baldwin, 455.

EXCEPTION 

This provision applies to exceptions arising on the evidence during the trial which become a part of the record only by a bill of exceptions, or order of court. If the bill is filed at any time during the term of court at which the trial is had, and shows an exception was taken at the time the decision was made, the presumption is time was given to reduce the exception contained in it to writing and it is not necessary for the record so to show, and the bill becomes a part of the record. Volger v. Sidener, 86 Ind. 

545; but if the record shows time was given and the bill is not filed within that time, it is not a part of the record, though it is filed during the term at which the exception was taken. Flory v. Wilson, 83 Ind. 391. The parties by agreement made of record may extend the time for filing a bill of exceptions. Lotz v. Briggs, 50

Ind. 346. If the record shows time was given to reduce the exception to writing, the bill must show when it is presented to the Judge for signature and, if presented to him in time, his delay in signing and filing it will not prevent its becoming a part of the record though filed by him after the time given. Burns 1933, 2—3106; Burns 1926, 686; Baldwin, 459. If the bill is signed by the Judge and filed within the time given it need not show when it was presented to the Judge. Hale v. Mathews, 118 Ind. 527, 21 N. E. 43. The time given must be shown by an order book entry and cannot be shown by the bill. Hancher v. 

Stevenson, 147 Ind. 498, 46 N. E. 916. If time is given until a day named to file a bill it must be tendered before that day. Corbin v. Ketcham, 87 Ind. 138; but if an act is to be done by a certain day, it may be done on that day. 

State v. Thorn, 28 Ind. 306. If a motion for a new trial is overruled and an exception then taken, time may be granted in which to file a bill of exceptions though other proceedings are had by the court after overruling the motion and before granting the time, if all are had on the same day. Kopelke v. Kopelke, 112 Ind. 435, 13 

N. E. 695.

MOTION TO MAKE MORE SPECIFIC 

Caption.

In the above entitled cause the defendant moves the court to require the plaintiff to make his complaint more specific in each of the following respects: 

1. In said complaint is found the following: (here set it out) the defendant asks the court to require the plaintiff to set out the facts upon which said statement or conclusion depends and which are necessary to support it. 

2. The defendant also moves the court to require the plaintiff to make this statement found in his complaint (here set it out) more specific by stating the time and place, when and where the facts set forth in said statement occurred. 

A. B. Attorney for Defendant.

This motion should be accompanied by a brief. 

The word "each" when applied to either the parties or pleadings in a case makes the same several and means the same as "separately and severally." Parker v. 

Thomas, 19 Ind. 213.

FORMS OF DEMURRER, ETC. 

1 The same general form of demurrer as to its being joint or several is applicable to both the answer and the reply, but in the demurrer to the answer the cause assigned must be the cause given in the statute, Burns 1933, viz., "does not state facts sufficient to constitute a defense" and to the reply "that the facts stated therein are not sufficient to avoid the paragraph of answer" to which it is pleaded, Burns 1933, 2-1026, Burns 1926, 381; Baldwin, 130. The language of the statute should in all cases be used in the demurrer as applied to the particular pleading demurred to.

All parties plaintiff must act jointly and if a cause of action is not stated in favor of all, the complaint is bad, for want of facts. Berkshire v. Schultz, 25 Ind. 523.

A demurrer to a complaint, an answer, or reply must be accompanied by a memorandum, stating why it is not good. Pittsburgh, etc., v. Home, etc., 183 Ind. 355, 108 

N. E. 525.

A set-off or a counterclaim is called an answer but in fact it is a complaint on which issues may be made as if it were a complaint and on which there may be a demurrer and answer and a reply, all as if it were an independent action. In all respects they are treated as complaints and the answers, replies and demurrers are governed by the law applicable to complaints, answers and replies. 

Wenger v. Clay Township, 61 App. 640, 112 N. E. 402; 

Pittsburgh, etc., v. Home, etc., 183 Ind. 355, 108 N. E. 

525; Stoner v. Swift, 164 Ind. 652, 74 N. E. 248; Burns 

1933, 2-1015; Burns 1926, 370; Baldwin, 130. 1

TRIAL 

39 

TRIAL. 

A trial is a judicial examination of the issues in an action, whether of law or of fact, and includes all the steps taken in a cause from the submission of the same until judgment is rendered. Burns 1933, 2-1901; Burns 

1926, 571; Baldwin, 324; Pitzer v. Ind. Co., 80 Ind. 569; 

Fisk v. Baker, 47 Ind. 534; Jenks v. State, 39 Ind. 1-10; 

Bush v. State, 189 Ind. 467-73, 128 N. E. 443; Lindley v. 

Kemp, 38 App. 355–59, 76 N. E. 798.

An issue of law arises upon any demurrer, motion or objection which requires the decision of the court. An issue of fact arises on any material fact affirmed by one of the parties and denied by the other and the truth of which must be determined by either the court or jury. 

All issues of law are triable by the court and also all issues of fact which prior to the 18th day of June, 1852, were of exclusive equitable jurisdiction. All other issues are triable by a jury unless the jury is waived by both parties. If there are joined in the same suit causes of action or defenses which are triable by a jury with those triable by the court, the former shall be tried by a jury and the latter by the court and both may be tried at the same or different times. Burns 1933, 2-1204; Burns 

1926, 437; Baldwin, 186. If any essential part of a cause is of exclusive equitable jurisdiction all is triable by the court. Field v. Brown, 146 Ind. 293.

If money alone is sought, the action is at law; if a specific decree is sought, the action is at equity. Causes of exclusive equitable jurisdiction are given in Story's

Equity Jurisprudence. Robertson v. McPherson, 4 App. 

595, 31 N. E. 478; Albrecht v. Lumber Co., 126 Ind. 318, 26 N. E. 157; Brighton v. White, 128 Ind. 320, 27 N. E. 620. 

The following causes are held triable by the court: 

Contempt, Garrigus v. State, 93 Ind. 239; injunction, Helm v. Bank, 91 Ind. 44; restraining order, Hopkins v.

TRIAL 

Greensburg, 46 Ind. 187; abatement of nuisance, Shroyer v. Campbell, 31 App. 83, 67 N. E. 193; enforcing liens, Tomlinson v. Bainaka, 163 Ind. 112, 70 N. E. 

155; Miller v. Bank, 99 Ind. 272; between partners for settlement, Kimble v. Seal, 92 Ind. 276; cancelling deeds, Monnett v. Turpie, 133 Ind. 424, 32 N. E. 328; cancelling mortgages, Johnson v. Johnson, 115 Ind. 112, 17 

N. E. 111; setting aside judgments, Quick v. The Lawrence Bank, 10 App. 523, 37 N. E. 186; habeas corpus, Baker v. Gordon, 23 Ind. 204; an accounting, Lupton v. 

Horn, 193 Ind. 499–503, 139 N. E. 177; writ of assistance, Rooker v. Fidelity Trust Co., 196 Ind. 373, 145 

N. E. 493; trust, Camp v. Camp, 52 App. 250, 100 N. E. 

478; judgment creditor's bill, Stoner v. American Trust 

Co., 81 App. 635, 142 N. E. 126; rescission of contract, Ferrell v. Hunt, 189 Ind. 45, 124 N. E. 745.

If some paragraphs of a complaint are triable by the Court and some by a jury, a motion to try all by a jury must be overruled, and if the same paragraph of a complaint states a cause of action triable by the Court and also one triable by a jury, both must be tried by the 

Court. McBride v. Wood, 80 Ind. App. 399, 141 N. E. 229. 

If the action is statutory, there is no right to a jury trial, unless the statute gives it. And the same is true as to the right to appeal. Gordon v. Corning, 174 Ind. 337, 92 N. E. 59. If actions at law and in equity are joined in the same complaint a general demand for trial by a jury must be denied. Watt v. Barnes, 41 App. 466, 84 

N. E. 158; and if a cause is triable by a jury and the defendant does not object to its being tried by the court, the right is waived. Indianapolis v. Brennan, 174 Ind. 

1-44, 87 N. E. 215; and if any essential part of a cause of action is triable by the Court all is triable by it. Towns v. Smith, 115 Ind. 480, 16 N. E. 811.

THE JURY 

41 

THE JURY. 

The Jury consists of not more than twelve, nor less than three persons, each of whom must be a voter and either a freeholder, or a householder, and reside in the county in which the case in which he is called is to be tried, Burns 1926, 577; Baldwin, 329; Burns 1926, 1833;

Baldwin, 1276. In felony cases the jurors may be drawn from an adjoining county. Burns 1933, 9-1507; Burns 

1926, 2260; Baldwin, 2247. The duty of the jury is, under the direction of the presiding Judge to find, from the evidence, the facts in issue between the parties to the case on trial. Bouvier. Before the trial begins each juror is sworn "to well and truly try the matter in issue between the parties and a true verdict give according to the law and the evidence." Burns 1933, 2-2007; Burns 

1926, 583; Baldwin, 338; Blackstone, Vol. 3, p. 365.

In criminal cases it must be twelve, Burns 1933, 9—1501; Burns 1926, 2254; Baldwin, 2246.

The jurors are selected either by the Jury Commissioners or by an officer under the direction of the court from the bystanders.

At the last term of the Circuit Court in any calendar year, the court shall appoint two persons as Jury Commissioners, who shall be voters and freeholders of the county, of opposite politics and one of whom shall reside in the town or city in which the court is held. They act under oath and select the jurors for the following year. Burns 1933, 4-3301; Burns 1926, 1817; Baldwin, 1266. Immediately after their appointment they shall select from the names of the legal voters of the county found on the tax duplicate for that year, the names of twice as many persons as will be required by law for jury service in all the courts of that county for the ensuing year, and write each name on a separate piece of paper of uniform size, shape and color, and deposit it in a box. 

This box is then by them delivered to the clerk of that court, locked and the key is kept by the Commissioner

THE JURY 

whose politics is not the same as that of the clerk. 

Burns 1933, 43304; Burns 1926, 1820; Baldwin, 1267.

On the Monday immediately preceding the commencement of any term of a criminal, circuit or superior court, at 10 o'clock, A. M., the clerk, after having well shaken the box, shall open the same in his office and publicly draw the names of those persons who are to serve as jurors. The first six names so drawn shall constitute the 

Grand Jury and the next twelve names shall constitute the petit jury, if there is but one court in that county, but if there is more than one, there shall be drawn twelve names as petit jurors for each court and in such counties as have a circuit court and a superior court of more than one judge, twelve additional jurors are drawn who shall attend and remain during the term, to be used as talesmen in any of said courts. Burns 1933, 4—3306; 

Burns 1926, 1822; Baldwin, 1270; Burns 1933, Baldwin, 1268. Provision is made for a special jury, for a struck jury by consent, upon notice and for putting new names in the box by the Jury Commissioners. Burns 

1933, 4-3309; Burns 1926, 1825; Baldwin, 1271; Burns 

1933, 4—3310; Burns 1926, 1826; Baldwin, 330; Burns 

1933, 4-3311; Burns 1926, 1827; Baldwin, 331; Burns 

1933, 4-3312; Burns 1926, 1828; Baldwin, 332; Burns 

1933, 4-3313; Burns 1926, 1829; Baldwin, 333. The box shall remain in possession of the clerk securely locked and the Commissioner who has possession of the key shall be present every time the box is to be opened. 

Burns 1933, 4-3305; Burns 1926, 1821; Baldwin, 1272.

And when the clerk draws the names of those who are to be jurors, he must enter the names of those so drawn on the order book and with them his certificate of the fact of such drawing, and the persons having such names are subpoenaed to attend court to serve as such jurors. Burns 1933, 4-3306; Burns 1926, 1822; Baldwin, 1270.

The jury always consists of 12 persons unless by agreement of the parties a less number is used. Burns 1933, 2-2001; Burns 1926, 577; Baldwin, 329. 4746 1

THE JURY 

43 When the persons selected for jury service are called to the jury box each is sworn on his voir dire as follows: "You and each of you, do solemnly swear that you will true answers make to such questions as shall be put to you touching your competency to serve on the present jury, so help you God." McDonald, p. 156. Then each is questioned by the parties to the suit concerning his competency to serve as a juror in that case. 

There are two kinds of challenges-peremptory and for cause. In all cases where the jury consists of six or more, each party shall have three peremptory challenges that is that many challenges without giving any cause. Each party means each side and not each person plaintiff or defendant. Burns 1933, 2-2006; Burns 

1926, 582; Baldwin, 339. Snodgrass v. Hunt, 15 Ind. 

274. A challenge for cause is based upon the fact that the examination of the prospective juror shows he does not possess the necessary qualifications to serve as such. 

These are that he is not a resident voter and either a freeholder or householder. Burns 1933, 4-3317; 

Burns 1926, 1833; Baldwin, 1276; has served in either the Circuit, Superior or Criminal Court in that county during the year immediately preceding. Burns 1933, 4-3318; Burns 1926, 1834; Baldwin, 1269; is interested in the pending or a similar suit; is of kin within the degree of second cousin to one of the parties; has a lawsuit pending against one of the parties; is personally hostile to one of the parties; is master or servant, landlord or tenant of one of the parties, or has eaten or drunk at his expense since being summoned as a juror, or has promised to find a verdict for or against one of the parties; has formed or expressed an opinion in the cause, or is a witness in it, or has been a juror in a former trial of it. Fleming v. State, 11 Ind. 234. Taxpayers of a city or county are not competent jurors in a case in which a judgment may be rendered against the city or county. Hearn v. City of Greensburg, 51 Ind. 

119; The Board v. Loeb, 68 Ind. 29, and if a corporation. is a party, an officer or stockholder in the same is not 1

THE JURY a competent juror. Inland Steel Co. v. Gillespie, 181 

Ind. 633, 104 N. E. 76; nor is one who does not understand the English language, or can not see. LaFayette v. Railroad Co., 13 Ind. 90; Rhodes v. State, 128 Ind. 

189, 27 N. E. 866; or who has formed an opinion from personal knowledge of the facts, or from talking to witnesses, but if he has formed an opinion from rumor or reading the papers and it will readily yield to the evidence he is competent. Elliott v. State, 73 Ind. 10; 

Guetig v. State, 66 Ind. 94. 

The right to challenge for cause cannot be exhausted and if a party permits the jury to be sworn without challenging a juror he waives the juror's incompetency.

Alexander v. Dunn, 5 Ind. 122. The specific reason for the challenge must be stated to the court; an exception must be taken to the ruling of the court, the examination of the juror in full must be made a part of the record by a bill of exceptions and the ruling of the court must be presented by a motion for a new trial, and if the ruling is wrong, no advantage can be taken of it unless the party against whom it is made had exhausted all his peremptory challenges. Shields v. The State, 149 Ind. 

395, 49 N. E. 351. If the complaining party knew of the incompetency of the juror and said nothing about it, or if he discovers it after the jury is sworn and before verdict and does not at once make it known he waives it. Stevens v. Stevens, 127 Ind. 560, 26 N. E. 1078; Henning v. State, 106 Ind. 386, 6 N. E. 803. If the incompetency of the juror is not discovered until after verdict, it will be cause for new trial. Hudspeth v. Herston, 64 Ind. 133. And if discovered before the jury retires, the proper practice is to move to set aside the submission and examine the juror on his voir dire. Kurtz v. 

State, 145 Ind. 119, 42 N. E. 1102. 

The right of a peremptory challenge may be waived by not exercising it, when there is an opportunity to do so. McDonald v. State, 172 Ind. 393, 88 N. E. 673; Burns 

1933, Ind. Const. Art. 2, S. 4; Burns 1926, 93; Baldwin,

Ind. Const. Art. 2, S. 4.

A challenge may be made to the array on the ground that the law has not been complied with in selecting it, but this must be done before the jurors are examined on their voir dire. Leas v. Patterson, 38 Ind. 465.

INSTRUCTIONS, EXCEPTIONS TO THE SAME, HOW MADE A PART OF THE RECORD. 

In every case tried by a jury, either civil or criminal, the court at the close of the argument must give general instructions to the jury. These need not be in writing unless a party to the cause asks that they be written. 

If such request is made, either orally or in writing, the instructions must be written, numbered consecutively and signed by the Judge. Burns 1933, 2-2008; Burns 

1926, 584; Baldwin, 341,-fifth; Burns 1933, 2-2010; 

Burns 1926, 586; Baldwin, 343; Burns 1933, 9—1805; 

Burns 1926, 2301; Baldwin, 2285-fifth.

The following form may be used, if the request is in writing: 

Caption. No. In the above entitled cause the plaintiff (or defendant) before the commencement of the argument to the jury moves the court to instruct the jury in writing, to number the instructions consecutively and to sign the same. 

A. B. Attorney for Plaintiff.

If the motion is oral, note of it should be made by the clerk in the order book. If it is in writing, the writing should be filed. Burns 1933, 2-3104; Burns 1926, 684; 

Baldwin, 462; Evansville Railroad Co. v. Christ, 116 Ind. 

446, 19 N. E. 310. Each party must make his own re-

quest before the argument to the jury begins. A party having made such request may withdraw it. If he does, his adversary cannot take advantage of the fact that the request is withdrawn, nor can he take advantage of the fact if the court refuses the request. Tevis v. Hammersmith, 170 Ind. 286, 84 N. E. 337; Hege v. Newsom, 96 Ind. 426. If such request is made by a party and the court does not instruct the jury in writing, and the proper exception is taken and presented on appeal, the cause will be reversed. Bradway v. Waddell, 95 Ind. 

170; Riley v. Watson, 18 Ind. 291. However, if the

Judge does not number and sign the instructions, this is not reversible error. Shield v. State, 149 Ind. 395, 49 N. E. 351.

The instruction named in the statute is the statement of a principle of law applicable to the case, which the jury are bound to apply in order to determine the rights of the parties. Lehman v. Hawks, 121 Ind. 541, 23 N. E. 670.

If written instructions are requested, any modification or explanation of them must be in writing. Provines v. 

Heaston, 67 Ind. 482; Bosworth v. Barker, 65 Ind. 595.

The court can not read from law books, or have a stenographer take oral instructions given by him to the jury. 

Sellers v. City of Greencastle, 134 Ind. 645, 34 N. E. 

534; Lesueur v. State, 176 Ind. 448, 95 N. E. 239; but the court may read the pleadings and give to the jury any direction which is not an instruction and may, at the request of the jury, or on its own motion, re-instruct them and withdraw any instruction given, but the parties must be notified and have an opportunity to be present in person or by counsel. Clouser v. Ruckman, 104 

Ind. 588, 4 N. E. 202; Dodd v. Moore, 91 Ind. 522; Sage v. Evansville, etc., 134 Ind. 100, 33 N. E. 771; Jones v. 

Johnson, 61 Ind. 257; Cox v. Peltier, 159 Ind. 355, 65 

N. E. 6.

Any party to the suit has a right to ask the Court before the commencement of the argument to give special instructions to the jury. Such instructions shall

be plainly written and numbered consecutively and signed by the party or his counsel, asking the same and delivered to the court. Burns 1933, 2-2008; Burns 1926, 584; Baldwin, 341,-fourth; Burns 1933, 2—2010; Burns 

1926, 586; Baldwin, 343; Burgett v. Burgett, 43 Ind. 78.

Unless everything the statute requires the party, desiring the instructions, to do, is done, no error can be based on the refusal of the court to give the instructions. Supreme Tent, etc., v. Ethridge, 43 App. 475, 87 N. E. 1049; 

Board v. Blair, 8 App. 574, 36 N. E. 216; Hutchinson v. 

Lemcke, 107 Ind. 121, 8 N. E. 71. The court shall indicate before instructing the jury by a memorandum in writing at the close of instructions so requested, the number of those given and of those refused, and such memorandum shall be signed by the judge. If the court shall modify any instruction requested, the instruction as modified shall be written out at full length and shall be given as one of the instructions given by the court of its own motion and the instruction as requested shall be refused. Burns 1933, 2-2008; Burns 1926, 584; Baldwin, 341,-sixth; Burns 1933, 2-2010; Burns 1926, 586; 

Baldwin, 343. This section does not apply to criminal actions, Donovan v. State, 170 Ind. 123, 83 N. E. 744. 

The following form may be used for such a request under said sections:

Caption. No. 

In the above entitled cause the plaintiff (or defendant) moves the court to instruct the jury in writing, to number and sign the instructions and to give to the jury each of the following instructions: (here set them out). 

A. B. Attorney for Plaintiff (or Defendant).

No one of the above sections requires the court to indicate before argument the instructions that will be given. 

By section 2-2011, Burns 1933, 587 Burns 1926 and 342 Baldwin, it is provided: Any party having a civil cause on trial before a jury in any circuit or superior court of this state, having an interest in the result of such trial, before the commencement of the argument to the jury may tender to the court instructions in writing,

properly numbered, to be given to the jury, and require the court to indicate before the argument such as will be given by writing opposite each the words: "Given," "Given as modified by the court," or, "Refused," and if the court desires, it may hear argument thereon by the respective counsel before acting on the instructions tendered.
