During the argument to the jury, any instruction so indicated to be given may be read to the jury as the law of the case, and the court shall give the same to the jury as the law when such jury is instructed by the court, and the court may of its own motion, and shall upon application of either party, also before the commencement of the argument, lay before the parties any instructions properly numbered which it will give to the jury and thereupon the same may be read by any one as the law while making an argument to the jury. The court also may give to the jury at the close of the argument such other instructions with those already approved as may be necessary to fully present the law to the jury and secure the ends of justice, and may modify an instruction he indicated would be given, City of Logansport v. 

Dykeman, 116 Ind. 15. This is the section which gives counsel the right to see the instructions before the commencement of the argument. Walker v. Johnson, 6 App. 

600, 33 N. E. 267.

The statute in this respect is mandatory; Southern 

Electric Co. v. Beard, 81 App. 272, 143 N. E. 173. If counsel desires to see the instructions before the argument begins and to read the instructions to the jury in his argument, he must ask for that right under this section, and unless the provisions of the section are complied with by him in making his request, it will not be error for the court to refuse the request. If the request is properly made and the court does not comply with it, and proper exception is taken and presented on appeal, the cause will be reversed. Southern Electric Co. v. 

Beard, 81 App. 272, 143 N. E. 173. Counsel cannot argue the correctness of such instructions, but may read them to the jury and apply them to the facts in evidence. Scott

v. Scott, 124 Ind. 66, 24 N. E. 666. The instructions asked must be signed by the party or his attorney; City of Logansport v. Greene, 192 Ind. 253, 135 N. E. 657; 

Wiley v. State, 200 Ind. 572, 165 N. E. 313. Signing the request is not sufficient.

The following general form is proper to be used in making a general request under said sections for the court to instruct the jury in writing and before argument to indicate the instructions that will be given to the jury:

Caption. No. In the above entitled cause, before the commencement of the argument to the jury the plaintiff (or defendant) moves the court to instruct the jury in writing, to number consecutively and sign the instructions given by the court to the jury on its own motion, to give to the jury each of the following instructions, (here set them out) to indicate before argument which of said instructions will be given by writing opposite each instruction the words "Given," "Given as modified" or "Refused." 

A. B. Attorney for the Plaintiff (or Defendant).

Any party to the cause objecting to the action of the court in giving or refusing to give instructions, may except to such action by writing on the margin or at the close of each instruction, "Refused and excepted to by plaintiff or defendant" or "Given and excepted to by plaintiff or defendant," which memorandum shall be signed by the Judge and dated. Burns 1933, 2-2009; 

Burns 1926, 585; Baldwin, 344. Exceptions under this section must be taken before the verdict is returned; 

Neff v. Masters, 173 Ind. 196, 89 N. E. 846; and must be taken to each instruction; Muncie Traction Co. v. 

Black, 173 Ind. 142, 89 N. E. 845; and must name the party excepting. If the exception is to a modification of an instruction, it should specify the portion excepted to. 

Helms v. The Wayne, etc., 73 Ind. 325. The provisions of the statute must be strictly complied with; Young v. 

Montgomery, 161 Ind. 68; City of Michigan City v. Phillips, 163 Ind. 449, 71 N. E. 205; Retsek v. Harbart, 176

Ind. 441, 96 N. E. 386; Conden v. Morning Star, 94 Ind. 

150. A party can not except to instructions given at his request. Blanchard v. Jones, 101 Ind. 542.

Exceptions to giving or refusing to give instructions may be taken at any time during the term, and the same may be taken orally and entered upon the record or minutes of the court, or in writing at the close of the instructions requested or given by the court of its own motion, in which case the party excepting or his counsel shall enter at the close of such instructions a memorandum which shall be dated and signed, setting forth in substance that such party excepts to the giving or refusing, as the case may be, of each of the above instructions designated by its number. Burns 1933, 2—2010; 

Burns 1926, 586; Baldwin, 343. This section does not repeal said section 585; Hoffbauer v. Morgan, 172 Ind. 

273, and to save the exceptions under this statute its provisions must be substantially complied with. Cleveland, etc. v. Powers, 173 Ind. 105, 88 N. E. 1073; Morgan Construction Co. v. Dulin, 184 Ind. 652-55, 109 N. E. 960.

The memorandum made at the close of the instructions and signed by the Judge must show which instructions are to be given and which refused; Morgan Construction 

Co. v. Dulin, 184 Ind. 652, 109 N. E. 960, and if the Judge notes on requested instructions those that will be given and those that will not, it is presumed in the absence of a contrary showing that the notes were made before the jury was instructed. Morgan Construction Co. v. Dulin, 184 Ind. 652, 109 N. E. 960. A memorandum cannot be made after the close of the term, nor can it be made a part of the record by a nunc pro tunc entry. Holcomb v. 

Norman, 43 App. 506, 87 N. E. 1057. Exceptions to the instruction under this section 586 may be taken at any time during the term at which the trial was had. Retsek v. Harbart, 176 Ind. 441, 96 N. E. 386. Under this section, oral exceptions to instructions may be taken when they are properly entered of record; Duckwall v. Davis, 194 

Ind. 670, 142 N. E. 113, and they must be entered on the minutes of the court in order to be effective. Providence

Washington Ins. Co. v. Wolf, 168 Ind. 690, 80 N. E. 26.

All exceptions to instructions may be shown by a bill of exceptions, and oral instructions may be excepted to and made a part of the record by a bill of exceptions. Burns 

1933, 2-3104; Burns 1926, 684; Baldwin, 462; Southerland v. Venard, 34 Ind. 390.

All instructions given and all instructions asked for by the parties must be by the Judge filed as a part of the record, but the instructions shall not be entered at large on the final record unless either party may wish to remove the cause to a superior court. Burns 1933, 2-2008; 

Burns 1926, 584; Baldwin, 341,-sixth. This statute is mandatory. In order for the instructions to become a part of the record without a bill of exceptions, it must appear from the record that the instructions were in fact filed. "All instructions requested as herein provided, whether given or refused, and all instructions given by the court of its own motion, together with all exceptions taken to the giving or refusing of instructions as herein prescribed and all entries upon the minutes or record of court in respect to such instructions and exceptions, shall be a part of the record without any bill of exceptions."' Burns 1933, 2-2010; Burns 1926, 586; Baldwin, 343. Indianapolis, etc. Ry. v. Ragan, 171 Ind. 569, 86 N. E. 966.

File marks of the Clerk are not evidence of filing the bill. 

McCormack v. Smith, 21 App. 617, 52 N. E. 1000; 

Thompson v. State, 190 Ind. 363, 130 N. E. 412; Peterson v. Struby, 25 App. 19, 56 N. E. 733. There must be a record entry showing that the instructions were filed; 

Killion v. Hulen, 8 App. 494, 36 N. E. 49; nor is it sufficient that the record shows the court ordered the instruction filed; Indianapolis Ry. Co. v. Ragan, 171 Ind. 569, 86 N. E. 966; and they must be filed after exceptions noted on the margin are written; Williams v. Chapman, 160 Ind. 130, 66 N. E. 460. Instructions attached to a bill of exceptions are not a part of the record; Mitchell v. 

Johnson, 9 App. 402, 36 N. E. 861; nor are they a part of

the record when merely copied therein; Olds v. Deckman, 98 Ind. 162; Burns 1933, 2-2010; Burns 1926, 586; Baldwin, 343.

The following is a proper form to be used by the plaintiff in saving instructions and exceptions thereto by a bill of exceptions:

Caption. No. 

Be it remembered in the above entitled cause that the plaintiff, before argument to the jury began, asked the court in writing to instruct the jury in writing, to number consecutively the instructions that would be given by the court and sign the same and to give to the jury each of certain instructions numbered from one to ten inclusive, asked by the plaintiff, to indicate before argument which of said instructions would be given, by writing on the margin of each "Given," or "Given as modified," or "Refused," and the same was signed by his counsel and presented to the court, and which written request is in the words and figures following, to-wit: (Here insert)

And be it further remembered in said cause the court did instruct the jury in writing and did give to the jury of its own motion instructions numbered from one to six inclusive, and signed the same, and to the giving of each of which instructions the plaintiff did at the time except; 

And be it further remembered that of said instructions so presented and requested by the plaintiff the Court gave those numbered one, two, and three, and refused to give each of those numbered four, five, six, seven, eight, nine, and ten, to which refusal of the court to give each of said instructions so numbered the plaintiff did at the time except; 

And be it further remembered that the defendant did in writing request the court to give each of the following instructions, numbered one, and four;

And be it further remembered that the court did give of said instructions those numbered one and four, and refused to give those numbered two and three, to the giving of each of which said instructions one and four the plaintiff did at the time except;

INTERROGATORIES TO THE JURY 

53 And be it remembered the instructions so given were all the instructions given in said cause, and be it further remembered that this bill of exceptions is presented to the Judge of said court on this tenth day of May, 1930, and is by said Judge signed, sealed and made a part of the record this the fifteenth day of May, 1930. 

A. B. Judge.

This bill of exceptions must be filed by the Judge of the court after he signs it. Burns 1933, 2—3106; Burns 1926, 686; Baldwin, 459. And there must be a record entry of the filing, Guirl v. Gillett, 124 Ind. 501, 24 N. E. 1036.

The same form may be used for the defendant's bill of exceptions if the word defendant is substituted for the word plaintiff. 

INTERROGATORIES TO THE JURY. 

In all law cases, tried by a jury, if the jury agree, they must return a general verdict, and in all such cases either party may ask the court to submit to the jury in writing, particular questions of fact in the form of interrogatories, on any or all the issues of the cause, which interrogatories must be answered and with the answers thereto returned with the verdict and recorded with it. Burns 

1933, 2-2022; Burns 1926, 598; Baldwin, 355.

The court may on its own motion, without a request from any party, submit interrogatories to the jury. Senhenn v. City of Evansville, 140 Ind. 675, 40 N. E. 69.

Each party must make his own request in writing, sign the same, write out the interrogatories, number them,

REQUEST FOR INTERROGATORIES, ETC. 

ask that the jury be required to answer each in case they return a general verdict and return them with their answers into court with their general verdict. Taylor v. 

Burk, 91 Ind. 252.

Caption. No. 

REQUEST. 

In the above entitled cause the plaintiff (or defendant) submits the following interrogatories and asks the court to submit each of the same to the jury and that the jury be instructed to answer each of them and return them with the answers thereto into court with their general verdict, if they return a general verdict.

Int. 1. Int. 2. 

Attorney for Plaintiff.

The interrogatories should be submitted by the court and it should not appear who asked that they be submitted, but if it does, it is not reversible error. Terre 

Haute, etc. v. Payne, 45 App. 132, 89 N. E. 413.

The court has discretion as to the time when the interrogatories shall be presented and unless presented before the argument begins it is not error to refuse to submit them, and if presented after the argument begins and without showing them to the opposite party and the court then submits them to the jury, it is not reversible error. 

Sherfey v. Evansville, etc. Co., 121 Ind. 427-31, 23 N. E. 

273; Cleveland, etc. v. The Monroe, etc. Co., 11 App. 423, 39 N. E. 172. Each interrogatory must be positively answered but if there is no evidence upon the subject,

REQUEST FOR INTERROGATORIES, ETC. 

55 the answer may be "no evidence." Hopkins v. Stanley, 43 Ind. 553; Louisville, etc. v. Thompson, 107 Ind. 442, 8 N. E. 18. If the questions are not properly answered or there is any defect in the answers, the party who objects to them must object to the verdict being received and the jury discharged and move that the answers, in the respect in which they are defective, be corrected and if the motion is not sustained, save the question by a bill of exceptions. Town of Kentland v. Hagan, 17 App. 1, 

46 N. E. 43. Each answer should be signed by the foreman; Louisville, etc. v. Kemper, 153 Ind. 618, 53 N. E. 

931; but the foreman may sign the answer to the last interrogatory and this will be sufficient. Sage v. Brown, 34 Ind. 464, and if no objection is made because the interrogatories are not signed, or to the manner of signing them, until after the verdict is received, and the jury discharged, the error is waived. Louisville, etc. v. Kemper, 153 Ind. 618, 53 N. E. 931. The facts to be found are those pertinent to and involved in the issue and essential to its support on one side or the other and must be material and substantial. Manning v. Gasharie, 27 Ind. 

399; The Louisville, etc. v. Hubbard, 116 Ind. 193, 18 

N. E. 611.

Each interrogatory should present but one question. 

Rosser v. Barnes, 16 Ind. 502; Pope v. Branch, etc. Co., 23 App. 210, 54 N. E. 835; not ask for opinions. Toledo and Co. v. Goddard, 25 Ind. 185; nor mere items of evidence, Louisville, etc. v. Hubbard, 116 Ind. 193, 18 N. E. 

611; nor be hypothetical. Lauter v. Duckworth, 19 App. 

535, 48 N. E. 864; nor involve a question of law. O. and 

M. Ry. Co. v. Stansberry, 132 Ind. 533, 32 N. E. 218; nor be without the issues, nor immaterial, nor covering the entire issue, Uhl v. Harvey, 78 Ind. 26; must not assume facts, Toledo, etc. v. Goddard, 25 Ind. 185; nor specify the particular items of damages allowed, O. & M. v. Judy, 120 Ind. 397, 22 N. E. 352; nor ask upon which paragraph of the complaint the verdict is based. Muncie, etc. Co. v. 

Davis, 162 Ind. 558, 70 N. E. 875; nor call for a conclusion of law, nor for a mingled question of law and fact.

REQUEST FOR INTERROGATORIES, ETC. 

O. & M. Co. v. Stansberry, 132 Ind. 533–35, 32 N. E. 218; 

May be leading, Rice v. Rice, 6 Ind. 100. If improper interrogatories are asked or given, objection must be made to the same at the earliest moment possible and saved by a bill of exceptions. Town of Kentland v. 

Hagan, 17 App. 1, 46 N. E. 43. In the absence of the evidence, the court on appeal will not consider an error in refusing to submit an interrogatory; Harness v. State, 143 Ind. 425, 41 N. E. 1029; but it is error for the court to refuse to submit proper interrogatories, properly presented. Miller v. White River School Tp., 101 Ind. 503.

The motion for judgment on the answers to the interrogatories need not be in writing. Terre Haute, etc. v. 

Clark, 73 Ind. 168. Nor does it waive the right to file a motion for a new trial. Ronan v. Meyer, 84 Ind. 390.

May be made after a motion for a new trial. There is no legal priority and both may be pending at the same time. 

Indianapolis, etc. v. Bailey, 54 App. 370, 102 N. E. 970.

The answers will not overthrow the general verdict if they can be reconciled with it, under any supposable facts provable under the issues and every presumption is in favor of the general verdict; Shuck v. State, 136 Ind. 63, 35 N. E. 993; and in considering them the evidence will not be considered. Cox v. Ratcliffe, 105 Ind. 374, 5

N. E. 5; they must be considered as a whole, Strecker v. 

Comm., 90 Ind. 469; are presumed to be true, Wilson v. 

Evers, 15 App. 46, 43 N. E. 572; if contradictory, they nullify each other, Grand Rapids, etc. v. McAnnally, 98 

Ind. 412-18, and if improper will be disregarded. Board v. Bonebrake, 146 Ind. 311–19, 45 N. E. 470.

All the interrogatories and answers must be considered as a whole and reconciled without strained construction. 

Presumptions and intendments arising in support of the general verdict do not arise to establish contradictions in the answers. Unreasonable presumptions are not to be considered. Southern Ind. Ry. v. Peyton, 157 Ind. 

690–97, 61 N. E. 722; N. Y., etc. v. Hamlin, 170 Ind. 20–30, 83 N. E. 343.

SPECIAL FINDING OF FACTS 

57 For a proper interrogatory in damage cases as to cause, see Board v. Bonebrake, 146 Ind. 311, 45 N. E. 470; and in will cases as to capacity, Bower v. Bower, 142 Ind. 

194, 41 N. E. 523.

SPECIAL FINDING OF FACTS. 

If one of the parties to a cause requests the court to find the facts specially and to state conclusions of law thereon, the court shall in writing state the facts and then the conclusions of law upon them and judgment shall be entered accordingly. Burns 1933, 2-2102; Burns 1926, 603; Baldwin, 360.

The request may be either oral or written, but the record must show by order book entry, bill of exceptions, or finding, that one of the parties made the request, Smith v. Wheeler, 99 Ind. 140; Bodkin v. Merit, 102 Ind. 

293, 1 N. E. 625. If the record does not so show, there is no valid finding and if one appears in the record it will not be considered as such, but will be regarded as a general finding, Weaver v. Shipley, 127 Ind. 526, 27 N. E. 

146. Each party must make his own request. Bindham v. Stage, 123 Ind. 281, 23 N. E. 756; and cannot predicate error on the request of his adversary. The request may be withdrawn by the party making it, if the withdrawal is made before the court makes known the finding. Beach v. Franklin Tp., 56 App. 220, 103 N. E. 498; and the request must be made at or before the trial begins. Tevis v. Hammersmith, 170 Ind. 286, 84 N. E. 337. The trial begins when the jury is sworn. Hummell v. State, 86

Ind. 431, or cause submitted, if tried by the Court. Burns 

1933, 2-1901; Burns 1926, 571; Baldwin, 324; Pitzer v. 

Indianapolis & Co., 80 Ind. 569. If the request is not made at the proper time, it is discretionary with the www

SPECIAL FINDING OF FACTS 

court to make the finding. Beach v. Franklin Tp., 56 

App. 220, 103 N. E. 498.

The finding must be in writing and signed by the Judge and filed with the clerk, Peoria v. Walser, 22 Ind. 73, but without being signed by the Judge it may be made a part of the record by a bill of exceptions, or order of court. Winstanley v. Breyfogle, 148 Ind. 618. Unless made a part of the record by a bill of exceptions, or order of court, the special finding must be signed by the Judge and filed with the clerk or it will be considered as a general finding. Service v. Gambrell, 110 Ind. 349, 11 N. E. 

434. The signature of the Judge should follow the finding but if the conclusions of law immediately follow the finding and the two constitute one written instrument, it may follow the conclusions of law. Martin v. Marks, 154 Ind. 

549, 57 N. E. 249. 

Evidence should not be found. Cottrell v. Nixon, 109 

Ind. 378, 10 N. E. 122; but the ultimate facts in issue should be, Eckart v. Ft. Wayne, 181 Ind. 352, 104 N. E. 

762; and if evidentiary facts are found they will be disregarded. Bartholomew v. Pierson, 112 Ind. 430, 14 N. E. 

249; and so will facts found which are not within the issues. Brunson v. Henry, 152 Ind. 310, 52 N. E. 407; and so will legal conclusions; Terre Haute v. Becker, 146 Ind. 

202, 45 N. E. 96.

The right to have a special finding of facts made applies only to civil actions which include both actions at law and suits in equity. Burns 1933; 2-101; Burns 

1926, 256; Baldwin, 14. It does not apply to habeas corpus proceedings-McClennan v. Margowski, 90 Ind. 

150; nor to proceedings supplementary to execution, Hutchinson v. Trauerman, 112 Ind. 21, 13 N. E. 412; nor to interlocutory orders; Starr v. Swain, 182 Ind. 313, 106 

N. E. 357; nor to any cause not a civil action, nor to any proceeding in a civil action which is not a final determination of the same.

The law does not require the trial court to paragraph and number the findings. Major v. Miller, 165 Ind. 275, 75 N. E. 159. If the special finding of facts, taken as a whole, when stripped of all extraneous matters, such as

SPECIAL FINDING OF FACTS 

59 facts not sustained by sufficient evidence, without the issues, contrary to law and legal conclusions, still contains facts properly within the issues upon sufficient evidence upon which to predicate the conclusions of law, such conclusions will be upheld and the surplus findings disregarded, Major v. Miller, 165 Ind. 275, 75 N. E. 159.

A fact not found is presumed not to exist. Wolverton v. Wolverton, 163 Ind. 26, 71 N. E. 123. The facts found must be sufficient to support the judgment, Sellers v. 

Hayes, 163 Ind. 422, 72 N. E. 119. If there are written instruments made a part of the pleadings, the findings may refer to them without setting them out. Miller v. 

Wayne and Co., 32 App. 480, 70 N. E. 180. If the finding is indefinite and uncertain or defective in form or contents, the remedy is by venire de novo; Cottrell v. Nixon, 109 Ind. 378, 10 N. E. 122; Hull v. Louth, 109 Ind. 315, 10 

N. E. 270. If a fact is not found, the remedy is by a motion for a new trial. Blair v. Curry, 150 Ind. 99, 46 N. E. 

672; Quick v. Brenner, 101 Ind. 230-35. Motions to add to, modify, or strike out parts of a special finding are not authorized. Conner v. Andrews Land Co., 162 Ind. 338, 70 N. E. 376; but this seems to be in conflict with Chicago v. Taylor, cited below. If the facts found are filed and are entered in the order book, they become a part of the record. Secor v. Souder, 95 Ind. 95. So long as the cause is in fieri the court may amend the findings. Chicago, etc. v. Taylor, 183 Ind. 240, 108 N. E. 1.

MOTION ASKING FOR A SPECIAL FINDING, ETC. 

Caption. No. 

In the above entitled cause the plaintiff (or defendant) before the trial of the same begins asks the Court to find the facts specially and state conclusions of law thereon. 

E. F. 

Attorney for Plaintiff.

FORM OF SPECIAL FINDING FORM OF SPECIAL FINDING. 

Caption. No. 

In the above entitled cause before the trial of the same began I was asked by the plaintiff (defendant) to find the facts specially and state conclusions of law thereon and do find the facts to be as follows: (here set out the facts either with or without paragraphing them) 

And on the facts so found I conclude the law is as follows: (here state each conclusion of law and number the same) to each of which conclusions of law both plaintiff and defendants except. 

E. F. Judge.

To aid the Court Counsel for the parties, plaintiff and defendant each should prepare a finding of facts with conclusions of law and present the same to the Court. 

CONCLUSIONS OF LAW. 

Burns 1933, 2-2102; Burns 1926, 603; Baldwin, 360; provides for conclusions of law on the facts found, but it does not provide when or how they are to be made or become a part of the record. It is usual for them to be made in writing, on the paper on which the finding is made immediately following it and at the time it is made and when so made and the paper is filed in the case they become a part of the record. Martin v. Marks, 154 Ind. 

549, 57 N. E. 249. But the conclusions of law do not have to be made at the time the finding of facts is made, nor

CONCLUSIONS OF LAW 

61 on the same paper, nor be then signed by the Judge, nor before a motion for a new trial is made or passed on. 

Indianapolis, etc. v. Harbaugh, 38 App. 115–20, 78 N. E. 

80. If the record shows the conclusions of law and that they are signed by the Judge this is sufficient. Winstanley v. Breyfogle, 148 Ind. 618, 48 N. E. 224.

To raise any question on the conclusions of law, an exception must be taken to them at the time they are stated or as soon as possible thereafter and before the party excepting takes any other step in the case. Winstanley v. Breyfogle, 148 Ind. 618, 48 N. E. 224; Helmes v. Wagner, 102 Ind. 385, 1 N. E. 730. The conclusions of law should be numbered, though the statute does not require this, and the exception should be to each conclusion, specifically, if more than one, identifying it, by each party excepting unless they have a joint cause for excepting. 

Baldwin v. Heil, 155 Ind. 682, 58 N. E. 200. On appeal the correctness of the conclusions of law is not presented by a motion for a new trial, but by assigning as error that the court erred in its conclusion of law specifically naming it. Smith v. James, 131 Ind. 131, 30 N. E. 902; 

Midland, etc. v. Dickason, 130 Ind. 164, 29 N. E. 775. An exception to conclusions of law admits the facts are correctly found. Warren v. Sohn, 122 Ind. 215, 23 N. E. 

779; but it does not preclude the filing of a motion for a new trial; Bertelson v. Bower, 81 Ind. 512, and if the conclusion is based on a fact not in evidence, or on a fact or facts not within the issues, it must be disregarded. 

Cincinnati, etc. et al. v. Chenoweth, 22 App. 685, 54 N. E. 

403; Thomas v. Dale, 86 Ind. 435. Exceptions to conclusions of law must be entered of record at the close of the conclusions, Cooney v. America, etc., 161 Ind. 193, 67 

N. E. 989. If an exception is made jointly to two or more conclusions of law and either is good, the exception will fail. Evansville v. Town of Ft. Branch, 149 Ind. 276, 49 N. E. 2; nor will it be considered unless the record shows the special finding was filed or made a part of the record by bill of exceptions or order of court. Chapin v. 

Du Shane, 32 App. 1, 69 N. E. 174.

DEMURRER TO THE EVIDENCE DEMURRER TO THE EVIDENCE. 

The statute makes no provision for demurring to the evidence, but the common law does and the Supreme Court adopts the common law and authorizes such demurrer. Blackstone, Vol. 3, 372. If any material point is not proved the demurrer must be sustained. 

Lyons v. Terre Haute, etc., 101 Ind. 419.

If a case is on trial by a jury, the defendant at the close of the plaintiff's evidence in chief may demur to it. The demurrer must be in writing, signed by the defendant or his counsel and filed as a paper in the case. It is not necessary for the opposite party to join in the demurrer but it must contain all the evidence given by the plaintiff. 

If the party offering the evidence thinks the demurrer does not correctly and fully set it forth, he may object to it for such reasons, stating them, and it is the duty of the court to see that it does correctly and fully set forth all such evidence before it is passed on and that it contains no other evidence. The effect of such demurrer is to withdraw the case from the jury. If the demurrer is sustained, the case is at an end and there must be a judgment for the defendant. If the demurrer is overruled, the damages are assessed by a jury, either conditionally by the jury first called to try the case or by another jury thereafter called to assess such damage. If the demurrer is sustained the party offering the evidence should except. If it is overruled, the party demurring should except. If the party offering the evidence joins in the demurrer, he admits the evidence is properly set out. In passing on the demurrer, no advantage can be taken of any defect in the pleading and the demurrer admits the facts of which there is any evidence and all conclusions, which can be fairly and logically deduced from those facts. The demurrer with the evidence, the ruling of the court thereon and the exception thereto are parts of the record, and to bring all of those things before the court

DEMURRER TO THE EVIDENCE 

63 on appeal it is only necessary to assign as error that the court erred in sustaining, or overruling the demurrer. If the demurrer is overruled the defendant may except to any error growing out of the assessment of damages which must be saved and presented as though there had been no demurrer to the evidence, and he may also present any other question. Lindley v. Kelly, 42 Ind. 294; 

Lake Shore, etc. v. Foster, 104 Ind. 293, 4 N. E. 20.

The party upon whom the burden of proof rests can not demur to the evidence and in passing on the demurrer no evidence offered by the demurring party can be considered. Fritz v. Clark, 80 Ind. 591. 

If the demurring party does not ask that the damages be assessed by a jury, he waives that right and the court may assess them. If he proceeds, without objection by the other party, and offers evidence, he waives the right to his exception to the ruling on the demurrer. If the other party objects to his offering evidence, it will be error for the court to permit such evidence to be given; but if he does not so object, he waives the error, and the cause in all respects proceeds as if there had been no demurrer to the evidence. Strought v. Gear, 48 Ind. 

100-5; McLean, Adm. v. Equitable, etc., 100 Ind. 127–30.

It seems the defendant may at any time after the plaintiff has rested in chief demur to the evidence; also, if the defendant admits the plaintiff's cause of action, he the plaintiff may demur to the evidence offered by the defendant to sustain his defense, and also if the defendant pleads a set-off or a counterclaim, the plaintiff may demur to the evidence offered in support of it. 

The following is an approved form for a demurrer by the defendant to the evidence of the plaintiff : 

Caption. No. 

Be it remembered in the above entitled cause that the plaintiff introduced the following evidence: (Here set out all evidence given by the plaintiff) and this being all the evidence in the cause the defendant says it is not sufficient for the plaintiff to have and maintain his action, and he demurs thereto and prays that the plaintiff may be required

MOTION FOR DIRECTED VERDICT 

to join in this demurrer, and the defendant admits the facts given in evidence by the plaintiff and every inference and conclusion the jury may rightfully and reasonably decree therefrom. 

A. B. Attorney for Defendant. 

Griggs v. Seeley, 8 Ind. 264-8.

MOTION FOR DIRECTED VERDICT. 

If there is no evidence to sustain the action, the defendant may, after the plaintiff has rested his case in chief, move the court to instruct the jury to return a verdict for the defendant and the court may, on its own motion, so instruct the jury and should do so if a demurrer to the evidence should be sustained. Steinmetz v. Wingate, 42 

Ind. 574; Lyons v. Terre Haute, etc., 101 Ind. 419.

Such a motion may be made and such direction be given after all the evidence, both for the plaintiff and for the defendant has been given. If the motion is overruled and the defendant introduces evidence, he waives the ruling on his former motion, and can only raise the question by renewing his motion at the close of all the evidence. Baltimore, etc. v. Connoyer, 149 Ind. 524–7, 48 

N. E. 352; Chicago, etc. v. Schipper, 75 App. 669, 131 

N. E. 232.

By making a motion for a directed verdict the defendant asserts the evidence is not sufficient to sustain a verdict in favor of the plaintiff. In deciding this question the court is required to consider only the evidence most favorable to the plaintiff, including all proper conclusions and excluding all evidence in favor of the defendant.

OBJECTIONS AND EXCEPTIONS 

65 The same rules apply to a motion to direct a verdict as to a demurrer to the evidence. Monfort v. Indianapolis, etc., 189 Ind. 683, 128 N. E. 842.

The motion need not be in writing and, if made by the defendant at the close of plaintiff's evidence and is overruled, he may then ask to introduce evidence and if he is not permitted to do so, it is error. If both parties ask for a directed verdict, the cause is taken from the jury and submitted to the court for decision, Goings v. Davis, 82 App. 231, 141 N. E. 473; Kleine v. Houk, 78 App. 146, 134 N. E. 872; Pritchard v. Mines, 56 App. 671, 106 N. E. 411. 

OBJECTIONS AND EXCEPTIONS. 

If the court has jurisdiction of the parties and the subject matter, no appeal will be considered unless the record affirmatively shows an exception taken by the appealing party to a decision of the trial court. 

No Exception, No Reversal. Butler v. Thornburgh, 141 

Ind. 152, 40 N. E. 514. An exception is an objection taken to a decision of the court upon a matter of law. 

Burns 1933, 2-3101; Burns 1926, 681; Baldwin, 454. The party who asks the particular decision of the court must state his reasons for so doing and this is done by demurrer, by motion or by objection. Every demurrer and every motion is in fact an objection and though generally not applied to the evidence in some instances they may be. Questions arising on the introduction of evidence are raised by a specific objection. In all cases the objection to evidence must state specifically the grounds upon which it is based and on appeal no other ground will be

OBJECTIONS AND EXCEPTIONS 

considered. Ohio & C. v. Walker, 113 Ind. 196, 15 N. E. 

234; Decker v. Mahoney, 64 App. 500, 116 N. E. 57.

The objection is made before the decision, and the exception is taken at the time of the decision, except when an exception is taken to giving or refusing instructions (not by a bill of exceptions) or at the earliest opportunity. Both are necessary. If either is absent the other is not effective. Burns 1933, 2-3102; Burns 1926, 682; 

Baldwin, 457; Falley v. Gribling, 128 Ind. 110, 26 N. E. 

794; Wabash and Co. v. Dykeman, 133 Ind. 56, 32 N. E. 

823; Rooker v. Fidelity Trust Co., 193 Ind. 450, 141

N. E. 4. The party aggrieved must except in the manner prescribed by law or he cannot afterward complain. 

Brownlee v. Hare, 64 Ind. 311-18. In excepting, the word "Except" must be used. Robinson v. State, 152 

Ind. 304, 53 N. E. 223. Time may be given to reduce the exception to writing but not beyond the term, unless by special leave of the court. Burns 1933, 2-3105; Burns 

1926, 685; Baldwin, 455. Where the decision objected to is entered on the record and the grounds of objection appear in the entry, the exception may be taken by the party objecting, causing it to be noted at the end of the decision that he excepts. Burns 1933, 2-3103; Burns 

1926, 683; Baldwin, 458; Burns 1933, 2-3104; Burns 

1926, 684; Baldwin, 462. By this section, all pleadings and motions are made parts of the record but that section does not apply to motions concerning the evidence offered at the trial. On the admission of evidence, the party who believes the evidence is incompetent must when it is offered, object, and then state his reasons for objecting. The party offering the evidence must then state what he proposes to prove in answer to the question. Then the court rules and the party against whom he rules must then state that he excepts. 

If a motion asks that something be done which should not be done, it must be denied though it asks that other things be done which should be done, unless the request is separately and specifically directed to each thing asked to be done, and if two or more parties jointly join in a

motion and ask that certain things be done and the motion is good as to one but bad as to the other, the motion must be overruled. Neal v. Baker, 198 Ind. 393–401, 153 

N. E. 768; Cuthrell v. Cuthrell, 101 Ind. 375. No error can be taken advantage of on appeal from incompetent evidence unless the attention of the court below was called to it and an opportunity given to the court to correct it. Pittsburg, etc. v. Knox, 177 Ind. 344-54, 98 N. E. 

295. A motion addressed to pleadings should be in writing and should state the grounds upon which it is based. 

Crystal Ice Co. v. Morris, 160 Ind. 651, 67 N. E. 502. 

A motion is an objection. Houk v. Barthold, 73 Ind. 21-29. 

BILL OF EXCEPTIONS. 

The purpose of a bill of exceptions is to put on the record that which is not otherwise a part of it. However, the statute, Burns 1933, 2—3104; Burns 1926, 684; Baldwin, 462, provides that anything which at common law was not a part of the record may be made a part of it by a bill of exceptions though it is made a part of the record by statute.

No particular form of exception is required. The bill must be in writing, contain the caption of the cause to identify it, a statement of the matter to which objection is made, the objection, the offer to prove, if the objection is to a question, the ruling of the court, the exception thereto, the time when the bill is presented to the Judge for signature, the signature of the Judge and, if it contains all the evidence given in the cause, a statement of that fact, must be filed as a paper in the case, which filing

must be shown by a record entry and not by a statement in the bill, unless it is filed at the term the exception is taken; Jenkins v. Wilson, 140 Ind. 544, 40 N. E. 39; 

Johnson v. State, 65 Ind. 269; Noblesville, etc. v. Teters, 1 App. 322, 27 N. E. 635; but the filing may be shown by the certificate of the clerk. Allen v. Gilkison, 76 App. 

233, 132 N. E. 12. The bill may contain one or more exceptions and more than one bill may be filed. 

Bowlers v. State, 130 Ind. 227, 28 N. E. 1115; and if time is given to file "a bill of exceptions" more than one may be filed within that time. Davis v. Mercer Lumber Co., 164 Ind. 413, 73 N. E. 899. The record other than the bill should show that time was given to file it and the length of time given and, if length of time is given beyond the term, there can be no extension of it except by agreement of the parties in writing filed with the clerk as a paper in the cause. Lotz v. Briggs, 50 Ind. 346, or as provided in Burns 1933, 2—3107; Burns 1926, 687; Baldwin, 460. In computing the time, the day time is given is excluded, and the last day on which it may be presented is included. Baker v. The Arctic and etc., 54 Ind. 310; 

State v. Thorn, 28 Ind. 306. If this day is Sunday it may be filed the next day. Burns 1926, 903; Baldwin, 1211.

The Judge who makes the decision to which the exception is taken must sign the bill of exceptions containing the exception to that ruling, unless he has ceased to be a Judge and has been regularly succeeded in the case by another Judge. Reed v. Worland, 64 Ind. 216. If an attorney in a case becomes Judge of the court in which the cause is pending, he can not sign a bill of exceptions in that cause. Winters v. Coons, 162 Ind. 26, 69 N. E. 

458. A special Judge who grants leave to file a bill of exceptions may sign it. Shugart v. Miles, 125 Ind. 445, 25 N. E. 551; Pottlitzer v. Citizens Trust Co., 60 App. 45, 108 N. E. 36.

A bill of exceptions is a record and the approval of it is a judicial act and cannot be performed by any one who is not a Judge of the court in which the cause is pending, nor can it be done by deputy. Toledo v. Rogers, 48 Ind.

427; Smith v. Baugh, 32 Ind. 163. The bill imports absolute verity. Walls v. The Anderson Co., 60 Ind. 56, and if there is a conflict between it and other portions of the record, the bill controls, The Indiana, etc. v. Adams, 112 

Ind. 302, 14 N. E. 80; but if there is a conflict in the bill it impeaches itself. Lee v. Templeton, 88 Ind. 256. The signing of the bill is a ministerial act and may be compelled by mandate. Jelley v. Roberts, 50 Ind. 1. The bill should be prepared by the party desiring it; Robinson v. 

Johnson, 61 Ind. 535, then presented to the Judge; who should in it state the time of presentation in order to fix the time; then be presented to opposing counsel who should approve or disapprove it, stating his objections and then presented to the Judge whose business it is to see that it states the truth before he signs it. Jelley v. 

Roberts, 50 Ind. 1. If the bill is presented to the Judge in time, his delay in signing and filing it will not prevent its becoming a part of the record and when signed it is his duty to cause it to be filed. Burns 1933, 2-3106; 

Burns 1926, 686; Baldwin, 459. The bill may be corrected at any time while the cause is in fieri. Moerbeke v. 

Bryan, 183 Ind. 591, 108 N. E. 948; and after the cause has ceased to be in fieri, it may be amended by a nunc pro tunc entry if there is some memorandum, memorial paper, record or other minutes of the transaction to amend by, of a date not later than the date of the bill of exceptions. Morgan v. Hays, 91 Ind. 132, but it can not be amended on parol evidence alone; Hamilton v. Burch, 28 Ind. 233.

If the bill is signed and filed the same day, the presumption is it was signed before it was filed. Minnick v. 

State, 154 Ind. 379, 56 N. E. 851. A bill of exceptions, signed on Sunday is invalid, unless a necessity for signing it then is shown. Roberts v. Farmers, etc., 136 Ind. 

154, 36 N. E. 128. If time is not given on overruling a motion for a new trial in which to file a bill of exceptions, time cannot thereafter be given, and if the bill is not filed at the term the motion is overruled, it cannot be made a part of the record. Taylor v. State, 191 Ind. 200,

132 N. E. 294. If a party has been diligent, free from negligence and in good faith, has his prepared bill at the court, office or any place where the Judge might reasonably be expected to be and is prevented from presenting it to the Judge because of his absence from the state or circuit, the act is such a presentation of the bill, if promptly presented to the Judge upon his return, as will authorize the latter upon notice and motion to insert nunc pro tunc the date when the presentation was prevented by his absence. Lengelsen v. McGregor, 162 Ind. 

258, 67 N. E. 524. A rule of court concerning the presentation of a bill of exceptions to opposing counsel and to the Judge is valid. State v. Adair, 34 App. 622, 73 

N. E. 611.

A written instrument, or documentary evidence, may be put in a bill of exceptions without copying it by the words "here insert" but the instrument must be clearly identified. Burns 1933, 2-3105; Burns 1926, 685; Baldwin, 455; Cincinnati v. Butler, 103 Ind. 31; Kesler v. 

Myers, 41 Ind. 543, and a plat may be so put in. Toledo v. Cupp, 8 App. 388, 35 N. E. 703; and when the transcript is made, the instrument must be written out in the bill at the place where it is made a part of the bill by a (Here Insert). Board v. Karp, 90 Ind. 236; Kesler v. 

Myers, 41 Ind. 543. Nothing can be put in by a (Here 

Insert) except that which the clerk must copy, unless thé statute authorizes it. Wagoner v. Wilson, 108 Ind. 210, 8 N. E. 925. A master commissioner's report of evidence can not be incorporated in a bill of exceptions by a (Here Insert). It must be inserted in the bill before it is signed. 

City of New Albany v. The Iron, etc., 141 Ind. 500, 40 

N. E. 44; Kealer v. Myers, 41 Ind. 543, and so a longhand manuscript of the evidence; The Ohio, etc. v. Voight, 122 

Ind. 288, 23 N. E. 774. Parties by agreement can not put anything into a bill of exceptions. Blair v. Curry, 150 

Ind. 99, 46 N. E. 672.

The original bill of exceptions containing the evidence and a copy of the instructions may be certified as a part of the transcript on appeal without copying it. Burns

1933, 2—3111; Burns 1926, 691; Baldwin, 456; Leach v. 

Mattix, 149 Ind. 146, 48 N. E. 791; but if the bill contains anything other than the evidence and a copy of the instructions that part so contained will not be considered. 

Leach v. Mattix, 149 İnd. 146, 48 N. E. 791; Mitchell v. 

Beissenherz, 192 Ind. 587, 135 N. E. 885; Acts 1933, p. 918.

The evidence and instructions should be in one bill and other things in another.

On overruling a motion for a new trial, if an exception is taken and time is given in which to file a bill of exceptions, all exceptions properly taken to every decision of the court which are included in the motion are carried forward to the time of ruling on the motion and may be included in the bill for which time is given to file. Burns 

1933, 2-3105; Burns 1926, 685; Baldwin, 455.

FORM OF BILL CONTAINING ALL THE EVIDENCE. 

Caption. No. 

Be it remembered in the above entitled cause that the plaintiff, to support the issues on his behalf offered the following evidence :- 

The plaintiff having been duly sworn, testified as follows:- 

Q. Are you the plaintiff in this case? 

A. Yes. 

Q. Did you have a conversation with the decedent in reference to the claim you have filed against his estate? A. Q. Yes. 

State that conversation.

The defendant objected for the reason (here state the ground of objection).

The plaintiff stated he can and will prove by the witness in answer to the question (here state what will be proved).

The court sustained the objection, to which ruling the plaintiff at the time excepted.

And the plaintiff also offered in evidence Exhibit A. to the introduction of which in evidence the defendant objected (here state grounds of objection).

The court overruled the objection to which ruling the defendant at the time excepted, and the Exhibit is read in evidence,

This should be followed by all the evidence offered by the plaintiff in chief, together with all objections, offers to prove, rulings of the court and exceptions thereto. 

Then the defendant to sustain the issues on his behalf offered the following evidence including all objections, offers to prove, rulings and exceptions:

C. D. the defendant, having been duly sworn, testified as 

Q. Are you the defendant in this case? 

A. Yes, as administrator.

Q. Did you have any conversation with the plaintiff about the claim sued on in this case? 

A. No. 

Q. Did you have any conversation with his wife about it in his presence? 

A. Yes. 

Q. State that conversation.

The plaintiff objected (here state the ground of objection). 

The defendant offered to prove and stated he could prove by the witness in answer to the question that the plaintiff said the claim had been paid.

The Court: Objection sustained. To which ruling of the court the defendant at the time excepted. 

And the defendant also offered in evidence Exhibit B, to the introduction of which in evidence the plaintiff objected (here state the ground of objection).

The Court: Objection overruled, to which ruling of the court the plaintiff at the time excepted, and the exhibit is read in evidence. (This should be followed by all the evidence offered by the defendant in answer, together with all objections, offers to prove, rulings of the court and exceptions in connection with such evidence.)

And the plaintiff offered in rebuttal the following (Here set out the questions, objections, offers to prove, rulings of the court and exceptions and answers as above.)

This should be followed by any evidence in rebuttal giving objections, etc., as above, and also any other evidence that may be offered with objections, etc., as above, always showing which party offered the evidence,

SPECIAL  

73 

And now

And this was all the evidence given in said cause. on this, the 20th day of June, 1931, the plaintiff presents to me, this, his bill of exceptions, containing the evidence in said cause and asks that the same be signed, sealed, filed and made a part of the record, which is done, this the 30th day of June, 1931. 

E. F. Judge.

SPECIAL BILL OF EXCEPTIONS. 

On appeal from any judgment, decree, or interlocutory order of a circuit or superior court in a civil action, or the Judge of any such Court in vacation, the bill of exceptions need not contain all the evidence, unless it is contended the verdict of the jury, or the decision of the Court, is not sustained by sufficient evidence, or is contrary to law.

It shall be sufficient if the bill contains a clear statement of the ruling or matter called in question, together with a succinct recital of the substance of such part of the evidence and proceedings as shall be necessary to advise the Court to which the appeal is taken of the pertinency or materiality of the matter sought to be reviewed. Burns 1933, 2-3108; Burns 1926, 688; Baldwin, 463; Burns 1933, 2-3109; Burns 1926, 689; Baldwin, 464. "Unless the bill of exceptions shall contain matter showing that the order, ruling, action, decision or matter called in question was harmless to the party appealing, or that the error, if any, was cured or corrected by the trial Court, the bill of exceptions prepared as herein provided shall be sufficient to bring the matter therein presented before the Court for review in as ample a form as though such bill of exceptions contained all the evidence and all the proceedings upon the trial." Burns 1933, 2—3110; Burns 1926, 690; 

Baldwin, 465.

Under this statute all questions of law which must be called to the attention of the Court by motion for a new

SPECIAL  

trial, except that the decision of the Court or verdict of the jury is not sustained by sufficient evidence or is contrary to law, may be presented on appeal without taking up all the evidence. The record must contain the pleadings, the question of law on which the decision was made, the objection, the exception, the motion for a new trial, and final judgment, and everything necessary to be shown if the appeal was supported by a general bill of exceptions, except that the special bill may be used instead of the general when the appellant is not relying on the verdict or decision being contrary to law or not supported by sufficient evidence. Lawrence v. Oliver, 51 App. 434, 99 N. E. 809; Cross v. Cross, 156 Ind. 378, 59 N. E. 1049.

As such proceeding is under a special statute it must be strictly complied with. Thomas v. City of Huntington, 80 App. 426, 140 N. E. 66. The bill must contain a clear statement of the ruling called in question and a succinct recital of the substance of such parts of the evidence and proceedings as may be necessary to advise the Court of the pertinency or materiality of the matter sought to be reviewed. Atkinson v. Maris, 40 App. 718, 81 N. E. 745.

It is not enough for the bill to say, "There was evidence tending to prove the material averments of the complaint. Petty v. Frakes, 75 App. 425, 129 N. E. 246.

Caption. 

SPECIAL BILL UNDER SECTION 2-3108 supra. 

BE IT REMEMBERED, in the above entitled cause, that A. B., a witness, produced by the plaintiff, testified as follows:- "I heard the defendant say he had not repaid to the plaintiff the money plaintiff had loaned him and for which he gave the note in suit." 

AND BE IT FURTHER REMEMBERED, that the plaintiff tendered to the Court, before the argument to the jury began, the following instruction and asked the Court to give it to the jury:- "If you find from the evidence in this case that the note in suit has not been paid then I instruct you that you should find for the plaintiff. Said request was entitled, in writing and signed by plaintiff. 

---

SPECIAL  

75 The Court refused to give this instruction to the jury, to which refusal of the Court the plaintiff did at the time except. 

AND BE IT FURTHER REMEMBERED, that on the trial of said cause, the defendant produced D, a witness, to whom the defendant asked the following questions:- :- "Did you hear the defendant say anything in regard to his having paid the note in suit?" To which question the witness said: "Yes." Defendant then asked this question:-"State what you heard him say" to which question the plaintiff objected for the reason that it was not shown that he, the plaintiff, was present when the statement was made. The Court overruled the objection to which ruling of the Court plaintiff at the time excepted, and the witness said "I heard the defendant say that he had fully paid the plaintiff all that he owed him.' 

AND BE IT Further RemeMBERED, there is no other evidence in the case which showed, or tended to show, that said note had been paid. 

AND BE IT FURTHER REMEMBERED, that the Court gave to the jury the following instruction:- "If you find that the defendant has paid to plaintiff the note in suit I instruct you that you should find for the defendant."

To the giving of which instruction plaintiff did at the time except. 

AND NOW ON THIS the 31st day of March 1933, the day on which the Court instructed the jury in said cause, plaintiff presents this his bill of exceptions to the Judge of said Court and asks that the same be signed, sealed, filed, and made a part of the record, which is done this 1st day of April 1933. 

G. H. Judge.

The above bill of exceptions is believed to be sufficient in all respects to present to the Court on appeal the error arising on the refusal of the Court to give the instruction tendered by the plaintiff, and also sufficient to present to such Court the error of the Court in the admission of improper evidence, and also sufficient to present to the Appellate Court the error in giving an improper instruction.

Any number of errors arising on the trial may be stated in the special bill of exceptions.

RESERVED QUESTION OF LAW 

Under this statute unless the appellant relies upon the verdict of the jury or the decision of the Court being not supported by the evidence or being contrary to law, there is no reason why all the evidence should be made a part of the record by a bill of exceptions and to make it so is an unjustifiable waste of time, energy and money. 

RESERVED QUESTION OF LAW. 

“Either party may reserve any question of law decided by the Court during the progress of the cause, for the decision of the Supreme Court. Any question of law so reserved may be taken to the Supreme Court upon the bill of exceptions showing the decision, or if it arises on demurrer, upon the pleadings involved. When the question so reserved is shown by the bill of exceptions, the party excepting shall notify the Court that he intends to take the question of law to the Supreme Court upon the bill of exceptions only; and the Court shall thereupon cause the bill of exceptions to be so made that it will distinctly and briefly embrace so much of the record of the cause only and the statement of the Court as will enable the Supreme Court to apprehend the particular question involved." Burns 1933, 2—3114; 

Burns 1926, 694; Baldwin, 469. The statute Burns 1933, 9-2102; Burns 1926, 2329; Baldwin, 2314, authorizes the

State in a criminal case to appeal on a reserved question of law.

This statute authorizes the reservation of questions of law arising on demurrer and those shown by a bill of exceptions which do not arise on a demurrer. As the

RESERVED QUESTION OF LAW 

77 record made on ruling on the pleadings always shows the particular questions involved it is not necessary for the Court to be told the case will be taken to the Supreme Court on such ruling. It is only when the case will be appealed on a reserved question of law arising on some other ruling that it is necessary to inform the Court of that fact. There can be no such appeal unless the Court is so informed, and the record must show that fact. Shugart v. Miles, 125 Ind. 445, 25 N. E. 551. If the reserved question of law arises on the trial of the cause, a motion for a new trial is necessary and all the evidence which has any bearing on the reserved question of law must be made a part of the record by a bill of exceptions. Connor v. The Town of Marion, 112 Ind. 517, 14 N. E. 488. The record must show an exception to the ruling complained of, but it is not necessary at each ruling to state to the court that an appeal will be taken on a reserved question. 

It is sufficient when the motion for a new trial is overruled for the party who filed it to then inform the Court an appeal will be taken on certain reserved questions, naming them. Shugart v. Miles, 125 Ind. 445, 25 N. E. 

551. There must be a final judgment, Taylor v. Board, 120 Ind. 121, 22 N. E. 108; and the record must contain the pleadings. Lawrence v. Typewriter Co., 51 App. 434, 99 N. E. 809. Any question on the instructions may be reserved by setting them out, the exceptions to them and so much of the pleadings and record as may be necessary to fully and accurately present on appeal the real question which was actually presented to and passed on by the trial court. Short v. Stuteman, 81 Ind. 115. "We have no doubt that an independent and distinct ruling upon evidence may be presented as a reserved question of law under the statute but we are equally clear that questions and answers connected with other evidence can not be reserved by a bill which does not bring in all the evidence upon the subject to which the questions and answers relate." Smith v. James, 131 Ind. 131-34.

Questions on the pleadings which do not arise on demurrer may be reserved and more than one question may

RESERVED QUESTION OF LAW 

be reserved. Elliott on Appellate Procedure, Sec. 237. It seems any question now made a part of the record without a bill of exceptions may be reserved without a bill, for since the above quoted act was passed many things which were not a part of the record except made so by a bill are now a part of it without a bill. Burns 1933, 2-3104; Burns 1926, 684; Baldwin, 462.

The statute says appeals may be taken to the Supreme Court on a reserved question of law, but as the Appellate Court has been organized since that statute was enacted and the statute Burns 1933, 4-214; Burns 1926, 1356;

Baldwin, 1356, gives jurisdiction to the Appellate Court in all cases in which it is not given to the Supreme Court, such questions may be presented on appeal to the Appellate Court in all cases that may be appealed to it, and this is confirmed by the practice in that Court. Thomas v. City of Huntington, 80 App. 476, 141 N. E. 358.

To appeal under this section the statute must be strictly complied with in all its essential provisions, 80 App. 476, supra. A party may abandon his appeal under this section and appeal under the law providing generally for appeals. Curry v. Glessinger, 50 App. 166, 96 N. E. 190; but he can not appeal under the law providing generally for appeals, abandon such an appeal and base his appeal on the above section. Shugart v. Miles, 125 Ind. 445, 25 

N. E. 551. Only questions of law can be reserved. Fouty v. Morrison, 73 Ind. 333 and "If the error complained of consists in sustaining or overruling a demurrer to the complaint, the transcript should only contain the complaint, the demurrer, ruling of the court thereon, an exception to such ruling, and the final judgment. If the question reserved arises on the ruling of the court on demurrer to the answer, then the complaint, answer, demurrer thereto, the ruling of the court thereon, the exception to such ruling and the final judgment should be embraced in the transcript. If the question reserved relates to the ruling of the court on a demurrer to the reply, the transcript should consist of the complaint, answer, reply,

ORDER OF COURT 

79 demurrer thereto, the ruling of the court, thereon, the exception and final judgment.' Buskirk's Practice, p. 72. 

When an appeal is taken on a reserved question of law, the presumptions that are considered in a general appeal are applied to such an appeal. Indiana, etc. v. Adams, 112 Ind. 302, 14 N. E. 80. "In the absence of any evidence relating to the points of exception, we are bound to presume that the evidence, if present, would sustain the rulings of the court and the overruling of the motion for a new trial." Downs v. Opp, 82 Ind. 166. 

Burns 1933, 2-3223, Burns 1926, 717, Baldwin, 497 is, "that when, in any case, an appeal is prosecuted upon the question of the correctness of instructions given or refused, or the modifications thereof, it shall not be necessary to set out in the record all the evidence given in the cause, but it shall be sufficient, in the bill of exceptions, to set out the instructions or modifications excepted to, with a recital of the fact that the same were applicable to the evidence in the cause."

ORDER OF COURT. 

The Statute Burns 1933, 2-3223; Burns 1926, 717;

Baldwin, 497, provides "every paper and pleading above excepted may be made a part of the record by (bill of) exceptions or order of the court on motion."

The power to make anything a part of the record by either a bill of exceptions or order of court is statutory. 

Close v. Pittsburg, etc., 150 Ind. 560, and can be done

VERDICT 

only on motion by one of the parties. Board v. Gibson, 158 Ind. 471; and that which is sought to be made a part of the record by such an order must be actually set outcopied into the order. It is not sufficient to refer to it. 

Close v. Pittsburgh, etc., 150 Ind. 560, and nothing can be so made a part of the record except papers and documents. Russ v. Russ, 142 Ind. 471. Why try to make a record this way? Is it not too dangerous? Pennsylvania 

Co. v. Hemmer, 189 N. E. 137.

VERDICT. 

The verdict of the jury is its decision on the facts in issue in the case, is in writing, must be signed by the foreman and delivered in open court to the Judge of the court, Burns 1933, 2-2020; Burns 1926, 596; Baldwin, 353; Willett v. Porter, 42 Ind. 250; Kelley v. Grand 

Trunk, etc., 46 App. 697, 93 N. E. 616; all of which must be shown by the record. If the action is for the recovery of money, the amount recovered must be stated. Burns 

1933, 2—2024; Burns 1926, 600; Baldwin, 357; and if for the recovery of specific personal property, the value of the same must be stated and also the amount of damage sustained, if there is a judgment for the return of the property. Burns 1933, 2-2025; Burns 1926, 601; Baldwin, 358. When the jury returns its verdict, either party may poll it, and if any juror dissents, they shall again be sent out to deliberate. Burns 1933, 2—2020; Burns 1926, 596; Baldwin, 353. To poll the jury is to ask each juror "Is this your verdict?" and the question is to be asked by the clerk. Bowen v. Bowen, 74 Ind. 470. It seems no

VERDICT 

81 particular words must be used, but whatever words are used they must mean the same as the above words and no more. State Life, etc. v. Postal, 43 App. 144, 84 N. E. 

156. Until the verdict has been received and the jury discharged any juror in a criminal case has the right to dissent and as the verdict must receive the assent of all the jurors, it is subject to reconsideration while in fieri. 

The jury may be instructed to return a verdict if, in a civil case, five-sixths or more of the jury agree. Either party may then poll the jury and if more than two members dissent, the jury shall be sent out to deliberate. 

Acts, p. 24 (1929); State Life v. Postal, supra; Gianino v. 

State, 183 Ind. 199, 108 N. E. 579. By agreement of opposing counsel and the court, the jury may be permitted after agreeing on a verdict, to seal it up, deliver it to the foreman who shall keep it and bring it into open court, when the jury, after separating, has reassembled, and then return the verdict in open court. If the verdict is defective, the court may on its own motion, or on the motion of counsel, order the jury to return to the jury room and make the correction, and if the correction is merely formal, it may be made by direction of the court in the presence and with the assent of the jury. Pehlman v. The State, 115 Ind. 131, 17 N. E. 270.

A verdict may be received on Sunday, Jones v. Johnson, 61 Ind. 257, but it cannot be received out of court, without consent of the parties nor in court in the absence of the parties or their attorneys, unless after notice they have an opportunity to be present and fail to do so. Cox v. Peltier, 159 Ind. 355, 65 N. E. 6; nor can the jury be re-instructed in the absence of the parties or their attorneys, unless they have had notice of the proposed instruction and fail to appear. Cox v. Peltier, 159 Ind. 355, 65

N. E. 6; nor can it be received by any one except the 

Judge who presided at the trial, or a special Judge appointed in that case. Willett v. Porter, 42 Ind. 250; 

Quinn v. State, 130 Ind. 340, 30 N. E. 300.

Surplusages in verdicts will be disregarded and a caption to a verdict is surplusage. Rogers v. Overton, 87

VENIRE DE Novo 

Ind. 410. Verdicts are good, though informal, if the court can understand them so as to render a judgment on them. Kelley v. Bell, 172 Ind. 590, 88 N. E. 58; and they must be reasonably construed and will be disregarded only from necessity. Kendall v. State, 183 Ind. 

162, 105 N. E. 899. If there are defects in the verdict and no objections to them are made, they are waived. Wolcott v. Yeager, 11 Ind. 84. A verdict arrived at by chance should be set aside, and that it was so rendered may be shown by any one who knows the fact, except that jurors are not competent to impeach their verdict but are competent to sustain it. The Chicago, etc. v. McDaniel, 134 

Ind. 166, 32 N. E. 728. Questions as to a defective verdict must be raised by motion for a venire de novo. Dolan v. 

State, 122 Ind. 141, 23 N. E. 761.

Special verdicts have been abolished and general verdicts now are the only ones known to the law. Kelley v. 

Bell, 172 Ind. 590-97; Burns 1933, 2—2021; Burns 1926, 597; Baldwin, 354.

VENIRE DE NOVO. 

A venire de novo is a motion asking, after a verdict has been returned, that the verdict be set aside and the case re-tried. It must be made before judgment. Smith v. 

Biesiada, 174 Ind. 134, 90 N. E. 1009; for safety, should be in writing. Douglas v. Indianapolis, etc., 37 App. 332, 76 N. E. 892; must, whether oral or written, state the reasons and the record must show these. Douglas v. 

Indianapolis, etc., 37 App. 332, 76 N. E. 892; is not applicable to a special finding by the court, Maxwell v. Wright, 483

MOTION FOR VENIRE DE Novo 

83 

160 Ind. 515, 67 N. E. 267; does not take the place of a motion for a new trial, and should not be granted unless the finding is so defective, ambiguous or uncertain that no judgment can be rendered on it. Knight v. Kerfoot, 184 Ind. 31, 110 N. E. 206; Page v. State, 193 Ind. 442, 139 N. E. 143; or where the verdict finds less than the whole matter in issue. Cottrell v. Shadley, 77 Ind. 348; 

Maxwell v. Wright, 160 Ind. 515. A motion for a venire de novo will not be sustained unless the verdict is so defective and uncertain upon its face that no judgment can be pronounced upon it. A verdict, however informal, is good if the court can understand it. It is to have a reasonable intendment, and is to receive a reasonable construction, and must not be avoided, except from necessity. Kelley v. Bell, 172 Ind. 590, 88 N. E. 58.

MOTION FOR VENIRE DE NOVO. 

Caption. No. 

In the above entitled cause the plaintiff moves the court for a venire de novo for each of the following reasons: 

1. The verdict is so defective, ambiguous and uncertain that no judgment can be rendered on it. 

2. The amount that the plaintiff should recover from the defendant is not stated in the verdict. 

3. Any other reason, etc. 

A. B. Attorney for Plaintiff.

NEW TRIAL NEW TRIAL. 

Both the common law, Blackstone Book 3, 387, and the 

Statute Burns 1933, 2-2401; Burns 1926, 610; Baldwin, 368, authorize the granting of a new trial and the granting of it supersedes the effect of the former trial. Louisville, etc. v. Miller, 141 Ind. 533, 37 N. E. 343; Blackstone 

Book 3, p. 391. If a new trial is erroneously granted the injured party may take his exception and present this error, if in the next trial he is defeated. Louisville, etc. v. Miller, 141 Ind. 533, 37 N. E. 343.

The question of a new trial is presented by a motion in writing in which is stated the causes for which a new trial is asked and which motion must be signed by the party asking the new trial, or his attorney, within 30 days from the time the verdict or decision is rendered, but if the court is adjourned before the expiration of the 30 days, the motion may be filed in the office of the clerk of said court within said 30 days. Burns 1933, 2-2404; 

Burns 1926, 613; Baldwin, 371; Burns 1933, 2-2403; 

Burns 1926, 612; Baldwin, 370. If the court is in session the motion must be filed in open court and presented to the court and then contain the reasons why it should be granted, merely writing out and filing a motion for a new trial is not making an application for it within the meaning of the statute. Emison v. Shepard, 121 Ind. 

184, 22 N. E. 883. If a paper in a case is authorized to be filed, it must be filed in open court and presented to the court, if the court is in session, and when it is filed in the office of the clerk because the court is not in session, it should be called to the court's attention within a reasonable time. Intermediate Co. v. Cunningham, 59 App. 

326-28, 108 N. E. 17.

The parties to a cause cannot extend the time for filing a motion for a new trial by an agreement. Talbot v. 

Meyer, 183 Ind. 585, 169 N. E. 841; but during the time for filing such a motion a supplemental motion may be filed, and a motion may be withdrawn and another filed

NEW TRIAL 

85 and the supplemental motion and the one to which it is supplemental will be considered as one motion. Fisher v. 

Southern Ry. Co., 55 App. 599, 104 N. E. 521; but a supplemental motion cannot be filed after the time for filing the original motion. H. W. Johns-Manville Co. v. 

South Shore Co., 70 App. 484, 123 N. E. 648. If causes for a new trial are discovered after the term at which the verdict or decision is rendered, the application for a new trial may be made at any time within a year after the final judgment was rendered, by a complaint filed with the clerk not later than the second term after the discovery and summons shall issue as on other complaints and the same shall be summarily decided by the court on the evidence. Burns 1933, 2—2405; Burns 1926, 614; Baldwin, 372. Such a proceeding is an independent action and it must appear such evidence is not cumulative, would probably change the result, that it was discovered subsequent to the term at which the original trial was had and that diligence was used to discover it before the original trial. Meldon v. Cox, 60 App. 403, 110 N. E. 1008. 

If the motion for a new trial is filed with the clerk, he shall make a minute of the date of the filing of the motion on the entry docket with his initials and shall also make a vacation order book entry showing the filing and the date of the same. Burns 1933, 2-2403; Burns 1926, 612; 

Baldwin, 370. 

There are eight causes for a new trial. Burns 1933, 2—2401; Burns 1926, 610; Baldwin, 368. But it is not necessary that these particular causes should be stated in it. It is sufficient, if the particular grounds for which a new trial will be granted are stated, without stating the particular class for which a new trial will be granted or to which the reasons belong. Buskirk, p. 86, Elliott S. 849.

The following are causes for a new trial under the first cause, i. e., irregularity in the proceedings, etc.: (a) Application for continuance civil and criminal, Collett v. State, 156 Ind. 64, 59 N. E. 168; Torphy v. 

State, 188 Ind. 30, 121 N. E. 659.

NEW TRIAL 

(b) Change of venue, civil and criminal, Scanlin v. 

Steward, 138 Ind. 574, 37 N. E. 401; Munce v. State, 187 Ind. 263, 118 N. E. 953. (c) Motion to suppress depositions. Burnett v. Milnes, 148 Ind. 230-36, 46 N. E. 464. (d) Granting or refusing trial by jury. Alley v. State, 76 Ind. 94. (e) (f) 

Incompetency of jurors. Townsend v. State, 147 

Ind. 624, 47 N. E. 19. 

Improper argument by counsel. Porter v. Choen, 60 Ind. 338. (g) Appointment of special judge. Walb v. Eshelman, 176 Ind. 253, 94 N. E. 586. (h) Motion to remand. Indpls., etc. Co. v. Seerley, 35 

App. 467, 75 N. E. 837.

No question arising on the pleadings is a cause for a new trial. Bradley v. Onstott, 180 Ind. 687, 103 N. E. 798. 

The following are causes for new trial under second cause, i. e., misconduct of jury or prevailing party: (a) Taking meals at restaurant of a juror. Pittsburg v. Welch, 12 App. 433, 40 N. E. 650. (b) Jurors conversing with others. Masterson v. State, 144 Ind. 240, 43 N. E. 138. (c) 

Drinking intoxicating liquor. 

Ind. 240, 36 N. E. 1108. 

Brown v. State, 137 (d) Taking notes of the evidence. Cheek v. State, 35

Tampering with juror. Huston v. Vail, 51 Ind. 299. (g) False pretense of prevailing party. City of Indpls. v. Jansel, 157 Ind. 463, 62 N. E. 35. (h) Reading from law books, etc. Johnson v. Culver, 116 Ind. 278, 19 N. E. 129. (i) (j) 

Improper argument to jury. 

160 Ind. 435, 67 N. E. 183. 

Perry, etc. v. Wilson, Can misconduct in argument be cured and how? 

Perry, etc. v. Wilson, 160 Ind. 435, 67 N. E. 183; 

West Muncie, etc. v. Slack, 164 Ind. 21, 72 N. E. 879.
