87 (k) The second, third, and seventh causes must be supported by affidavit. Burns 1933, 2-2404; Burns 

1926, 613; Baldwin, 371. 

The following are causes for new trial under third cause, i. e., accident or surprise, etc.: (a) Statements made by one party to the other which mislead him. Haynes v. State, 45 Ind. 424. (b) Statements by court which mislead. Edsall v. 

Ayres, 15 Ind. 286. (c) Accident, sickness, etc. Cyc. Vol. 29, p. 859. C. J. 

Vol. 46, p. 214.

The party surprised, if in court, should move a continuance. Ellis v. City of Hammond, 157 Ind. 267, 61 

N. E. 565. The motion for a new trial for this cause should set forth the facts upon which a continuance was asked, the testimony, if for an absent witness, aver that no other witness will testify to the same facts, show by the statement of facts that the party asking it has not been guilty of negligence in the matter, and be supported by affidavit. Burns 1933, 2-1524; Burns 1926, 483; 

Baldwin, 234; Burns 1933, 2-2404; Burns 1926, 613;

Baldwin, 371. As a rule a new trial for this cause will not be granted on account of surprise at any testimony competent under the issues. Fudge v. Marquell, 164 Ind. 

447, 72 N. E. 565. 

The following are causes for new trial under the fourth cause, i. e., excessive damages: (a) This cause applies to tort cases only. Conner v. 

Andrews Land Co., 162 Ind. 338, 70 N. E. 376; 

Federal Life Ins Co. v. Maxam, 70 App. 266, 117 

N. E. 801. (b) If the damages are considered excessive, a new trial must be asked on that ground. Rout v. Menifree, 59 Ind. 525. (c) If judgment is rendered for more than what is demanded in the complaint, this is no cause for a new trial. McKinney v. State, 117 Ind. 26, 19 N. E. 613.

NEW TRIAL 

(d) The damages are never regarded excessive unless they indicate prejudice, partiality, corruption, etc. 

Chicago, etc. v. Stierwalt, 87 App. 478, 153 N. E. 807. (e) A new trial for this cause may be prevented by entering a remitter, by the court or the party. 

Schafer v. Smith, 63 Ind. 226; Conwell v. Jeger, 21 

App. 110, 51 N. E. 731. (f) The evidence alone can be considered in determining whether the damages are excessive. Cleveland, etc. v. Miller, 165 Ind. 381, 74 N. E. 509. 

The following are causes for new trial under fifth cause, i. e., error in the assessment in the amount of money, whether too large or too small: (a) If the action is on contract, this cause must be assigned and no other, when the amount of money is either too large or too small. Federal Life Ins. 

Co. v. Maxam, 70 App. 266, 117 N. E. 801. (b) And the same principle applies if this action is for the improper detention of property. Chicago, etc., Co. v. Barger, 82 App. 266, 144 N. E. 646. (c) A failure to assess nominal damages is no cause for a new trial. Coffin v. State, 144 Ind. 578, 43 

N. E. 654. (d) The motion must state in what the error consistswhether too large or too small. Boggs v. Toney, 50 App. 289, 98 N. E. 306. (e) State ex rel. v. Richeson, 36 App. 373, 75 N. E. 846. 

See this case.

The following are causes for new trial under the sixth cause, i. e., the verdict or decision is not sustained by sufficient evidence, or is contrary to law. (a) The word "verdict" is applicable only to the finding of the jury. (b) The word "decision" is applicable only to the finding of the court. Gates v. Baltimore, etc., 154 Ind. 

338, 56 N. E. 722; Indpls., etc., v. Trapschuh, 63 

App. 120, 114 N. E. 99.

NEW TRIAL 

89 (c) Under this cause when the case is tried by a jury, the proper assignment is "the verdict is not sustained by sufficient evidence," or "is contrary to law." Stevens v. Leonard, 154 Ind. 67, 56 N. E. 27. (d) If the case is tried by the court, it may be assigned as a cause for a new trial that the "finding, › in- stead of the "decision" is not sustained by sufficient evidence, or is contrary to law. Ellison v. 

Ganiard, 167 Ind. 471, 79 N. E. 450. (e) The word "decision" as employed in the above section of the code means the finding of the court upon the facts where the cause is tried by the court and has no application where the trial is by a jury. 

Gates v. Baltimore, etc., 154 Ind. 338, 56 N. E. 722. (f) If a special finding is made, the decision of the court includes the entire finding. Major v. Miller, 165 Ind. 275, 75 N. E. 159. (g) The evidence must be sufficient to justify a recovery on the theory of the complaint, secundum allegata et probata. Louisville, etc., v. Renicker, 8 App. 404, 35 N. E. 1047. (h) A verdict or decision is contrary to law when it is contrary to the principles of law applicable to the case. Bosseker v. Cramer, 18 Ind. 44; McGuire v. 

State, 50 Ind. 284; Robinson v. Chandler, 56 Ind. 575. (i) (j) The court on appeal will not set aside a verdict or decision on the weight of evidence. Hoskinson v. 

Cavender, 143 Ind. 1, 42 N. E. 358.

A verdict based on a bad complaint is contrary to law. Cincinnati, etc., v. Darling, 130 Ind. 376, 30 

N. E. 416. (k) If there is no evidence to support any material averment in a pleading, the verdict or decision will be set aside. Robbins v. Spencer, 140 Ind. 483, 38 

N. E. 522. (1) If there are answers to interrogatories and they are inconsistent with the general verdict, then it may be assigned for a new trial that they are not

NEW TRIAL 

sustained by the evidence, or that they are contrary to law. If the answers are not inconsistent with the general verdict, the assignment should be that the verdict is not sustained by sufficient evidence or is contrary to law. Sievers v. The Peters 

Box Co., etc., 151 Ind. 642, 50 N. E. 877.

The following are causes for new trial under the seventh cause, i. e., newly-discovered evidence, material for the party applying, which he could not, with reasonable diligence, have discovered and produced at the trial: 

The following is not newly-discovered evidence for which new trial will be granted: (a) Evidence known before the finding of the court is announced. Burke v. Mathews Glass Co., 40 App. 

81, 81 N. E. 88. (b) Evidence that bears only on the question of damages. Ellis v. City of Hammond, 157 Ind. 267, 61 

N. E. 565. (c) Evidence for impeachment. Martin v. Garver, 40 

Ind. 351. (d) Evidence that is merely cumulative. Ray v. Baker, 165 Ind. 74, 74 N. E. 619. (e) Evidence of the same kind to the same point is cumulative but evidence of a different kind but to the same point is not cumulative. Greenleaf, Vol. 1, Sec. 2; Kochel v. Bartlett, 88 Ind. 237.

Under this reason for a new trial, such a trial will not be granted, if by diligence the evidence might have been produced at the trial and the acts showing diligence must be set out in the motion. Zimmerman v. 

Weigel, 158 Ind. 370, 63 N. E. 566; the new evidence must be competent, admissible over objection, and probably produce a different result. Campbell v. Nixon, 25 App. 

90, 56 N. E. 248; Jackson v. Swope, 134 Ind. 111, 33 

N. E. 909. The affidavits of witnesses as to what they will swear to must be secured if possible and the court may compel a witness to make an affidavit. Heaton v. 

White, 85 Ind. 376; Rater v. State, 49 Ind. 507 and this cause must be supported by affidavit. Burns 1933, 2-2404; Burns 1926, 613; Baldwin, 371.

91 There is no provision for a new trial on appeal on an interlocutory order. Shoemaker v. Smith, 74 Ind. 71; 

Guynn v. Newman, 174 Ind. 161, 90 N. E. 759.

FORM OF MOTION FOR A NEW TRIAL FORM OF MOTION FOR A NEW TRIAL. 

In the following form but one instance will be given under each of the eight causes. Any number more can be given until all are stated. 

Caption. No. 

In the above entitled cause the plaintiff moves for a new trial of the same for each of the following reasons: 

1. The court erred in refusing a trial by a jury as asked by the plaintiff. 

2. A new trial should be granted the plaintiff because the defendant in argument was permitted by the court to read to the jury portions of law books. 

3. Because the defendant told the plaintiff the account sued on in the first paragraph of complaint would be admitted and the plaintiff believed such statement, relied on it, did not prepare to prove the account, though he had evidence to make such proof and the defendant did not admit it but introduced evidence tending to show such account was not correct. 

4. The damages are excessive. 

5. The amount of money assessed in favor of the plaintiff is too small. 

6. The verdict (or decision) is not sustained by sufficient evidence or is contrary to law. 

7. Since the verdict was rendered in said cause the plaintiff has discovered new, competent and material evidence for him which he could not with reasonable diligence have discovered and produced at the trial, which will be made to appear by the affidavits of James Smith, John Stevens and himself filed herewith. 

8. a. The court erred in giving to the jury each of the following instructions asked by the defendant. 2, 3, 7 and 10. b. The court erred in permitting A. B. to answer the following question (here set it out) and the answer to which is as follows (here set it out). Indianapolis & W. R. Co. v. Ragan, 171 Ind. 569, 86 N. E. 966.

INSTRUCTIONS AND EXCEPTIONS THERETO 

93 

JUDGMENT. 

It is settled law in this state that the form or substance of a judgment cannot be first questioned in this court, but the question must be first presented to the court below by a motion to modify which must specify wherein it should be corrected and modified and the motion must particularly point out the defect or mistake complained of and ask that the same be corrected. If the court rules against the party asking such correction, such ruling of the court below must be assigned as error in this court. Unless this is done no objection can be made available for reversal here, however erroneous in form or substance such judgment may appear to be. 

Evans v. The State, 150 Ind. 651-55, 50 N. E. 820.

INSTRUCTIONS AND EXCEPTIONS THERETO. 

How made a part of the record.

There are four ways in which instructions may be made a part of the record. 

First. By order of court. 

In Pennsylvania Co. v. Ebaugh, 152 Ind. 531, 53 N. E. 

763, it is held that instructions may be made part of the record under Burns 1933, 2-3223; Burns 1926, 717;

Baldwin, 497, and to do so they must be fully set out in the order. See Indiana, etc. v. Sullivan, 53 App. 239, 101 N. E. 401. 

Second. By complying with Burns 1933, 2-2009; 

Burns 1926, 585; Baldwin, 344.

INSTRUCTIONS AND EXCEPTIONS THERETO 

To do this there must be written on the margin of each instruction or at the close of the same "refused and excepted to" or "given and excepted to" by the party excepting, and which writing must be dated and signed by the Judge; Habick v. University Park, etc., 177 Ind. 

193, 97 N. E. 539; and the record must show by order book entry that the instructions were filed. Muncie, etc. v. Black, 173 Ind. 142, 89 N. E. 845. File marks of the clerk on the instructions will not be considered. Masterton v. So. R. R., 170 Ind. 296, 84 N. E. 505. The exceptions must be taken before the verdict is returned. Jones v. Van Patten, 3 Ind. 107; Roach v. Cumberland, 60 App. 

547, 111 N. E. 320. 

Third. By complying with Burns 1933, 2-2010; 

Burns 1926, 586, Baldwin, 343.

All instructions requested, whether given or refused, and all instructions given by the court of its own motion shall be filed with the clerk of the court at the close of the instruction of the jury. Exceptions to the giving or refusing of instructions may be taken at any time during the term and the same may be taken orally and entered upon the record or minutes of the court or in writing at the close of the instructions requested, or given by the court of its own motion, in which case the party excepting, or his counsel, shall enter at the close of such instructions, a memorandum which shall be dated and signed, setting forth in substance, that such party excepts to the giving or refusing, as the case may be of each of the above instructions designated by its number. All such instructions and all such exceptions and all entries on the record in respect to the same shall be a part of the record without a bill of exceptions. 

Burns 1933, 2-2010; Burns 1926, 586; Baldwin 343.

To make such instructions a part of the record and to save exceptions thereto, under this section, its provisions must be substantially complied with. Morgan 

Construction Co. v. Dulin, 184 Ind. 652, 109 N. E. 960.

Special instructions requested must be signed by the parties or their attorneys. City of Logansport v. Green,

www

INSTRUCTIONS AND EXCEPTIONS THERETO 

95 

192 Ind. 253, 135 N. E. 657. It is not error for the court to give an instruction not signed by a party or his attorney, if it is correct. Terry v. Davenport, 170 Ind. 

74, 83 N. E. 636. If requested instructions do not have a memorandum at the close made and signed by the Judge showing those to be given and those refused, they will not be part of the record, Morgan Construction Co. v. Dulin, 184 Ind. 652, 109 N. E. 960, and this must be made and signed before the jury is instructed and if the Judge notes on them those that will be given and those that will be refused, the presumption is this was done before the jury was instructed. Morgan Construction 

Co. v. Dulin, 184 Ind. 652, 109 N. E. 960. It must be shown by the record that instructions were filed at the close of the instruction to the jury. Indiana Quarries Co. v. Lavender, 64 App. 415, 114 N. E. 417. Exceptions to instructions under this section may be taken at any time during the term at which the trial is had, and can not be taken after the close of that term. Neff v. Masters, 173 Ind. 196, 89 N. E. 846, and an exception is not taken until it is signed and dated. Neff v. Masters, 173 Ind. 

196, 89 N. E. 846. The following are not instructions; a direction not to consider certain evidence. Madden v. 

State, 148 Ind. 183, 47 N. E. 220; withdrawing an erroneous instruction. Robinson v. State, 182 Ind. 329, 106 

N. E. 533; direction as to the form of a verdict. Bradway v. Waddell, 95 Ind. 170; directions as to answering interrogatories. Lehman v. Hawks, 121 Ind. 541, 23 N. 

E. 670.

Fourth. By a bill of exceptions. 

Instructions may be put into the record by a bill of exceptions. Burns 1933, 2-3223; Burns 1926, 717; 

Baldwin, 497; Citizens Street Railroad Co. v. Hobbs, 15 App. 610, 43 N. E. 479. And must be in criminal cases, Gillespie v. State, 194 Ind. 154, 142 N. E. 220. 

The following form is proper for plaintiff's bill:

PLAINTIFF'S  ON INSTRUCTIONS Caption. No. 

PLAINTIFF'S BILL. 

Be it remembered, in the above entitled cause, that before the argument of the same to the jury began the plaintiff presented to the court the following request and instructions: (Here Insert) and of said instructions the court gave to the jury each of those numbered 1, 4, and 5, and refused to give each of those numbered 2, 3, and 6, and to the refusal to give each of those numbered 2, 3, and 6 the plaintiff at the time excepted. 

And be it further remembered, that before the argument to the jury began the defendant presented to the court the following request and instructions: (Here Insert) And of said instructions, the court gave to the jury each of those numbered 6, 8, and 9, to the giving of each of said instructions numbered 6, 8, and 9, the plaintiff did at the time except. 

And be it further remembered that the court of its own motion gave to the jury instructions numbered 1, 2, and 3, and which instructions are as follows, to-wit: (Here Insert) And to the giving of each of said instructions the plaintiff did at the time except; and

Be it further remembered that the instructions so designated as given were all the instructions given in said cause. 

And said plaintiff now on this the 10th day of May 1931, tenders this, his bill of exceptions containing the instructions given and refused in said cause and asks that the same be signed, sealed, filed and made a part of the record, which is done this the 12th day of June, 1931. 

A. B. Judge of the Shelby Circuit Court.

The same form may be used for the defendant's bill if the word "defendant" is substituted for the word "plaintiff." 1

FROM WHAT AN APPEAL CAN BE TAKEN 

97 

FROM WHAT AN APPEAL CAN BE TAKEN. 

There can be no appeal unless the statute gives it. 

Brown v. Brown, 168 Ind. 654, 80 N. E. 535. An appeal can be taken from certain final judgments and also from certain interlocutory orders, to either the Supreme or 

Appellate Court.

A final judgment is one that disposes of all the issues in the case as to all the parties and leaves nothing further to be done by the court in which the cause is pending. Wehmeier v. Mercantile and etc., 49 App. 454, 97 

N. E. 558; Champ v. Kendrick, 130 Ind. 545. The courts from which an appeal may be taken from such judgments are the Circuit, Superior, Burns 1933, 2—3201; 

Burns 1926, 695; Baldwin, 471; Juvenile, Burns 1933, 9-2818; Burns 1926, 1709; Baldwin, 1772; Municipal, Burns 1933, 4-2510; Burns 1926, 1733; Baldwin, 1725; 

Probate, Burns 1933, 4-2920; Burns 1926, 1772; 

Baldwin, 1797; and Criminal, Burns 1933, 9-2301; 

Burns 1926, 2377; Baldwin, 2366; but an appeal cannot be taken in any case where nothing but money is involved unless the amount in controversy exclusive of interest and costs exceeds fifty dollars. If however there is involved in the same a question duly presented, of the validity of a franchise, or of an ordinance of a municipal corporation, or the constitutionality of a statute, state or federal, or the proper construction of a statute, or rights guaranteed by the state or Federal 

Constitution, there may be an appeal. Burns 1933, 2-3201; Burns 1926, 695; Baldwin, 471; Burns 1933, 4-211; Burns 1926, 1353; Baldwin, 1353; Burns 1933, 4-213; Burns 1926, 1355; Baldwin, 1355. If the complaint shows that the amount in controversy does not exceed fifty dollars there can be no appeal though the prayer of the complaint asks for more than that sum. 

Breidert v. Krueger, 76 Ind. 55. If a case originates before a justice of the peace or mayor of a city and there is no set-off or counterclaim and the plaintiff in the court

FROM WHAT AN APPEAL CAN BE TAKEN 

where the cause was commenced recovers not more than fifty dollars and on appeal to the Circuit Court, he recovers not more than that sum, the defendant cannot appeal to the Supreme or Appellate Court, because the sum now in controversy is the last judgment. Cincinnati, etc. v. McDade, 111 Ind. 23, 12 N. E. 135; Painter v. Guirl, 71 Ind. 240. The amount in controversy in the Supreme, or Appellate Court settles the jurisdiction; Little v. Danville, etc., 18 Ind. 86. When no affirmative judgment is rendered in favor of the defendant upon a counterclaim or set-off and the result of the lawsuit is simply to defeat the plaintiff's claim, the amount in controversy as to the plaintiff's right of appeal is determined by his complaint; but if there is a counterclaim or set-off by the defendant and the plaintiff recovers judgment from which the defendant appeals, then the amount in controversy as to his right to appeal is to be ascertained by aggregating the amount of plaintiff's recovery and the amount involved in the counterclaim or set-off. Pickett v. Hollingsworth, 6 App. 436, 33 N. E. 911.

In a special proceeding there can be no appeal unless the act authorizing it grants the right of appeal. State v. Smith, 190 Ind. 690, 127 N. E. 545.

If the main thing involved in the case is something other than money there may be an appeal though the money recovered did not exceed fifty dollars. Knowlton v. Smith, 163 Ind. 294, 71 N. E. 895.

One in whose favor a judgment has been rendered may appeal therefrom, but he can not do this after receiving money thereon. Burns 1933, 2-3201; Burns 1926, 695; 

Baldwin, 471. Thomson v. Midland, etc., 37 App. 459, 77 N. E. 299; unless he is entitled to it under all circumstances. City of Indpls v. Stutz Motor Car Co., 94 App. 211.

An interlocutory order is one made in the progress of the case and leaves the main question involved for future determination. Rooker v. Fidelity Trust Co., 198 

Ind. 207, 151 N. E. 610. The statute, Burns 1933, 2--3218;

APPEALS BONDS, ETC.-TERM TIME 

99 

Burns 1926, 712; Baldwin, 490 gives the right of appeal from the following interlocutory orders: 

First. For the payment of money, or to compel the execution of any instrument of writing, or the delivery or assignment of any securities, evidences of debt, documents or things in action. 

Second. For the delivery of the possession of real property or the sale thereof. 

Third. Granting or refusing to grant, or dissolving or overruling a motion to dissolve, temporary injunctions either in term or in vacation. 

Fourth. Orders and judgments upon writs of habeas corpus made in term or in vacation.

Fifth. An appeal may be taken on an order of court appointing or refusing to appoint a receiver. Burns 

1933, 3—2603; Burns 1926, 1302; Baldwin, 1157.

APPEALS-BONDS, ETC., CIVIL ACTIONS. 

Term Time. There are two kinds of appeals-term time and vacation.

When an appeal is taken during the term at which the judgment in a civil action is rendered, if it is rendered after a motion for a new trial is overruled, or at the term during which the motion for a new trial is overruled, if it is overruled after judgment is rendered, "It shall operate as a stay of all further proceedings on the judgment, upon an appeal bond being filed by the appellant with such penalty and surety as the Court shall approve and within such time as it shall direct, with condition that

APPEALS BONDS, ETC.-TERM TIME 

he will duly prosecute his appeal and abide by and pay the judgment and costs which may be rendered or affirmed against him; and if the appeal is taken from a judgment for the recovery of real property, or the possession thereof, by the party against whom the judgment for the recovery is rendered, then the condition of the bond shall further provide that the appellant shall also pay all damages which may be sustained by the appellee for the mesne profits, waste or damage to the land during the pendency of the appeal; and if from a judgment for the recovery or return of personal property, or for such property or its value, then that if he deliver or return the property, he will also pay the reasonable value of its use and any damage it may sustain during the pendency of the appeal." Burns 1933, 2-3204; Burns 1926, 698; Baldwin, 480; Blaemire v. 

Barnes, 173 Ind. 657, 91 N. E. 232.

The transcript must be filed in the office of the Clerk of the Supreme Court within sixty days after the bond is filed unless the trial Court extends the time for filing it. If it is so extended, it must be filed in the office of the Clerk of such Court, within such extended time, provided the petition for an extension of time is filed in the office of the Clerk of the trial Court within the time allowed by law for filing the transcript in the office of the Clerk of the Supreme Court, Burns 1933, 2-3204, Burns 1926, 698, Baldwin, 480. This is within 180 days from the rendition of the judgment or overruling the motion for a new trial as above stated. Such is a term time appeal, and no notice is required.

The only effect of filing the appeal bond is to stop execution and all proceedings on the judgment, Nill v. 

Comparett, 16 Ind. 107. As to the effect of appealing and giving bond in a disbarment case, see Walls v. 

Palmer, 64 Ind. 493; in a partition case, see Randles v. 

Randles, 67 Ind. 434; in a habeas corpus case, see Willis v. Willis, 165 Ind. 332, 75 N. E. 655; in a remonstrance case, see Padgett v. State, 93 Ind. 396; in an attachment case, see Waring v. Fletcher, 152 Ind. 620, 52 N. E.

APPEALS BONDS, ETC.-VACATION 

101 

203; in an injunction case, see Tel. Co. v. Board, 110 Ind. 

203, 10 N. E. 922. In this last case it is said, "It is contended by the appellants that the appeal dissolved the injunction and that they were not therefore guilty of a contempt in doing the prohibited acts. This contention cannot prevail for the appeal did not nullify or impair the decree awarding the injunction. ** * The effect of a supersedeas is to restrain the appellee from taking affirmative action to enforce his decree, but it does not authorize the appellant to do what the decree prohibits him from doing.

Indeed the only effect of an appeal to a Court of error, when perfected, is to stay execution on the judgment from which it is taken. In all other respects the judgment, until annulled, or reversed stands binding on all the parties, as to every question decided." * * ** Vacation Appeal. After the close of the term at which the judgment is rendered, if it is rendered after a motion for a new trial is filed, or after the motion for a new trial is overruled, if it is overruled after judgment is rendered, an appeal may be taken by service of notice in writing on the adverse party, or his attorney, and also on the Clerk of the Court in which the proceedings were had, stating the appeal from the judgment, or some specific part thereof; or by procuring from such clerk a transcript of the record and proceedings in the suit, or so much thereof as is embraced in the appeal and filing the same in the office of the Clerk of the Supreme Court who shall endorse on the same the time of filing and issue a notice of the appeal to the appellee, Burns 1933, 2-3206, Baldwin, 482. Such is a vacation appeal.

In a vacation appeal the only way an execution may be stayed is by obtaining from the Court to which the appeal is taken a supersedeas. The application must be in writing, entitle the case, be signed by the appellant or his attorney, addressed to the Court to which the appeal is taken, state the questions involved, made after the transcript and assignment of errors has been filed in the

APPEALS BONDS, ETC.-VACATION 

office of the Clerk of the Supreme Court, be sworn to, and be accompanied by a brief, making a prima facie showing that the transcript presents one or more questions for decision, with reference to the pages and lines of the transcript, pointing out the errors relied on and facts showing the court has jurisdiction. Rules 12, 13, 14, 33. Notice of the petition accompanied by a copy of the motion shall state the time fixed for hearing it, and together with proof of service shall be attached to the petition when it is presented for filing and ten days notice must be given Rules 7, 15, Burns 1933, 2-3208, Burns 1926, 712, Baldwin, 483. Henderson v. Holliday, 10 Ind. 24. The brief above referred to will not be received as a brief on final hearing, Rule 33, and a brief on the merits must be filed within sixty days, or the cause will be dismissed, Carriger v. Kennedy, 134 Ind. 

107, 33 N. E. 909. 

If a term time, or vacation appeal is perfected and in the latter a supersedeas is granted, the bond does not prevent the appellee from suing on the judgment, or prosecuting collateral, and independent proceedings, Line v. State, 131 Ind. 468, 30 N. E. 703. If a bond is given in a term time appeal, or a supersedeas bond is given in a vacation appeal and in either case the appeal is not perfected, or is dismissed, the surety on the bond is not required to pay the judgment rendered in the trial court, McKinney v. Hartman, 143 Ind. 224, 42 N. E. 681; 

Line v. State, 131 Ind. 468, 30 N. E. 703, but there may be a liability on other conditions of the bond. 

The supersedeas may be granted by the court or any Judge thereof and at the time of making the order shall direct that the appellant shall give bond to the appellee with conditions as required when the appeal is taken during the term, Burns 1933, 2-3208; Burns 1926, 702; 

Baldwin, 483. The appeal bond in a term time appeal must be approved by the Court but a supersedeas bond and the surety may be approved by the Clerk of the Supreme Court or by the Clerk of the Court below, and upon filing the bond, execution and all other proceedings

APPEALS-BONDS, ETC.-DECEDENT'S ESTATE 103 

on the judgment in the Court below shall be stayed and if an execution has been issued the Clerk shall notify the sheriff of the appeal and order to stay proceedings and the execution shall be forthwith returned and the levy made shall be relinquished. Burns 1933, 2—3209; Burns 

1926, 703; Baldwin, 484.

It is not necessary that all the judgment defendants should sign the appeal bond, Rockey v. Herschman, 193 

Ind. 168, 138 N. E. 339. An appeal bond signed by a surety alone without a principal is sufficient and proceedings may be stayed on the execution of a defective bond, if it is accepted and approved, Ward v. Buell, 18 Ind. 

104. A supersedeas bond must contain the same conditions as are required in a term time appeal bond, Burns 

1933, 2-3208; Burns 1926, 702; Baldwin, 483. Defects in appeal bonds are cured by statute, Railsback v. Greve, 58 Ind. 72. United States & Co. v. Poetker, 180 Ind. 255, 102 N. E. 372. As the bond does no more than stop execution, any other action on the judgment or decree, except to sue on the same, is prevented by the party so acting being in contempt of Court, Burns 1933, 2—1512; 

Burns 1926, 471; Baldwin, 222. Telegraph Co. v. Board, 110 Ind. 203, 10 N. E. 922.

In Decedent's Estate. An executor, administrator or guardian may have an appeal and stay of execution without giving an appeal bond, Burns 1933, 2-3217; Burns 

1926, 711; Baldwin, 489; Burns 1933, 6-2003; Burns 

1926, 3312; Baldwin, 3280; but if it is a term-time appeal, the appeal must be prayed and a stay of proceedings secured in the court below, or it will be a vacation appeal, Stultz v. Gibler, 146 Ind. 501, 45 N. E. 340; Holderman v. Wood, 34 App. 519, 73 N. E. 199. In Koons v. Mellett, 121 Ind. 585, 23 N. E. 95, this is said on page 590: "The rule to be deduced from the decisions upon the subject is that in all proceedings under the law providing for the settlement of a decedent's estate, where the exercise of the probate jurisdiction of the court is invoked, the appeal is governed by sections 2454 and 2455 R. S. 1881; but in all

APPEALS BONDS, ETC.-DECEDENT'S ESTATE 

actions authorized by the code and not involving the exercise of the probate jurisdiction of the court the appeal is governed by the code, and these sections have no application.” 

"Where 

In Roach v. Clark, 150 Ind. this is said on p. 96 :the remedy sought by or against an estate is not provided by the probate procedure act, but must be enforced under the civil code, the appeal is not under sections 2609, 2610, Burns R. S. 1894 but is under the civil code." 

FORM FOR PETITION AND BRIEF FOR A SUPERSEDEAS. 

In the Supreme (or Appellate) Court 

STATE OF INDIANA. 

MAY TERM, 1933. 

A. B. Appellant V. 

C. D. Appellee.

In the above entitled cause the appellant petitions the Court to grant a supersedeas in the same and in support of such petition he states the following facts: 

1. Judgment was rendered in said cause against the appellant on the (here state the date and line, and page of the transcript where the judgment will be found, or when the motion for a new trial was overruled, if it was overruled after the judgment was rendered.) 

2. The transcript with an assignment of errors was filed in the office of the Clerk of this Court, (here state the date of the filing of the same.) 

3. It is assigned as error that the Court erred in overruling the appellant's demurrer to the complaint, and also in overruling the appellant's motion for a new trial, to which ruling of the 993

APPEALS BONDS, ETC.-DECEDENT'S ESTATE 105 

Court the appellant in each case excepted. (Here state the line and page of the transcript where each of said facts is found.) 

4. The suit was brought against the appellant by the appellee for malicious prosecution and the jury returned a verdict against the appellant in the sum of $9,000.00 on which judgment was rendered, (here state the line and page of the transcript where such facts are found.) 

5. This is a vacation appeal.

Wherefore on the facts herein stated the appellant prays the Court to issue a supersedeas in said cause and fix the amount of the bond and make all further orders concerning the same which are proper and necessary. 

Respectfully submitted, A. B. 

Attorney for Appellant. day of 

Subscribed and sworn to before me this 

Notary Public. 

The following are not appeals under the decedents' act but are under the Code: Suits to construe a will, Simmons v. Beagel, 125 Ind. 362, 25 N. E. 344; to contest a will, Gohen v. Stirlen, 193 Ind. 246, 139 N. E. 359; to establish and to admit to probate a will, Morell v. Morell, 157 Ind. 

179, 60 N. E. 1092; a proceeding by a creditor to have a legacy applied on his debt, Koons v. Mellett, 121 Ind. 

585, 23 N. E. 95; a civil action to collect damages for death, Campbell v. Payne, 80 App. 606; actions by executors or administrators to collect debts due the estate, Mark v. North, 155 Ind. 575, 57 N. E. 902; an action by a widow to have her rights established in property conveyed by her husband, Crawfordsville, etc. v. 

Ramsey, 55 App. 40, 100 N. E. 1049; an appeal by an administrator from an order removing him, Moore v. 

Bankers Surety Co., 34 App. 633, 73 N. E. 607; an action commenced by one who died and his administrator is substituted, Heller v. Clark, 103 Ind. 59, 3 N. E. 844; an action joining one with a decedent's estate with whom

APPEALS BONDS, ETC.-DECEDENT'S ESTATE 

he was not jointly bound, Claypool v. Gish, 108 Ind. 424, 9 N. E. 382; an action where an administrator is made a party in a proceeding supplementary to execution to answer as to money in his hands belonging to the debtor. 

Dillman v. Dillman, 90 Ind. 585. 

Burns 1926, Sec. 3311 is: "Any person considering himself aggrieved by any decision of a circuit court, or judge thereof in vacation growing out of any matter connected with a decedent's estate may appeal to the Supreme Court upon filing with the Clerk of such Circuit Court a bond with penalty in double the sum in controversy in cases where an amount of money is involved, or where there is none, in a reasonable sum to be designated by such clerk with sufficient surety, payable to the opposite party in such appeal conditioned for the diligent prosecution of such appeal, for the payment of the judgment which may be affirmed and all costs, if costs be adjudged against the appellant."

Appeals may now be taken to the Appellate Court. 

Burns 1933, 4-214; Burns 1926, 1356; Baldwin, 1356.

The following cases illustrate the meaning of the statute and are governed by it. Appeals from judgments concerning claims against estates or for the sale of property to pay claims, Vail v. Page, 175 Ind. 126, 93 N. E. 

705; from a judgment in a proceeding to set aside a final settlement, Webb v. Simpson, 105 Ind. 327, 4 N. E. 900; from a judgment on exceptions to a final report, Bake v. 

Smiley, 84 Ind. 212; from a judgment against heirs for debt of decedent, Jones v. Henderson, 149 Ind. 458, 49

N. E. 443; from a judgment in an action to set aside a fraudulent conveyance by decedent, Galentine v. Wood, Admr., 137 Ind. 532, 35 N. E. 901, and where a widow petitions for her allowance, Bender v. Wampler, 84 

Ind. 172. "Such appeal bond must be filed within thirty days after the decision complained of is made unless for good cause shown, the Court to which the appeal is prayed shall direct

APPEALS-BONDS, ETC.-DECEDENT'S ESTATE 107 

such appeal to be granted on the filing of such bond within one hundred and eighty days after such decision. But any person who is aggrieved, desiring such appeal, may take the same in his own name without joining any other person. 

The transcript shall be filed in the Supreme Court within ninety days after filing the appeal bond." Burns 1933, 6-2003; Burns 1926, 3312; Baldwin, 3280.

The bond must be filed within thirty days, unless otherwise ordered by the court to which the appeal is taken, Mid West, etc. v. Scott, 80 App. 452, 141 N. E. 228, and the application for such extension must be made within the 30 days. In re Whisler, 56 App. 269, 105 N. E. 158.

The transcript must be filed within the time fixed by statute, or the appeal will be dismissed, Nash, Admr. v. Burgess Ex., 79 App. 460, 135 N. E. 800. After the submission of a cause, joinder in error and filing briefs on the merits of the case, it is too late to move for dismissal because no bond has been filed, Jones v. Henderson, 149 

Ind. 548, 49 N. E. 403. The time for filing the bond begins to run from the date of the rendition of the judgment, if it is rendered after the motion for a new trial is overruled, and from the overruling of the motion for a new trial, if it is made after judgment, and the time for filing the transcript is within 120 days from the rendition of the judgment in the one case and from overruling the motion in the other, Leslie v. Ebner, Admr., 67 App. 32, 117 N. E. 511. The same law applies to an executor or administrator though he is not required to give bond. 

Simons v. Simons' Trustee, 129 Ind. 248, 28 N. E. 702.

The above statute is mandatory and must be strictly complied with, Rinehart v. Vail, 103 Ind. 159, 2 N. E. 330, and where it does not specifically provide what shall be done in the matter of procedure the procedure provided in the civil code must be observed. Scharer v. Ingerman, Admr., 110 Ind. 428, 11 N. E. 8; Leslie v. Ebner, Admr., 67 App. 32, 117 N. E. 511.

If an order is desired for an extension of time a formal and proper application must be made by the appellant LL

APPEALS-BONDS, ETC.-INTERLOCUTORY ORDER 

and in it he must show there is merit in the appeal, what he has done and why he has been prevented from appealing within the time provided by statute, must be sworn to and 10 days' notice to the appellee must be given of the time when the application will be presented to the Court. 

Browning v. McCracken, 97 Ind. 279-82; Duncan v. 

Gainey, 108 Ind. 579, 9 N. E. 470 (Rules 7-12).

Interlocutory Order. An appeal may be taken from an interlocutory order of any Circuit, Superior or Probate Court, or Judge thereof, to the Supreme Court in any of the cases prescribed in Burns 1933, 2-3218; Burns 1926, 712; Baldwin, 490; Burns 1933, 4—214; Burns 1926, 1356;

Baldwin, 1356. Such appeal may be taken only within 

30 days from the date of the order appealed from and to perfect such appeal, the appellant shall file an appeal bond as in the case of term appeals from final judgments and shall file his transcript and assignment of errors all within said period of thirty days, Burns 1933, 2—3219; 

Burns 1926, 713; Baldwin, 491, and such appeal shall not stay proceedings upon the order appealed from more than thirty days unless the Supreme Court or some Judge thereof shall order otherwise. Burns 1933, 2—3220; 

Burns 1926, 714; Baldwin, 492.

Jurisdiction of appeals from interlocutory orders is exclusively in the Supreme Court. Nation v. Green, 65 

App. 136, 116 N. E. 840. If several interlocutory orders are made in the same cause they may all be presented in one appeal. Gaynor v. French Lick Co., 163 Ind. 687, 72 N. E. 849. There can be no appeal from an interlocutory order unless it is expressly authorized by statute and such statute is strictly construed. Nisius v. Chapman, 178 Ind. 494, 99 N. E. 785. The provisions of the code apply to all proceedings, whether under special statutes or not, unless excluded by the provisions of the act. 

Robertson v. State, ex rel., 109 Ind. 79-87, 10 N. E. 582. 

Restraining Order. There is no appeal from a temporary restraining order. The difference between such an order and a temporary injunction is that the former issues without notice on a sworn application showing

APPEALS BONDS, ETC.-RECEIVERS 

109 an emergency while the latter issues only after notice and hearing, Mason v. Milligan, 185 Ind. 319, 114 N.

E. 3. Receivers. "In all cases in which a receiver may be appointed or refused, the party aggrieved may, within ten days thereafter, appeal from the decision of the court to the Supreme Court, without awaiting the final determination of such case; and in case a receiver has been or shall be appointed, upon the appellant filing an appeal bond with sufficient surety in such sum as may have been required of such receiver, conditioned for the due prosecution of such appeal and the payment of all costs or damages that may accrue to any officer or person by reason thereof, the authority of such receiver shall be suspended until the final determination of such appeal." Burns 1933, 3-2603; Burns 1926, 1302; Baldwin, 1157.

In such appeals the statute must be strictly followed. 

Daugherty v. Payne, 175 Ind. 603, 95 N. E. 233. "Receivers shall not be appointed, either in term or vacation in any case, until the adverse party shall have appeared, or shall have had reasonable notice of the application for such appointment, except upon sufficient cause shown by affidavit." Burns 1933, 3-2602; Burns 1926, 1301; Baldwin, 1156.

Excepting the appointment of a receiver for a corporation because of insolvency, or danger of insolvency, the appointment of a receiver is ancillary to an action and a part of it, and a receiver can not be appointed until a complaint is filed and summons delivered to the sheriff for service, and the cause for appointing a receiver must be shown by the allegation of facts, not opinions, sworn to which show there is an emergency. Tucker v. Tucker, 194 Ind. 108, 142 N. E. 11; General Motors Oil Co. v. 

Matheny, 185 Ind. 114, 113 N. E. 4. If a temporary restraining order is sufficient to protect all interests a receiver should not be appointed without notice. Tucker

APPEALS--BONDS, ETC.-CRIMINAL ACTIONS 

v. Tucker, 194 Ind. 108, 142 N. E. 11. An exception must be taken at the time of the ruling of the Court unless the proceedings up to that time are ex parte and if so the exception may be taken at the earliest opportunity. 

Wabash Co. v. Dykeman, 133 Ind. 56, 32 N. E. 823; Ryder v. Shea, 183 Ind. 15, 108 N. E. 104. The appeal must be taken from the order appointing or refusing to appoint and it is not necessary to move to set aside the appointment, Wabash Co. v. Dykeman, supra, and a motion for a new trial is not necessary, Shoemaker v. Smith, 74 Ind. 71, and on an appeal from the appointment of a receiver without notice, the merits of the controversy will not be considered.

If an appeal is taken in the main action, the receiver shall not be enjoined from taking possession of the property during the appeal. Chicago, etc. v. Kenney, 29 App. 

506, 68 N. E. 20. The appeal must be taken and perfected within ten days after the date of the order, and if a term time appeal is taken the trial court must fix the amount of the bond and approve the sureties within the term at which the order is made, and if the appeal is taken from an order made in vacation, notice of the appeal must be given within the ten days, Daugherty v. Payne, 175 Ind. 603, 95 N. E. 233. 

Cities, Etc. If a city has a right to appeal, it may do so without bond, Burns 1933, 48—1811, Burns 1926, 10335, Baldwin, 11469; and a civil suit brought in the name of the State of Indiana, or in the name of the State of Indiana on relation of the Attorney General, or on relation of any State Board created by law may be prosecuted without giving any bond for costs or damages. 

Burns 1933, 49-1906; Burns 1926, 11720, Baldwin, 15077, Burns 1933, Burns 1926, 645, Baldwin, 406, and so may a board of commissioners, a county, a civil or school township and the trustee and advisory board of such township. Acts 1933, p. 115.

Criminal Cases. 

"In all criminal cases where no special provision has been made in this act, the rules of pleading and practice in civil

APPEALS BONDS, ETC.-CRIMINAL ACTIONS 

111 actions shall govern so far as applicable." Burns 1933, 9-2407; Burns 1926, 2406; Baldwin, 2395.

If the criminal code furnishes an adequate remedy for the review of decisions in criminal cases, it alone must be looked to for guidance on the subject. Frazier v. The 

State, 106 Ind. 562, 7 N. E. 378; and if there is no provision in the criminal code for raising objections to and testing the sufficiency of pleadings, the rules of the civil code for testing pleadings apply. Boos v. State, 181 Ind. 

562, 105 N. E. 117; Sanders v. State, 85 Ind. 318. If one is entitled to some relief and there is no statutory remedy, neither criminal nor civil, and the common law furnishes a remedy, then that remedy will be looked to and will furnish the relief. Sanders v. The State, 85 Ind. 318. "The prosecuting attorney may except to any decision of the court during the prosecution of any cause, and reserve the point of law for the decision of the Supreme Court. The bill of exceptions must state clearly so much of the record and proceedings as may be necessary for a fair statement of the question reserved. In case of the acquittal of the defendant, the prosecuting attorney may take the reserved case to the Supreme Court upon an appeal at any time within one year." Burns 1933, 9—2102; Burns 1926, 2329; 

Baldwin, 2314.

The State can not appeal unless the defendant is acquitted and can only appeal when the indictment or information is quashed, when the judgment is arrested, or on a question of law reserved, Burns 1933, 9-2304; 

Burns 1926, 2378, Baldwin, 2368; State v. Hunt, 137 Ind. 

537, 37 N. E. 409.

If the defendant is not acquitted, the State can not appeal and the acquittal must be shown by the record. 

State v. Hodgin, 139 Ind. 498, 39 N. E. 161; and the

State can not appeal if one count in an indictment is quashed but another remains. State v. Evansville Co., 107 Ind. 581, 8 N. E. 619; but if there are several counts

APPEALS BONDS, ETC.-CRIMINAL ACTIONS 

1 and one or more are quashed but another, or others remain, the State may nolle those that remain and then appeal. State v. Wabash Co., 21 App. 167, 48 N. E. 653.

A bill of exceptions is not necessary if the record properly presents the facts, and if the Court accepts a plea of guilty to manslaughter under an indictment for murder, over the objection of the prosecuting attorney, the State may appeal. State v. Morrison, 165 Ind. 461, 75 N. E. 968. 

If the defendant is acquitted, the State can not move to arrest the judgment, and appeal from the judgment, but if the defendant moves to arrest the judgment and the motion is sustained the State may appeal. State v. 

Rousch, 60 Ind. 309.

The statute must be strictly complied with; the opinion of the Court must be excepted to; the record contain so much of the evidence as is essential to a proper understanding of the question presented on appeal; and if there is not, or under the law could not be, a motion for a new trial, the evidence though all set out, is no part of the record. State v. Bartlett, 9 Ind. 569. The prosecuting attorney may take all necessary steps to perfect the appeal, State v. Sutherlin, 165 Ind. 339, 75 N. E. 642; but the State can not have a question of fact reviewed, State v. Phillips, 25 App. 579, 58 N. E. 727; but may except to the exclusion of evidence offered by the State and have the decision reviewed on appeal, State v. 

Laughlin, 171 Ind. 66, 84 N. E. 756; may have reviewed on appeal instructions given or refused, State v. Harrison, 162 Ind. 542, 70 N. E. 877; State v. Sharp, 169 Ind. 

128, 81 N. E. 1150; and in the latter case the record must show there was evidence to which the instructions were applicable, State v. Kern, 127 Ind. 465, 26 N. E. 1076.

In misdemeanor cases the State has the right to appeal on any question of law within sixty days after the rendition of the judgment, Burns 1933, 9-2305, Burns 1926, 2379, Baldwin, 2371, and when the State appeals from a question reserved by it, it shall not be necessary for the clerk to certify in the transcript any part of the

APPEALS BONDS, ETC.-CRIMINAL ACTIONS 113 

proceedings and record, except the pleadings, the bill of exceptions and the judgment of acquittal and, if the question is defectively stated, the Court to which the appeal is taken may direct any part of the proceedings and record to be certified to that court, Burns 1933, 9-2306; Burns 1926, 2380; Baldwin, 2369. The record on appeal must contain the charge on which the proceedings are based, Riley v. State, 149 Ind. 48, 48 N. E. 345; but this need not be made a part of it by a bill of exceptions, State v. Vanderbilt, 116 Ind. 11, 18 N. E. 266. It is not the duty of the Court to which the appeal is taken on its own motion to cause the record to be corrected, State v. Hollowell, 91 Ind. 376. This should be done by the complaining party, State v. Weil, 89 Ind. 286. "An appeal by the State may be taken by the service of a written notice upon the Clerk of the Court where the judgment was rendered, stating that the appellant appeals from the judgment, and a similar notice must be served upon the defendant or his attorney. If neither the defendant nor his attorney can be found then the notice to them may be given by posting up such notice three weeks in the clerk's office in a conspicuous place. If the appeal be taken by the defendant, a similar notice must be served on the prosecuting attorney. The parties may waive such written notice, or enter in writing their appearance to such appeal, but any such appeal by either party shall not be deemed perfected until the filing of the record thereof in the office of the clerk of the court to which such appeal is taken." Burns 

1933, 9-2310; Burns 1926, 2384; Baldwin, 2374.

There is no term time appeal in criminal cases and the only way an appeal can be taken in such cases is by giving notice as the statute requires. McLaughlin v. 

State, 66 Ind. 193. Service of notice upon a "deputy prosecuting attorney" is not sufficient. The statute must be strictly complied with and the notice must be served on the prosecuting attorney. Hizer v. State, 173 Ind. 

192, 89 N. E. 844. The appeal is deemed taken when the notice is given, Price v. State, 74 Ind. 553; but it

APPEALS-BONDS, ETC.-CRIMINAL ACTIONS 

is not perfected until the statutory notices are given and the transcript and assignment of errors are filed in the office of the clerk of the Supreme Court. Flatter v. State, 182 Ind. 514, 107 N. E. 9. An appearance, joinder in error and submission of a cause is a waiver of notice, Beck v. State, 72 Ind. 250, and when the defendant appeals it is not necessary to serve notice on the clerk. 

Darr v. State, 82 Ind. 11. "All appeals must be taken within one year after the judgment is rendered, or in case a motion for a new trial is filed, within one year after the ruling on such motion. The transcript must be filed within ninety days after the appeal is taken. Burns 1933, 9-2308; Burns 1926, 2382; Baldwin, 2372. 

An appeal is taken by service of notice of appeal and the time for filing the transcript dates from that time. 

Price v. State, 74 Ind. 553. If the transcript is filed and notice served on the prosecuting attorney within the ninety days the appeal will be perfected. Biggs v. State, 122 Ind. 54, 23 N. E. 693; and an appearance and joining issue waives the right to have the appeal dismissed because not perfected in time. State v. Walters, 64 Ind. 226. 

The defendant may, as a matter of right, appeal from any judgment rendered against him and upon the appeal any decision of the court or intermediate order made in the progress of the case may be reviewed, Burns 1933, 9-2301; Burns 1926, 2377; Baldwin, 2366. An appeal can only be taken from a final judgment and a suspended judgment is a final judgment. Montgomery v. State, 182 Ind. 276, 106 N. E. 370. The insufficiency of the indictment or of the affidavit can not be assigned as error, Robinson v. State, 184 Ind. 208, 110 N. E. 980; but errors must be assigned, May v. State, 140 Ind. 88, 39 N. E. 701. "The bill of exceptions must contain so much of the evidence as is necessary to present the questions of law upon which the exceptions were taken; but it shall not be neces-

APPEALS-BONDS, ETC.-CRIMINAL ACTIONS 115 

sary for the bill to contain all of the evidence given in the cause or proceeding, unless the decision of the court or verdict of the jury shall be called in question as being contrary to law or not sustained by sufficient evidence. In case an original bill of exceptions shall be incorporated into the transcript of the record of any case on appeal, such original bill shall, in every case, constitute a part of such transcript as if copied therein by the clerk whether such original bill or a copy thereof be specified in the precipe, or otherwise directed to be incorporated into such transcript: Provided, That every pleading, motion in writing, report, deposition or other paper, filed or offered to be filed, in any cause or proceeding, whether received by the court, refused or stricken out, shall be a part of the record from the time of such filing or offer to file; and any order or action of the court in respect to any such pleading, motion in writing, report, deposition or other paper, and every exception thereto taken by any party shall be entered by the clerk on the minutes or record of the court, and the same, when so entered shall be a part of the record without any bill of exceptions. Every oral motion and the ruling of the court thereon and the exceptions taken thereto shall be entered upon the minutes or record of the court and shall be a part of the record without any bill of exceptions: Provided, further, That a motion to strike out or insert any matter, and the ruling thereon, shall not be a part of the record without a bill of exceptions, unless the matter to be stricken out or inserted is set out in full in the motion. And Provided further, That nothing herein shall be construed to prevent the bringing into or putting into the record by a proper bill of exceptions any matter, ruling or exception, except the indictment or affidavit, the motion to quash, the pleading in answer to such indictment or affidavit, the verdict, the motion and reasons for a new trial and the final judgment." Burns 1933, 9-2105; Burns 1926, 2332; Baldwin, 2317.

THE TRANSCRIPT-BASIS OF APPEAL THE TRANSCRIPT-THE BASIS OF THE APPEAL. 

Every appeal to either the Appellate or the Supreme Court is determined on the record made in the trial court. 

If that Court had jurisdiction of both the subject matter of the suit and of the parties to the same, no appeal will be considered on its merits unless the record shows that the appealing party excepted to the decision of the court which it is claimed is erroneous and also that a final judgment in the case was rendered. Watson v. Finch, 150 Ind. 183, 48 N. E. 245; Butler v. Thornburg, 141 Ind. 

152, 40 N. E. 514; Gray v. Singer, Adm., 137 Ind. 257, 36 N. E. 209. However, an appeal may be taken from an interlocutory order and such an order is regarded as final for appeal therefrom. Burns 1933, 2-3218; Burns 

1926, 712; Baldwin, 490.

The record for an appeal is obtained by a party to the suit who wishes it, by filing in the office of the clerk of the court where the final judgment was rendered, or the interlocutory order was made, a written precipe in which he shall state the part of the record he wants. If a transcript of the entire record is wanted, it will be sufficient for the precipe so to state. If the entire record is not wanted, the precipe must state the part wanted and if anything else is placed in the transcript, it will not be considered. The precipe will constitute a part of the record and be copied in the transcript immediately before the certificate of the clerk and the clerk shall include in the transcript everything asked for in the precipe, Burns 

1933, 2-3112; Burns 1926, 692; Baldwin, 466.

Prior to the Legislative Act 1903, Burns supra, every part of the record placed in the transcript had to be copied by the clerk and certified in making the transcript. 

By that act the Legislature provided that a Bill of Exceptions containing the evidence could be certified without the clerk copying it. This then was the only part of the record that could be certified without copying. Leach v. Mattix, 149 Ind. 146, 48 N. E. 791,

THE TRANSCRIPT BASIS OF APPEAL 

117 9 The Legislature in 1933, Chapter 185, page 918, amended Section 7, supra, of the Act of 1903, to read as follows: 

"When any matter may be brought into the record by a 

Bill of Exceptions the original Bill of Exceptions, whether containing the evidence or any other matter, may be incorporated into the transcript of the record of such cause on appeal to the Supreme Court or the Appellate Court, and such original Bill of Exceptions shall, in every case, constitute and be considered as a part of such transcript, the same as if copied therein by the Clerk, whether such original Bill or copy thereof be specified in the precipe or otherwise directed to be incorporated into such transcript.” 

By this much of this act it seems that the Legislature intended that anything properly made a part of the record by a Bill of Exceptions might be certified as a part of the transcript without copying.

The proviso in this Legislative act immediately following the portion above quoted is as follows: "Provided, However, that if the original written instructions as given and/or refused by the Court shall be incorporated into any Bill of Exceptions, the Court shall certify in his certificate to such Bill of Exceptions that such original written instructions are incorporated therein, and the Bill containing such original written instructions shall be copied into the transcript by the clerk." 

Did the Legislature by this proviso intend that the original instructions given and refused should remain on file as a part of the record of the trial court and that in making the transcript including these instructions the clerk should copy the Bill, or did it mean that the original instructions given and refused might be copied and placed in a Bill of Exceptions and when so done and the Bill containing this copy was filed and became a part of the record that the clerk should then certify the same without copying? There is a difference of opinion upon the part of those competent to judge as to the meaning of this proviso concerning the Bill of Exceptions contain-

THE TRANSCRIPT-BASIS OF APPEAL 

1 ing the instructions. The Legislature certainly has left the matter in doubt and the doubt will not be removed except by Legislative act or Judicial construction. The safe way is to put the instructions in a separate Bill and order the clerk to copy it. Such a bill of exceptions included in the transcript is a part of the same, though the precipe calls for a "transcript" thereof. New 

American, etc. v. Troyer, 166 Ind. 402, 76 N. E. 253. It has been held that the precipe may be copied in the transcript before the certificate at a place other than immediately preceding it. Cleveland, etc. v. Morrey, 172 

Ind. 513, 88 N. E. 932. If the original bill of exceptions containing the evidence is incorporated in the transcript of the record it shall constitute and be considered as a part of the transcript, the same as if copied therein by the clerk, whether such bill or a copy of it be specified in the precipe. Burns 1933, 2-3112; Burns 1926, 692;

Baldwin, 466; and if the transcript does not contain a precipe it is presumed the clerk was orally directed to make a complete transcript. Scott v. LaFayette Co., 

42 App. 614, 86 N. E. 495. Any party to the record may file a precipe for any part of the record not included in a transcript prepared in obedience to the precipe filed by another party. Burns 1933, 2—3112; Burns 1926, 692; 

Baldwin, 466.

The transcript must be authenticated by the certificate of the clerk, attested by the seal of the court. The form of this is prescribed by the legislature and should be accurately followed. Burns 1933, 2—3112; Burns 1926, 692; Baldwin, 466. If the seal of the court is omitted the transcript will be rejected and the cause dismissed. 

Hinton v. Brown, 1 Blf. 429, and so will it be if the certificate is not substantially as the form prescribed. Reed v. 

Houston, 49 Ind. 181. Burns 1933, 2—3112; Burns 1926, 717; Baldwin, 497; provides that in making a transcript a summons where all the parties named in it have appeared in the action and subpoenas for witnesses or when a pleading first filed, when there is an amended pleading of the same matter subsequently filed, etc., shall not be certified but the amended pleading shall be certified. 1
