And so on to and including the last entry in the cause, stating every entry in the order book made in the case and including every paper in the case referred to by a (here insert) called for by the precipe. The precipe must be made a part of the transcript and precede the clerk's certificate which must be in the following form, and the entries should be in chronological order. 

STATE OF INDIANA COUNTY OF 

SS. 

Clerk of the Circuit Court within and for said county and state, do hereby certify that the above and foregoing transcript contains full, true and correct copies, or the originals of all papers and entries in said cause required by the above and foregoing precipe (or precipes). 

Witness my hand and the seal of said court at Brookville, Indiana, this the 10th day of December, 1933. Clerk.

Wherever in the above form there is "here insert" the paper referred to must be copied by the clerk in full and inserted in the place designated unless the paper referred to is a Bill of Exceptions containing the evidence. Such a bill containing the evidence may by virtue

of the statute be certified without copying and so may any other bill which the statute says may be certified without copying.

RULES OF COURT. 

The statute gives authority to the Supreme Court "to establish modes of practice which may be necessary in the exercise of its authority and to make regulations respecting the same and cause them to be printed." Burns 1933, 4-109; Burns 1926, 1335; Baldwin, 1317. "The Supreme

Court derives its authority to establish rules from the constitution and not from the legislature and the legislature has no power to establish rules for the Supreme Court nor to say what constitutes sufficient briefs. This court has power to make its own rules as to briefs and as to the conduct of business before the court. It is not a legislative function to make rules for the court. The rule was not made for the benefit of the appealing parties exclusively, but also to aid the court in expediting the business before it. * * ** The court had the right to make the rule under consideration and when made, it had the force and effect of law binding upon the court as well as appealing parties.

The court, therefore, has the power as well as the imposed duty, to enforce it of its own motion in all proper cases and this power can not be taken away by legislative action." Epstein v. State, 190 Ind. 

693, 127 N. E. 421.

APPEALS. 

Rule 1. If an appeal is taken in term time, but the transcript is not filed in the office of the clerk within the time limited, the appeal as of term shall be deemed to be abandoned; and the appeal shall be treated as a vacation appeal taken as of the time when the transcript is filed, and shall be governed as to notice and like matters by the rules and practice governing appeals not taken in term. 

Rule 2. If notice of an appeal not taken in term shall be given below, the transcript must be filed in the clerk's office within sixty days from the time of giving such notice, and before the expiration of the time allowed for taking the appeal, or the notice shall be without effect. But if the time for taking an appeal has not expired a notice may be issued and served as in other cases of vacation appeals.

PREPARATION OF TRANSCRIPTS. 

Rule 3. The appellant shall cause the transcript to be neatly and securely bound and to be paged at the bottom and the lines. of each page to be numbered. He shall also cause marginal notes on each page to be placed on the transcript in their appropriate places, indicating the several parts of the pleadings in the cause, the exhibits, if any, the rulings of the court, and the bills of exceptions. Where the evidence is set out by deposition or otherwise, the name of each witness, and whether the examination is direct, cross or re-direct, shall be stated on the margin of each page. The appellant shall also note on the margin all motions and rulings thereon, the instructions given and refused, and shall prepare an index referring to the initial page of the direct, cross and re-examination of each witness, and of each pleading, exhibit and other paper in the record, such index to form the first page of the transcript.

ASSIGNMENT OF ERRORS AND CROSS-ERRORS. 

Rule 4. The appellant shall, on the transcript, or on some paper attached thereto, make a specific assignment of the errors upon which he relies, and he shall therein properly entitle the cause. Each specification of error shall be complete in itself, and shall be properly numbered. Amendments of the assignment of errors shall not be made after the cause is submitted, except upon notice and leave applied for in writing, nor shall leave be granted unless it appear that due care and diligence were exercised in the first instance to make the assignment complete.

Rule 5. The appellee may, as of right and without notice, assign cross-errors within sixty days after the submission of the cause, but if cross-errors are not assigned within that time, then notice shall be given the appellant and leave applied for in writing. The application for leave to assign cross-errors shall show a satisfactory excuse for the failure to assign the crosserrors within sixty days from the time of the submission of the cause, or the application will be denied. No assignment of cross-errors seeking a reversal of the judgment shall be made after the time allowed for taking an appeal.

PARTIES-PROCESS. 

Rule 6. The assignment of errors shall contain the full names of all parties to the judgment, and process when necessary shall issue accordingly. NOTICE.

Rule 7. Where notice is required under these rules, and it is not otherwise provided by them, or not otherwise provided by law, ten days' notice shall be deemed to be intended and required, unless otherwise ordered by the court.

APPEARANCES. 

Rule 8. A joinder in error, or the filing of a pleading or a brief upon the merits by a party in person or by his attorney, or an agreement in writing to submit a cause, or an unqualified 1

written appearance, filed in the clerk's office, shall be deemed a general appearance which shall not be withdrawn without leave of court.

Rule 9. A special appearance shall be so designated, shall be in writing, and shall state the particular relief demanded together with the facts in support thereof. 

MOTIONS. 

Rule 10. When a motion or petition is founded on a matter of fact which is not admitted or apparent on the record, it must be supported by affidavit. 

Rule 11. Each motion shall state specifically the grounds upon which it is based. If based on the record the motion shall refer specifically to the line and page of the record. 

Rule 12. All motions and petitions addressed to the court shall be in writing, filed with the clerk, and shall state facts showing that the court to which they are addressed has jurisdiction, and shall be accompanied by such affidavits and briefs as are necessary to support them.

Rule 13. Notice to the party affected thereby shall be given of all motions and petitions, except petitions for a writ of certiorari made prior to the submission of the cause, and petitions for first extensions of time. The notice shall be accompanied by a copy of the motion or petition filed and a copy of the mover or petitioner's brief thereon, and proof of the service of such notice and copy and brief shall be filed in the clerk's office within ten days thereafter. The party upon whom notice has been served shall have ten days, unless otherwise ordered by the court, from the date of service thereof within which he may file such papers and briefs as he may desire. Thereafter at such time as may be convenient, the motion or petition will be decided by the court on the papers and briefs filed. 

SUBMISSION OF CIVIL CAUSES. 

Rule 14. Every appeal in a civil case shall be submitted at the expiration of 30 days from the date of filing the transcript and assignment of errors, except when it is necessary to issue and serve notice after such filing in order to perfect the appeal,

in which case the cause shall be submitted at the expiration of 30 days from the date when such notice is served. Provided, that upon agreement of parties, a cause may be submitted immediately, and for cause the court may order a cause to be submitted at such time as it may direct. Appeals from the 

Industrial Board of Indiana shall be submitted immediately on the date of filing.

Rule 15. A cause may be submitted by a written agreement filed with the clerk of the Supreme and Appellate Courts. 

Rule 16. Where the appellee objects to the submission of a cause under Rule 14, and files written objections, accompanied by a verified statement that the objections are made in good faith, the clerk shall not enter a submission, but shall refer the matter to the court, and in such a case submission shall not be entered except upon the order of the court. Upon entering an order of submission the clerk shall mail notice of such submission to one, at least, of the attorneys whose names are appended to the assignment of errors, and shall also mail notice of the entry of submission to the clerk of the court from which the appeal came.

SUBMISSION OF CRIMINAL CAUSES. 

Rule 17. Criminal causes shall be submitted immediately upon filing the transcript and notice.

SUBMISSION OF HABEAS CORPUS CASES. 

Rule 18. On appeal in a case of habeas corpus, either party may submit, on motion, at any time after the appeal is perfected, provided he has given the opposite party, or his attorney, and any other person or persons having an adverse interest in the cause, three days' previous written notice of the intended motion to submit. The notice, with proof of service as provided by law, stating the time and manner of service, shall be filed with the clerk of this court, before the motion to submit shall be made. 

Such a cause may also be submitted by agreement, as other causes.

BRIEFS. 

Rule 19. The appellant shall have sixty days after submission in which to file his brief, and if the brief is not filed within

the time limited the clerk shall enter an order dismissing the appeal, unless a petition for extension of time is on file. The appellee shall file his brief upon the assignment of errors within ninety days after submission, except that in criminal cases such brief shall be filed within one hundred twenty days after submission. In cases where appellant has been granted an extension of time appellee shall have thirty days after the expiration of the time granted to appellant in which to file briefs. 

If cross-errors are assigned, the appellee shall file his brief thereon within sixty days from the date of the cross-assignment or the same shall be stricken out. The appellant shall file his reply brief within fifteen days from the expiration of the time allotted appellee. If the appellee's brief on the assignment of errors or the appellant's reply brief are not filed within the time limited the clerk shall refuse to receive and file the same except upon leave of court first obtained for sufficient cause shown by the party in default. No supplemental or additional briefs by either party will be received. Further citations of authorities in support of any proposition stated in the briefs may be filed by either party at any time, without comment or argument. 

Rule 20. Petitions for extensions of time to file briefs will not hereafter be granted, unless facts are stated therein showing that the court in which the cause is pending has jurisdiction thereof and that the brief will be on the merits of the cause. 

When filed by the appellee such petition shall also show that all motions to dismiss and all dilatory motions in the cause on behalf of the petitioner have been filed. Five days' notice to the adverse parties must be given of a second application by a party, seeking to re-extend the time for filing his brief. All applications must show the date of submission of the cause, the date upon which the time of the application for filing briefs will expire, and the order of the court will fix the date on or before which the brief shall be filed. No extension of time will be granted in appeals from awards of the Industrial Board. See 

Acts 1917, Chap. 63, page 155, sec. 3.

Rule 21. The brief of appellant shall contain a short and clear statement disclosing:

First. The nature of the action. 

Second. What the issues were.

Third. How the issues were decided, and what the judgment or decree was. 

Fourth. The errors relied upon for reversal.

Fifth. A concise statement of so much of the record as fully presents every error and exception relied on, referring to the pages and lines of the transcript. If the insufficiency of the evidence to sustain the verdict or finding in fact or law is assigned, the statement shall contain a condensed recital of the evidence in narrative form so as to present the substance clearly and concisely. The statement will be taken to be accurate and sufficient for a full understanding of the questions for decision unless the opposite party in his brief shall make necessary corrections or additions. When error is predicated on the giving or refusal of instructions the statement must recite the instructions that were given. 1 Sixth. The briefs shall contain under the heading "Propositions and Authorities" a copy of each assigned error relied on stating its number as designated in the original assignment of error, and in case the error assigned is the overruling of the motion for a new trial, then the cause relied upon numbered as in the motion. Each of the foregoing assignments shall be separately considered by separately numbered propositions concisely stated without argument, supported by separately numbered or lettered points and authorities. Assigned errors not treated as herein directed shall be deemed as waived. In citing cases the names of the parties must be given, with the book and page where reported. Indiana cases officially reported shall be cited from the book and page of the official report. No alleged error or point not contained in the statement of errors or points shall be raised afterwards either by reply brief or oral or printed argument, or by a petition for a rehearing. 

Rule 22. Appellee, in his answer brief, may point out any omissions or inaccuracies in appellant's statement of the record. 

Where appellee relies upon facts appearing in the record which are not recited in appellant's brief, to render harmless an alleged error committed by the trial court, he shall set them out in his brief. If the making of such corrections requires the appellee to set out parts of the record omitted by appellant, which these rules require him to set out, the court may tax against appellant

any costs incurred by appellee on account of such omission. 

Following such statement, if any, the brief shall contain a short and clear statement of the propositions by which counsel seek to meet the alleged errors, and to sustain the judgment or decree, or by which such errors are obviated, and specifically apply them to the propositions presented by appellant, in the same order that they were presented by appellant. Under each of such propositions shall be grouped the points of law and authorities relied on to support it, in like manner as required in the appellant's brief. The brief of appellee on cross-errors shall be prepared in the manner required in preparing appellant's brief. 

The brief of appellant, in answer thereto, shall be prepared in the manner required for appellee's brief in answer to the assignment of errors. Reply briefs shall be prepared substantially like answer briefs.

Rule 23. The brief of any party may be followed by an argument in support of such brief, which shall be distinct therefrom, but shall be bound with the same. The argument shall be confined to discussion and elaboration of the points contained in the briefs. The names of counsel shall be affixed to all briefs filed by them. 

Rule 24. All briefs shall be printed in pica or 11-point type and adequately leaded, or typewritten, but in either case the work to be done in a neat and workmanlike manner, on white opaque, unglazed paper, seven by nine inches, leaving a margin of at least one inch at the left side and at least three-eighths of an inch on the right side, bound in book or pamphlet form, with a suitable cover containing the number and title of the cause and the court in which the case is pending. If typewritten, the lines shall be double spaced. Quotations may be single spaced. The fifth part of the statement required by 

Rule 21, may, however, on leave of court, be bound separately from the remainder of the brief. Not less than ten copies of each brief, three of which, if typewritten, shall be ribbon copies, shall be filed with the clerk, one of which shall be for the use of the opposite party. No carbon copies will be received unless clearly legible.

Rule 25. Upon notice to the adverse party, and the presentation of a verified application setting forth the proposed amend-

ment and the reasons on which it is based, the court, in its discretion, may grant leave to amend briefs, upon such terms as it shall prescribe.

ORAL ARGUMENTS. 

Rule 26. Upon separate petition in writing by either party, the court will hear oral argument on the questions presented on the assigned errors, if the application is made within the time allowed for filing briefs; otherwise, the court in its discretion will refuse the application. The court will order, without application, oral argument in such cases as it deems to require the same. When a cause is set down for oral argument, the clerk shall at once notify the respective counsel by mail. Oral argument will not be heard on motions or petitions unless ordered by the court.

Rule 27. Oral argument shall be limited to some definite time, not exceeding two hours (to be equally divided between the parties) except in cases in which counsel shall request and secure in advance of the argument a longer time. 

Rule 28. Oral argument shall be confined to the discussion of the propositions and authorities contained in the briefs. The failure to discuss in oral argument points properly made in the briefs shall not be deemed a waiver of such points, but they will be fully considered in determining the cause. 

PETITIONS FOR REHEARING. 

Rule 29. Application for a rehearing of any cause shall be made by petition, separate from the briefs, signed by counsel, filed with the clerk within sixty days from the rendition of the judgment, stating concisely the cause for which the judgment is supposed to be erroneous, which application shall be supported by briefs only, with arguments set out therein, if desired. Ten copies of the brief must be filed at the same time the petition is filed, and one copy of the brief shall be delivered at once by the clerk to each judge. When a rehearing is granted, the order granting it shall not have the effect to set aside the submission of the cause unless specially directed; but the record shall be returned to the files of the clerk's office and the cause shall stand

for hearing as a submitted cause, unless, upon leave granted on special request, in writing, time is given. 

PETITIONS TO TRANSFER TO SUPREME COURT. 

Rule 2912. An application to transfer a cause from the

Appellate Court to the Supreme Court, after a petition for rehearing has been overruled, shall set forth (1) that the Appellate Court has decided the case, or some material part thereof, against the party who asks that it be transferred, (2) that a written opinion setting forth the reasons on which such judgment was based was filed by the Appellate Court, giving the date when it was filed, (3) that the Appellate Court overruled a petition for rehearing duly presented by such party within the time allowed, stating on what date it was overruled; and having thus shown the jurisdiction, the petition (4) shall particularly and specifically and without argument point out what there is in the opinion of the Appellate Court which contravenes a ruling precedent of the Supreme Court, or which erroneously decides a new question of law, indicating the ruling precedent, or concisely stating the new question of law, referred to. Such petition (5) shall be signed by the petitioner or by his attorney. (6) If briefs are filed, they shall be separate from and filed with the petition; but they shall not be necessary to invoke the action of the court on the petition. Parties opposing a transfer may file briefs within 40 days after the ruling on the petition for rehearing.

POSSESSION AND REMOVAL OF RECORDS. 

Rule 30. Parties will be entitled to the possession of the transcript during the time limited for preparing and filing briefs. Thereafter the transcript shall not be taken from the clerk's office by any party, without leave of court first obtained on written application. Possession of the transcript by any party, after the expiration of the time for filing his briefs, without leave of court first obtained, may be treated as a contempt of court. 

Rule 31. After a case has been decided, neither the record nor the opinion shall be taken from the office of the clerk, except

by a judge of the court or by the official reporter; and except that the attorneys may receipt for and take out the transcript for the purpose of preparing a petition for rehearing and briefs thereon. And the clerk is required to enforce this rule.

DOCKETING AND DISTRIBUTION OF CAUSES. 

Rule 32. At the expiration of the time for filing all briefs the clerk will enter each cause upon the general docket or the advanced docket, in the order in which the causes were submitted. Upon the advanced docket the clerk shall enter all criminal causes, appeals from the Public Service Commission and Industrial Board, and from judgments in habeas corpus, such causes as are advanced by operation of law, such civil causes as the court may order transferred from the general docket, and such civil causes as shall be transferred from one court to the other, where the causes pending in the court to which it is transferred that were submitted on the date of its submission have been distributed. Upon the general docket the clerk shall enter all civil causes, except as otherwise provided in this rule. 

On written application of either party, civil causes which involve questions as to the constitutionality of any law, public revenue, public health, or the settlement of trusts, or which the court may deem of general public concern, may be transferred to the advanced docket, when fully briefed. Causes upon the advanced docket shall be entitled to precedence. All causes that stand for final hearing shall be determined in the order of their submission as nearly as may be done.

SUPERSEDEAS. 

Rule 33. An application for supersedeas must be accompanied by a brief making a prima facie showing that the transcript presents one or more questions for decision, referring to the transcript by pages and lines and pointing out the errors relied on. Briefs upon application for supersedeas will not be received as briefs upon the final hearing. 

CERTIORARI. 

Rule 34. Applications for certiorari shall be verified and shall clearly designate the parts of the record asserted to be

defective, improperly omitted, or improperly incorporated in the transcript. Affidavits may be filed in support of the application. 

The respective parties may file briefs, but oral argument will not be heard.

SECURITY FOR COSTS. 

Rule 35. All appeals prosecuted without a bond sufficient to secure the payment of costs may be dismissed upon motion and a sufficient showing by affidavit that the appellant is a nonresident of the state, unless a bond sufficient to cover costs shall be filed within a time fixed by the court. Notice shall be given as in the case of other motions. Notice of the order made upon such motion shall be given by the clerk to the party or his attorney.

DISMISSAL OF CAUSES. 

Rule 36. Where a case not appealed in term has been on the docket ninety days or more, and there is no appearance by the appellee, and no steps have been taken to bring him into court; or where a notice has been issued and proves ineffectual from any cause, and no steps are taken for more than ninety days after the issuance of such ineffectual notice to bring the appellee into court, the clerk shall enter an order dismissing the appeal. 

OPINIONS, WHEN TO BE CERTIFIED. 

Rule 37. Opinions and judgments shall not be certified to the court below by the clerk of this court, until the expiration of sixty days, unless by order of this court, or on the filing of a waiver of a petition for rehearing, which order of court or filing of waiver shall be certified by the clerk with the opinion; except that in criminal cases where the defendant has failed to give bail and obtain a stay of judgment pending his appeal, under Acts 1911, Chapter 154, page 410, as amended by Acts 1913, Chapter 225, page 647 (Burns 1914, sec. 2218a, et seq.), and is not at large by reason thereof, the judgment and opinion on appeal shall be certified to the trial court immediately.

10 

SUPREME COURT REPORTER. 

Rule 38. The opinions of the court shall not be delivered to the reporter until the expiration of sixty days from the determination of the cause, unless certified as hereinbefore provided; and, in cases where petitions for rehearing are filed, the opinions therein shall not be delivered to the reporter until such petitions are overruled. If a petition for rehearing is overruled by the 

Appellate Court the opinion of that court shall not be delivered to the reporter until thirty days thereafter, and in case a petition to transfer the cause to the Supreme Court is filed, not until such petition is ruled on. 

WITHDRAWAL OF PAPERS AFTER DISMISSAL. 

Rule 39. Where an appeal shall have been dismissed, the transcript shall not be withdrawn from the files of this court to be used in another appeal or for any other purpose, without special leave of the court in term or of a judge thereof in vacation, and only on good cause shown by affidavit. 

COURT LIBRARY. 

Rule 40. No book belonging to the law library shall be removed from the library room, except for the purpose of oral argument, into the court or consultation room, and it shall be delivered to the court or returned to the library at the conclusion of the argument. Any violation of this rule will be treated as a contempt of court.

ROLL OF ATTORNEYS AND ADMISSIONS TO BAR. 

Rule 41-1. The names of all attorneys, who, prior to July 1, 1931, were admitted to the bar of this court, whose names have not subsequently been stricken therefrom and who, since the date of their respective admissions, have not been removed or suspended by any court of record, shall be and constitute the roll of attorneys of this court on July 1, 1931.

The clerk of this court shall keep a permanent record known as the roll of attorneys in which the names of all such attorneys and all attorneys and persons subsequently admitted to practice law shall be listed in their alphabetical order,

ADMISSION WITHOUT EXAMINATION-TEMPORARY PRACTICE. 

Rule 41-2. Any court may permit an attorney who is not a resident of this state to practice law therein during any term of such court, upon his taking an oath for the faithful discharge of his duties." S. 833, ch. 38, Acts 1881, S. 1035, Burns 1926.

ADMISSION WITHOUT EXAMINATION-TO PRACTICE GENERALLY. 

Rule 41-3. Any attorney who, prior to July 1, 1931, was duly admitted to practice law in a circuit court of any county of this state and who, on that date, was actively engaged in the practice of law, may be admitted to practice law by this court upon submitting to this court a certificate signed by the judge of the circuit court of the county in which he is engaged in the practice of law or by the president of the bar association of such county, showing that such attorney is a voter, is a person of good moral character, is in good standing at the bar and is actually engaged in the practice of law in said county, and has been engaged in the practice of law for a period of at least six months immediately prior thereto.

ADMISSION WITHOUT EXAMINATION-UPON FOREIGN LICENSE. 

Rule 41-4. Any person who has been admitted to practice law in the highest court in any other state or territory or the District of Columbia of the United States, or in another country whose jurisprudence is based upon the principles of the English Common Law and who has actually practiced at least three years in such court or in the highest court of original jurisdiction therein, may be admitted to practice law in this state upon his filing with this court a "judge's certificate of practice upon foreign license" (form to be provided the applicant by the clerk of this court) and upon the motion of a member of the bar of this court who shall show that the applicant is a citizen of the United States, a person of good moral character and that he has become a bona fide resident voter of the State of Indiana. 

ADMISSION UPON EXAMINATION. 

Rule 41-5. There is hereby constituted a State Board of Law 

Examiners for the State of Indiana, for the purpose and with

the powers hereinafter set forth. Said Board shall consist of five members of the bar, one from each Supreme Court Judicial District, and shall be appointed by this court to serve for terms of three years and until their successors are appointed. The board shall elect annually a president, vice-president and secretary-treasurer.

Rule 41-6. The board shall meet and conduct three examinations annually in Indianapolis, in quarters to be designated by the court, beginning on the first Monday in March, the second 

Monday in July, and the first Monday in October. Such part of said examinations as deals with questions on legal subjects shall be conducted by printed interrogatories, shall be uniform and shall be supervised by the members of the board as a body: provided, however, that the board may supplement said written examination with an oral examination by the board. A majority of the board shall constitute a quorum. In the absence of a quorum, the court shall appoint an Examiner or Examiners pro tem from the bar of the Supreme Court. The board shall certify to the court those who have met the requirements for admission to the bar, and the applicants so certified shall appear in person before this court and may be admitted to the bar on motion in open court. Such admission shall entitle the attorney to practice in any court of the state. 

Rule 41-7. Any resident voter of the State of Indiana who is of good moral character shall be entitled at any time, under such rules and regulations as the Board of Law Examiners shall provide, and subject to the rules and regulations herein set forth, to make application for admission to the bar. Each applicant shall file the application hereinafter provided for and shall present to the Board of Law Examiners in the manner hereinafter provided, satisfactory proof that he is a resident voter of the state, and also that he is a person possessed of those requisites of good moral character necessary to qualify one to serve in the dual capacities of an attorney, to-wit, as an officer of the courts, and as legal counselor or practitioner; and to faithfully perform his duties to the courts, the public and his clients. 

Rule 41-8. Each application for admission must be made on forms prescribed by the board and filed with the clerk of this court in duplicate at least three weeks before a stated meeting of

the board, and must be accompanied by all proofs required and a fee of fifteen dollars ($15.00). The clerk shall forward one copy of said application and the fee deposited to the secretary of the board. If said applicant shall be unable to take the examination for any reason, then the applicant, at his election, may permit the fee to stand for a subsequent examination, or upon demand upon said Secretary, be entitled to a return of one-half thereof. After examination no part of said fee shall be returnable.

Rule 41-9. At the time the members of the Board of Law 

Examiners are appointed there shall be appointed by this court a Committee on Character and Fitness in each Supreme Court Judicial District, consisting of at least one attorney at law from each county in such district and the member of the board for that district. The members of such committee shall continue in office until their successors are appointed. The board shall send a copy of each application for admission to the bar of this state to the member of the board of Law Examiners from the district in which the applicant resides. The committee shall require the attendance before it or some member designated by the board member for the district, of each applicant from that district, and inquire into the question as to whether or not the applicant is possessed of those requisites of good moral character (other than a knowledge of the law) necessary to qualify him to serve as an attorney. The applicant shall present to the committee, or its member, at least three practicing attorneys of such district who are personally acquainted with said applicant, residing in the district with such applicant, to testify as to such requisites of good moral character and the general fitness of such applicant for admission to practice law. In the event that said applicant finds it impossible to secure the personal attendance of said attorneys, or all of them, he may submit in lieu thereof their affidavits as to said subject-matter; provided that said affidavits shall set forth in detail the facts upon which the opinion is based. If the applicant attended an organized law school said applicant may present in lieu of such affidavits, similar affidavits from at least three of his law professors; or one affidavit from the dean of the law school, certifying that a majority of his faculty concur in the statement of facts contained in the

affidavit. The committee shall make such further inquiry into the matter as it sees fit.

The committee or member hearing the matter shall make a finding and recommend the approval or disapproval of the applicant and forward the finding and recommendation and all papers filed in connection therewith to the board, which shall at its next stated meeting review said finding, make such further inquiry as it sees fit and take such action as the case requires. 

Rule 41-10. The application shall contain a sworn statement showing the full name, age, birthplace, place of residence and length of residence in such place of said applicant; if born in a foreign country, at what age he came to the United States, and when and where he was naturalized; the name, birthplace, resi- ´ dence and occupation of his parents; the schools attended by him, and the dates of such attendance; the name and location of the college or colleges attended by him, and degrees received, if any; the time employed in law offices, if any, together with a list of such offices and the dates of employment in each; whether he has applied for admission to practice law in any other state or country, and if so, when and where and whether he was admitted to practice in such other state or country; and if not admitted, why he was not admitted; whether he was engaged in any occupation, business or profession and if so when and where, giving the names and addresses of his employers, the positions occupied by him and the period of employment; and whether he has ever been a party to a legal action, or proceeding of any kind, and if so, the full details of his interests therein. "State" as used in this rule shall include the District of Columbia, and any territory or possession of the United States. 

Rule 41-11. If an applicant for admission to the bar by examination shall be rejected at a second examination he shall not again be admitted to an examination until one examination has intervened after such rejection. If an applicant shall be rejected at a third or fourth examination he shall not again be admitted to an examination until two examinations have intervened after such rejection. Before a third or subsequent examination is allowed he must furnish evidence satisfactory to the board that he has diligently pursued the study of law since his last examination.

Rule 41-12. Applications for admission and all information in reference thereto shall be made upon blank forms furnished by the clerk of this court.

Rule 41-13. The board shall have authority to prescribe such forms and adopt such rules as are necessary, not inconsistent herewith. The members of said board shall be allowed and paid out of the fees received by the board from applicants for admission, their necessary expenses and reasonable compensation, such amounts to be fixed by the court and not to exceed the amount received in fees.

Rule 41-14. The Board of Law Examiners shall audit an nually the accounts of its secretary-treasurer and shall report to the court before the October meeting a detailed statement of the finances of the board together with such recommendations as shall seem advisable.

Rule 41-15. The Board of Law Examiners shall act and re port on all applications within forty-five days after the stated meeting at which the applicant presented himself for admission. 

Rule 41-16. Applicants for admission to the bar who are eligible to admission under the foregoing rules may be admitted by appearing in person before the court on a day when the court is sitting and having their admission moved by a member of the bar of the court.

CERTIFICATION TO COUNTIES OF ADMISSION. 

Rule 41-17. Upon the admission of an applicant to practice law the clerk of this court shall issue to said applicant a proper certificate showing such admission and the clerk shall send to the circuit court of the county of the applicant's residence for entry in the records of such court, a certified copy of the Supreme 

Court order book entry of such admission.

CERTIFICATION FROM COUNTIES OF REMOVALS AND SUSPENSIONS. 

Rule 41-18. When any court of record has finally removed or suspended an attorney from practicing therein in the manner provided by law (S. 842–846, ch. 38, Acts 1881, S. 1044-1047,

Burns 1926) the Attorney General or/and the prosecuting attorney of the judicial circuit in which said court is located, shall

cause a certified copy of such judgment of removal or suspension to be filed in this court. 1 The court appoints as members of the Board of Law Examiners for terms of three years and until their successors are appointed the following members of the bar:

First Supreme Court Judicial District-Bernard C. Gavit, Bloomington.

Second Supreme Court Judicial District-Julian Sharpnack, Columbus.

Third Supreme Court Judicial District-Remster A. Bingham, Indianapolis.

Fourth Supreme Court Judicial District-Milo N. Feightner, Huntington.

Fifth Supreme Court Judicial District-Lenn J. Orae, South 

Bend. July 7, 1931.

Rule 42. Applications to be admitted to bail pending appeals of criminal cases as provided in Chapter 121, Acts 1929 shall be filed in duplicate and shall be typewritten upon blank printed forms to be provided by the clerk, setting out in separately numbered items the information indicated by such forms.

The application shall be signed and sworn to by the applicant, and the discovery of any false statement in an application will result in the summary dismissal of the application, or if bond has been given, in the cancellation thereof.

The applicant shall serve upon the prosecuting attorney of the circuit in which judgment was rendered, a copy of such application, and acknowledgment or proof of such service endorsed on the original copy of the application shall be required by the clerk before filing the same. The clerk, upon the filing of the application, shall forthwith deliver a duplicate copy thereof to the Attorney-General, who shall execute a receipt for the same on the original copy.

The application shall stand for hearing or consideration at the earliest convenience of the court, or any judge thereof during its vacation (recess), on or after the seventh day (excluding 

Sundays and holidays) following its filing. The Attorney-General and the prosecuting attorney may either or both contest the application by objections in writing to be filed not later than

said seventh day. Oral hearings or arguments will not be granted except on the court's own motion, or the motion of a judge thereof during the vacation (recess) of the court. 

The clerk shall keep a bail docket for each court (Supreme and Appellate) properly indexed in which he shall show the filing of applications, and objections, the action of the court and the filing and approval of bonds.

The undertaking of said bonds shall conform to the provisions of the statute, S. 2388 Burns 1926. All sureties and bonds shall be approved by the clerk, and said clerk may accept bonds executed by surety companies authorized by law to do business in this state, he may accept the amount of the bonds in cash or certified bank checks and in accepting cash bonds may accept any properly endorsed obligation of the United States government or of any county, township or municipality of Indiana in lieu of cash. He may accept personal bonds only where he is convinced from his own knowledge or investigation that the sureties are solvent and worth more than twice the amount of the bonds in unincumbered real estate.

The above rules supersede all former rules of court and became effective November 15, 1933.

A typewritten ribbon copy of a brief can be reproduced on paper of the same quality and style at a price very much less than it costs to have such copy printed. 

While the rules of the court now in force do not specifically authorize such a reproduced brief, it is believed if one wants to file in the court such a brief and will file a petition asking permission to do so and with his petition one page of the reproduced brief, the court will grant him the right to file such a brief.

These rules seem to be clear and to enable attorneys to know what they mean. But as there has been much misunderstanding of the rules heretofore adopted by the court, concerning the preparation of briefs, there will be annotations of some of them showing how the courts have construed them. wwww

ASSIGNMENT OF ERRORS 

141 1 

PREPARATION OF THE TRANSCRIPT. 

The party obtaining the transcript must cause the same to be neatly and securely bound, the lines numbered, pages numbered at the bottom, marginal notes placed on the margin of each page showing the several parts of the pleadings in the cause, the exhibits, the rulings of the court, and the bill of exceptions. If the evidence is set out by deposition or otherwise, the name of each witness, whether the examination is direct, cross or redirect, shall be stated on the margin of each page and shall also note on the margin all motions and rulings thereon, the instructions given and refused and shall prepare an index referring to the initial page of the direct, cross and re-examination of each witness and of each pleading, Exhibit, or other paper in the record, and this index shall form the first page of the transcript. 

Rule 3. If the index is too long for one page, the several pages of it may be properly numbered and all of them may in their proper order be attached to the front page of the transcript. Sexauer v. Star Milling Co., 173 Ind. 

342, 90 N. E. 474. This rule must be complied with or the case will be dismissed, unless a reasonable excuse is offered for the non-compliance. Egan v. O. & M. Co., 138 

Ind. 274, 37 N. E. 1014; and if it is complied with in part, that part only will be considered. Roberts v. Wolfe, 165 

Ind. 199, 74 N. E. 990.

ASSIGNMENT OF ERRORS. 

The appellant shall on the transcript, or on a paper attached thereto, entitle the cause and make a specific

ASSIGNMENT OF ERRORS 

assignment of the errors upon which he relies for reversal. Each specification of error shall be complete in itself and shall be properly numbered. The party or parties appealing are the plaintiff, or plaintiffs, and are designated as the appellant, or appellants, and the party or parties not appealing are the defendants and are designated as the appellee, or appellees. The assignment of errors is the complaint in the court to which the appeal is taken and must be signed by the party or parties appealing or by their attorneys, and must contain the full names of the parties to the appeal as they appeared in the cause in the trial court. Rule 4, State ex rel. v. 

Delano, 34 Ind. 52; Ruby v. Hough, 161 Ind. 203, 67 N. E. 

267. A joint assignment must be good as to all who join in it, or it is good as to none. Hawkins v. Heinzman, 126 Ind. 551, 25 N. E. 708. The assignment of errors should be made before the transcript is filed, but it may be made after the filing of the transcript, if made within the time for the appeal. It cannot be made after the time for appeal has passed. Lawrence v. Wood, Admr., 122 Ind. 452, 24 N. E. 189; and if not so made the cause will be dismissed. Several assignments may be made by different parties on an appeal from a joint judgment and there should be but one title to the case and it is not necessary that the title be repeated for each separate assignment. Moorehouse v. Kunkalman, 177 Ind. 471, 96 N. E. 600. The assignment may be amended without leave or notice before submission. Rule 4. If the cause has been submitted, a petition to amend must be filed, notice given and leave granted. Rule 4.

If the assignment of errors is fatally defective and the time for appeal is passed the assignment can not be amended. Prough v. Prough, 174 Ind. 57, 91 N. E. 337; nor will permission to amend it be granted after submission unless due care and diligence are shown. Rule 4. 

Ex parte Washington National Bank, 163 Ind. 476, 72 

N. E. 260.

ASSIGNMENT OF ERRORS-GENERAL RULES 143 FORM FOR THE ASSIGNMENT OF ERRORS. 

IN THE SUPREME COURT (APPELLATE COURT) STATE OF INDIANA JOHN JONES, SAMUEL SMITH, Appellants,

V. 

JOHN JAMES, SAMUEL SMALL, Appellees.

MAY TERM, 1931. 

In the above entitled cause the appellants say there is manifest error in the judgment and proceedings in the same, in this, to-wit:- 

1. The court erred in sustaining the appellees' demurrer to the first paragraph of appellants' complaint. 

2. The court erred in overruling the appellants' demurrer to the second paragraph of the appellees' answer. 

3. The court erred in sustaining the appellees' demurrer to the second paragraph of the appellants' reply. 

4. The court erred in each of its conclusions of law. 

5. The court erred in overruling the appellants' motion for a new trial. 

WHEREFORE, for each of said errors the appellants ask that the cause be reserved. 

J. M.

O. C. 

Attorneys for Appellants.

As tending to make clear what must be assigned as error I quote the following from Buskirk's Practice, p. 113: "Three General Rules.-There are three general rules, which, if borne in mind by counsel, will greatly aid them in making correct assignments of errors, and they are:

ASSIGNMENT OF ERRORS-GENERAL RULES 

First. If the error complained of relates to the ruling of the court on the pleadings, whether the question is raised by a demurrer or motion to reject or strike out, or to make more specific, such ruling need not be assigned as a reason for a new trial, but the assignment should specify the paragraph of the pleading to which the demurrer was sustained or overruled, what paragraph was rejected or struck out, and if the motion to reject or strike out was overruled, the number of the paragraph to which the motion was addressed should be given. 

Second. If the error complained of relates to a matter which has to be assigned as a reason for a new trial, the simple assignment that the court erred in overruling the motion for a new trial will present such question for review in the Supreme Court. We will hereafter more fully consider this proposition. 

Third. If the error complained of does not relate to rulings on the pleadings, and does not constitute a reason for a new trial, there should be a specific assignment of error, which will distinctly point out the particular ruling complained of.

Cases Coming within Third Proposition.-The following may be mentioned as coming within the third class: 1. That the court erred in sustaining or overruling a demurrer to the evidence. 2. That the court erred in its conclusions of law upon the facts specially found. 3. That the court erred in overruling or sustaining the motion for judgment upon the answers to interrogatories, notwithstanding the general verdict. 4. That the court erred in overruling the motion for a venire de novo. 5. That the court erred in sustaining or overruling the motion in arrest of judgment. 

6. That the court erred in sustaining or overruling the motion to vacate, change, or modify the judgment rendered. 

7. That the court erred in its order requiring the payment of the money therein specified. 8. That the court erred in its order compelling the execution of the instrument of writing therein specified. 9. That the court erred in its order for the delivery or assignment of the securities, or evidences of debt, or documents, or things in action therein specifically www

ASSIGNMENT OF CROSS-ERRORS 

145 described and pointed out. 10. The court erred in its order for the delivery of the real estate therein described. 11. That the court erred in its order for the sale of the real estate therein described. 12. That the court erred in granting the injunction set out in the record. 13. That the court erred in dissolving the injunction set out in the record. 14. The court erred in overruling the motion to dissolve the injunction set out in the record. 15. That the court erred in granting, in vacation, the injunction set out in the record. 

16. That the court erred in discharging the prisoner or other person named in the writ of habeas corpus. 17. That the court erred in refusing to discharge the prisoner or other person, and in remanding such person into custody. 

It should be noted that six of the above assignments of error are in appeals from final judgments, and the others are in appeals from interlocutory judgments, under section 576 of the code."

ASSIGNMENT OF CROSS-ERRORS. 

The appellee or appellees may, as of right and without notice, assign cross-errors, within sixty days after the submission of the cause, but if cross-errors are not assigned within that time, then notice shall be given the appellant, or appellants, and leave applied for in writing, which must show a satisfactory excuse for the failure to assign such errors within sixty days from the submission of the cause, or leave will not be granted, and no such assignment seeking to reverse the judgment shall be made after the time for taking an appeal, Rule 5. Crosserrors must be assigned on the transcript or they will not be considered. Merchants Nat. Bank v. Delaware School 

Tp., 185 Ind. 658, 114 N. E. 450; and the record must affirmatively show the cross-errors were assigned within

FORM FOR ASSIGNMENT OF CROSS-ERRORS 

the time allowed. It is not necessary that the assignment of cross-errors should set out the names of all the parties. 

Busenbark v. Clements, 22 App. 557, 53 N. E. 665. There may be assigned as a cross-error any ruling of the court which the party assigning it might have appealed from and on which he could have assigned error. Patoka Tp. v. Hopkins, 131 Ind. 142, 30 N. E. 896; American Ins. 

Co. v. Bertram, 163 Ind. 51, 70 N. E. 258. If the appellee asks that the judgment be affirmed it will not be reversed on cross-errors assigned by him. Thomas v. Simmons, 103 Ind. 538, 2 N. E. 203. The dismissal of the cause will not carry with it a dismissal as to the cross-errors assigned, but they remain and will be considered. Feder v. 

Field, 117 Ind. 386, 20 N. E. 129.

FORM FOR ASSIGNMENT OF CROSS-ERRORS. 

The appellees by way of assignment of cross-errors say that the court committed the following errors against them, to-wit:- 

1. The court erred in overruling their demurrer to the 2nd paragraph of answer to the first paragraph of complaint. 

2. The court erred in overruling their motion to modify the judgment.

And the appellees pray the court to consider and decide the errors assigned by them. 

W. & B. Attorneys for Appellees.

WHO ARE PARTIES TO THE APPEAL, ETC. 147 WHO ARE PARTIES TO THE APPEAL AND HOW 

IT IS PERFECTED. 

Either party may appeal, but not after receiving money on the judgment. Burns 1933, 2—3201; Burns 1926, 695; 

Baldwin, 471; unless he may receive it in any event, 94 

App. 211.

No one who was not a party to the cause in the trial court, or who does not represent such a party can be a party on appeal. If one sues, or is sued in a trust or representative capacity, he must be named in the assignment of errors in that capacity, and if he is not so named, the assignment may be corrected, if made before the time for appeal has expired, but it cannot be made afterward. 

Milburn v. Cory, 184 Ind. 341, 110 N. E. 193.

It is not enough to designate such a one as trustee, executor, administrator, but the name of the beneficiary must also be stated. Bender v. State, 176 Ind. 70, 95 

N. E. 305. The death of any or all of the parties before judgment shall not cause the proceedings to abate, but the names of the proper persons may be substituted upon consent or upon notice and the cause may proceed. Burns 

1933, 2-3216; Burns 1926, 710; Baldwin, 479. Wilkinson v. Vodermark, 32 App. 633, 70 N. E. 538. If such death occurs after judgment and before appeal, the appeal may be taken in the name or names of the personal representatives of the deceased and against the personal representatives of the deceased by service of notice of the appeal. Burns 1933, 2-3215; Burns 1926, 709; Baldwin, 478. This means that if any person, a party to the cause, dies after judgment and who is a necessary party to the appeal, either as appellant or appellee, his administrator, executor or heirs must be substituted for him and notice of the appeal must be given by or served on such representatives in the same manner as would have been required as to him, if he had not died. 

It is not necessary to make the heirs parties unless the title to real estate is involved. Burns 1933, 2-227;

TERM TIME APPEAL 

1 

Burns 1926, 284; Baldwin, 37. Rich Grove Tp. v. Emmett, 163 Ind. 560, 72 N. E. 543. If the parties to the judgment, or their representatives are not parties to the appeal, it will be dismissed, and they can not be made. parties after the time for appeal has passed. Thompson v. Newson, 52 App. 444, 100 N. E. 772. If the death of any or all of the parties occurs after submission no change of parties is required, and judgment is rendered as at the time the submission was made. Burns 1933, 2-3235; Burns 1926, 729; Baldwin, 510. Helms v. Cook, 58 App. 259, 108 N. E. 147.

Parties to the suit in the trial court may be made parties to the appeal in either of two ways-by a term time or vacation appeal. 

Term time appeal. A term time appeal is perfected by doing the following things at the term at which the judgment appealed from is rendered, or if it is rendered before a motion for a new trial is overruled, at the term such ruling is made, viz.: the appeal must be prayed, the amount of the bond fixed, the sureties named, the time fixed within which the bond must be filed in the case, and it must be filed within that time and the transcript must be filed in the office of the Clerk of the Supreme Court within sixty days after filing the bond. Provision is made for one extension of time with which to file the transcript. Burns 1933, 2–3204; Burns 1926, 698; Baldwin, 480. Tuttle v. Fowler, 183 Ind. 99, 107 N. E. 674; 

Equitable Surety Company v. Taylor, 71 App. 382, 121 

N. E. 283; Rohrbaugh v. Leas, 63 App. 544, 114 N. E. 762.

If the bond is filed and approved in open court at the term at which the judgment was rendered, or the motion for a new trial was overruled, if overruled after judgment is rendered, the appeal is a term time appeal and no notice of the appeal to any party to the suit is necessary in order to bring him into the court to which the appeal is taken. Wilson v. Bennett, 132 Ind. 210, 31 N. E. 

184; Thompson v. Ryan, 183 Ind. 232, 108 N. E. 98. The clerk cannot approve the sureties on the bond. This must be done by the court; an appeal is taken when all the

VACATION APPEAL 

149 11 steps named in the statute to confer jurisdiction upon the court to which the appeal is taken, have been taken. 

Daugherty v. Payne, 175 Ind. 603, 95 N. E. 233. The purpose of the bond is to stop execution. Buchanan v. 

Milligan, 125 Ind. 332, 25 N. E. 349. 

Vacation appeal. A vacation appeal is perfected by serving notice in writing on the adverse party, or his attorney, and also on the clerk of the court in which the judgment was rendered stating an appeal from the judgment, or some part thereof; or by procuring from the clerk of the court a transcript of the record or so much as is embraced in the appeal and filing the same in the office of the clerk of the Supreme Court, who shall issue notice of the appeal to the appellee. Burns 1933, 2-3206; 

Burns 1926, 700; Baldwin, 482. The first method of serving the notice is called the "unofficial" and the second the "official" method. Antioch Baptist Church v. Morton, 52 App. 546, 100 N. E. 874. If a term time appeal is prayed but not perfected, a vacation appeal of the same case may be maintained if the time for appeal is not passed, and if the transcript has been filed, notice may be given by the clerk as is required in a vacation appeal without withdrawing the transcript, and an appearance of the appellee to the appeal dispenses with notice. Cleveland, etc. v. Smith, 177 Ind. 524, 97 N. E. 164. In such a case notice to the appellee is necessary unless waived and if “unofficial” notice is given the transcript must be withdrawn and refilled, after the notice is given. Daugherty v. Brown, 21 App. 115, 51 N. E. 729. An appeal is perfected when the transcript with a proper assignment of errors is filed with the clerk of the Supreme Court within the time limited for taking an appeal without service of notice on the appellee. Tate v. Hamlin, 149 Ind. 94, 41 

N. E. 356. If the unofficial method is pursued, the notice may be prepared and served by the appellant or his attorney, on the clerk of the court from which the appeal is taken and on the appellee or appellees, or his or their attorney. Both must be served, and service on the attorney of record is sufficient, unless the appellant knows

VACATION APPEAL 

such attorney no longer represents the litigant, Masters v. Abbitt, 51 App. 429, 99 N. E. 815. The notice must be in writing and personally served and cannot be served by mail. Chicago, etc. Co. v. Sanders, 63 App. 

586, 114 N. E. 986. Proof of service may be shown by the written acknowledgment of the person served, or by the affidavit of the party who made the service, and the proof of service is a part of the files of the court to which the appeal is taken and may be a separate paper and not bound with the transcript. Perkins v. Indiana Mfg. Co., 58 App. 220, 108 N. E. 165. The transcript must be filed within sixty days after giving the notice of appeal and within the time for taking an appeal. Johnson v. 

Stephenson, 104 Ind. 368, 4 N. E. 46.

If the "Official" notice is given it cannot be given until the transcript is filed with the clerk-may be given before errors are assigned. Price v. Baker, 41 Ind. 572.

Must be served on the appellee in person, and not on his attorney. Burns 1933, 2-3206; Burns 1926, 700; Baldwin, 482. Tate v. Hamlin, 149 Ind. 94, 41 N. E. 356. No notice to the clerk is necessary. Clark v. State, 187 Ind. 

276, 117 N. E. 965. If the transcript and assignment of errors were filed within the time for appeal the notice may be given after the time for appeal has expired. Tate v. Hamlin, 149 Ind. 94, 41 N. E. 356;-but if the cause is on the docket for ninety days without notice, or attempt to get notice, the cause will be dismissed. Rule 36. Bechtell v. Central, etc., 182 Ind. 568, 107 N. E. 73. Notice by publication can only be given when the appellee is a nonresident of the state and has no attorney of record residing in this state. Tate v. Hamlin, 149 Ind. 94, 41 

N. E. 356. The necessity for notice is waived by joining in error, Lowe v. Turpie, 147 Ind. 652, 44 N. E. 25; submission by agreement, Higbee v. Rodeman, 129 Ind. 244, 28 N. E. 442; and the filing of a brief on the merits. An appearance cannot be withdrawn except the court grants leave so to do. Cleveland, etc. Co. v. Smith, 177 Ind. 524, 97 N. E. 164. Rule 9.

CO-PARTIES, HOW MADE PARTIES TO APPEAL 151 CO-PARTIES, HOW MADE PARTIES TO THE APPEAL. 

Whenever a part of any number of co-parties against whom a judgment has been taken shall appeal from such judgment to the Supreme or Appellate Court, under the statute providing for term time appeals, it shall not be necessary to make such co-parties not appealing, parties to the appeal, nor name them in the assignment of errors, either as appellants or appellees, Rockey v. Hershman, 193 Ind. 168, 138 N. E. 339; and they shall be bound by the judgment on appeal as if they had been made such parties. Burns 1933, 2—3212; Burns 1926, 706, Baldwin, 476.

Co-parties means parties to the judgment and not parties plaintiff or defendant who are not parties to the judgment appealed from. Ward v. Yarmelle, 173 Ind. 

535-42, 91 N. E. 7. Co-parties are all those in whose favor a final judgment is rendered, or against whom it is rendered and one in whose favor a judgment is rendered is not a co-party with one against whom it is rendered, nor vice versa.

This applies only to term time appeals. Shuman v. 

Collis, 144 Ind. 333, 43 N. E. 257; and if a term time appeal is prayed and not perfected, it does not apply to it. Kellogg v. Ridgley, 40 App. 423, 81 N. E. 1158; nor does it apply to those who do not join in a term time appeal, but afterwards when the same is perfected they assign errors on the record as provided by the above section. Goodrich v. Stangland, 155 Ind. 279, 58 N. E. 148.

If a vacation appeal is taken, to either the Supreme or Appellate Court, a part of several co-parties may appeal, but they must serve written notice of the appeal upon all other co-parties, or their attorneys of record, and file proof of the same with the clerk of said court. If any of them are not residents of the state and have no attorney of record in the court below, or if such attorney

CO-PARTIES, HOW MADE PARTIES TO APPEAL 

cannot be served with such notice in the state, the court may order that notice of the appeal be given to such nonresident co-party by publication and when this is done they are bound by the decision as if they had been personally served with notice of the appeal. Unless they appear after such notice and decline to join in the appeal, they shall be regarded as having joined and shall be liable for costs. If they decline to join, their names may be stricken out and they shall not take an appeal afterwards, and shall not derive any benefit from the appeal, except persons under disabilities. Burns 1933, 2-3213; Burns 

1926, 707; Baldwin, 474. This section applies only to vacation appeals. The co-parties that the statute requires to be notified means parties to the judgment who would be affected by it, Lowe v. Turpie, 147 Ind. 652, 44 

N. E. 25; and not one who has disclaimed any interest in the matter in issue. Crane v. Hensler, 196 Ind. 341, 141 

N. E. 51. Parties who make default must be made parties to the appeal, Kaufman v. Preston, 158 Ind. 361-62, 63

N. E. 570; and so must parties to the record, though not to the judgment, if their interests will necessarily be affected. Trippeer v. Clifton, 178 Ind. 198, 97 N. E. 791.

The notice of the appeal must be in writing and must be given to all the other co-parties and unless they are so notified, the court has no jurisdiction over them and the cause must be dismissed, unless they enter their appearance, Prebster v. Henderson, 186 Ind. 21, 113 N. E. 241; and the failure to move to dismiss the appeal until after filing briefs on the merits will not prevent a dismissal for want of notice. Michigan, etc. v. Frankel, 151 Ind. 534, 50 N. E. 304. Notice to a co-party must be given and proof thereof filed within the time allowed for appeal, and leave will not be given to make one a party and serve notice after the time for appeal has passed. Brown v. 

Brown, 168 Ind. 654, 80 N. E. 535. Names in the notice to co-parties must correspond with their names mentioned in the judgment. Lilly v. Somerville, 142 Ind, 298, 40 N. E. 1088.
