TIME WITHIN WHICH APPEALS MAY BE TAKEN. 

All appeals are statutory and the time within which they may be taken is as follows:- 

1. From interlocutory orders thirty days. Burns 1933, 2-3218; Burns 1926, 712; Baldwin, 490; Burns 1933, 2-3219; Burns 1926, 713; Baldwin, 491. 

2. From the appointment of a receiver, ten days. Burns 

1933, 3-2603; Burns 1926, 1302; Baldwin, 1157. 

3. In decedents' estates one hundred twenty days. Burns 

1933, 9-1815; Burns 1926, 2311; Baldwin, 2298. 

4. In all other civil cases one hundred and eighty days or within that time from the time the disability is removed if the appellant is under disability. Burns 

1933, 2-3202; Burns 1926, 696; Baldwin, 472; Burns 

1933, 4—2510; Burns 1926, 1733; Baldwin, 1725.

SUBMISSION 

5. The state may in criminal cases of misdemeanor appeal within sixty days after the rendition of judgment, Burns 1933, 9-2305; Burns 1926, 2379; Baldwin, 2371; all appeals must be taken within one year after the judgment is rendered or in case a motion for a new trial is filed within one year after the ruling on such motion. Burns 1933, 9-2308; Burns 1926, 2382; Baldwin, 2372. 

6. In compensation cases an appeal may be taken if the award is made by the full board, within thirty days, Burns 1933, R'29, p. 568; Burns 1926, 9506; Baldwin, 6437. 

7. From the juvenile court to the appellate court within 

30 days from the date of the judgment. Burns 1933, 9-2818; Burns 1926, 1709; Baldwin, 1772. 

8. From the municipal court to the appellate court within 180 days, Burns 1933, 4-2510; Burns 1926, 1733; 

Baldwin, 1725.

SUBMISSION. 

Every appeal in civil cases shall be submitted at the expiration of 30 days from the date of filing the transcript and assignment of errors, except when it is necessary to issue and serve notice after such filing in order to perfect the appeal, in which case the cause shall be submitted at the expiration of 30 days from the date when such notice is served. Rule 14.

By agreement of the parties, a cause may be submitted immediately and the court for cause may order a case submitted at such time as it may direct and appeals from the Industrial Board of this state are submitted on the

SUBMISSION 

157 date of filing. Rule 14 and 15 and so in criminal cases 

Rule 17.

Submission means to refer it to or to place it before the court for determination and this cannot be done until all the parties are before the court, either by perfecting a term time appeal or a vacation appeal. Thompson v. 

A. J. Thompson Co., 81 App. 442, 144 N. E. 150. The right of appeal is given by statute, or it does not exist. 

In acquiring jurisdiction over a particular case this court does not exercise its inherent powers, but must exact a compliance with statutory provisions. Brown v. Brown, 168 Ind. 654. Since no presumption of jurisdiction attaches to Appellate tribunals, the burden rests upon the appellant to bring himself within a reasonable construction of the statute authorizing the appeal. Statutes limiting the time in which an appeal can be taken are jurisdictional and mandatory and the time cannot be extended by agreement of the parties and the failure of the appellee to move a dismissal will not confer jurisdiction and the court has power on its own motion to dismiss it. 

In a vacation appeal, the service of the notice and the filing of the proof are essential to the jurisdiction of the appellate court. It is a fundamental maxim in our jurisprudence that before any court can proceed to adjudicate upon any subject matter, it must first acquire jurisdiction over all persons whose rights will necessarily be affected by such adjudication. Thompson v. A. J. Thompson Co., 

81 App. 442, 144 N. E. 150. An appearance shall be entered in the clerk's office in writing. (Rule 8).

A joinder in error, or the filing of a pleading or brief upon the merits by the party in person, or by his attorney, or an agreement in writing to submit a cause or an unqualified written appearance filed in the clerk's office, shall be deemed a general appearance which shall not be withdrawn without leave of court. Rule 8. A special appearance shall be so designated, be in writing, and shall state the particular relief demanded, with the facts in support of the same. Rule 9. A joinder in error is a waiver of notice. Cleveland, etc. v. Smith, 177 Ind. 524, 97 N. E.

BRIEFS WHERE FILED 

164. Until a cause is submitted the court will not consider it on its merits but will consider it as to any question arising on jurisdiction. A joinder in error and agreement to submit waives defects in the certificate to the transcript, to the failure to number the pages of the same and the lines thereof, to make marginal notes thereon. Cooper v. Cooper, 86 Ind. 75; but a party does not waive any right by failure to file objections to the submission of the cause where the submission is by virtue of the rule of court and not by his doing anything which amounts to an agreement to submit. Elliott, § 427.

The appellee may object to a submission under the rule of court by filing written objections, verified and stating they are made in good faith, and then the clerk shall not enter a submission but the matter shall be referred to the court and the cause shall not be submitted until the court so orders. When a cause is submitted the clerk shall mail notice of the same to at least one attorney representing the appellant, as shown by the assignment of errors, and also to the clerk of the court from which the appeal is taken stating the date of the submission. Rule 16. The importance of the submission and notice of the same is found in the fact that certain rights in connection with the appeal must be exercised within a time fixed by the date of submission. BRIEFS. 

When Filed. The cause on appeal is presented by briefs. "The appellant shall have sixty days after submission in which to file his brief and if the brief is not

BRIEFS HOW PREPARED 

159 filed within the time limited, the clerk shall enter an order dismissing the appeal." Rule 19. The time is computed by excluding the date of submission and including the sixtieth day thereafter. Burns 1933, 2—4704; Burns 

1926, 903; Baldwin, 1211. Smith, Trustee v. Wells Mfg. 

Co., 144 Ind. 266. If the last day falls on Sunday, that day is excluded and the next is included. Burns 1933, 2-4704; Burns 1926, 903; Baldwin, 1211; but if the last day falls on any other day it is included, though it be a legal holiday. Board v. Tichener, 49 App. 221, 97 N. E. 

822. If one or more appellants file their briefs in time. but others do not, the cause will be dismissed as to those who did not file briefs, or who did not file them in time. 

Sourbier v. Brown, 188 Ind. 554–72, 123 N. E. 802.

Time for filing the brief by appellant may be extended by filing a petition to the court showing the date of submission of the cause, the date upon which the time for filing briefs will expire, and facts showing the court in which the cause is pending has jurisdiction and that the brief will be on the merits of the case, and reasons for asking the extension. The order of the Court will fix the date on or before which the brief must be filed. Five days' notice must be given the adverse party when an application is made for a re-extension of the time for filing the brief. No such notice is necessary for the first extension. When the time for filing appellant's brief has passed, the time will not be extended, unless he can show a justifiable excuse for not asking for an extension of time. Cline v. Gould, 17 App. 647, 47 N. E. 237. Rule 20. 

How Prepared. All briefs shall be printed or typewritten in a neat and workmanlike manner, on white opaque unglazed paper nine by seven inches, thick enough that the letters will not show through from the next page, leaving a margin of at least one inch at the left side, and at least three eighths of an inch on the right side, bound in book or pamphlet form, with a suitable cover containing the number and title of the cause and the court in which the case is pending. If typewritten, the lines shall be double spaced and on not less than twenty pound

WHAT APPELLANT'S BRIEF SHALL CONTAIN 

paper. Quotations may be single spaced. Not less than ten copies of each brief shall be filed with the clerk, three of which, if typewritten, shall be ribbon copies, one of which shall be for the opposite party, and no carbon copies will be received unless clearly legible. The fifth part of the statement required by Rule 21 may on leave of court be bound separately from the remainder of the brief. Rule 24.

WHAT APPELLANT'S BRIEF SHALL CONTAIN (RULE 21). 

"First. The nature of the action. Second. 

What the issues were. 

Third. How the issues were decided, and what the judgment or decree was. 

Fourth. The errors relied on for reversal.' 

These four provisions of the rule seem so clear that they can not be misunderstood, but as showing a compliance with them reference may be made to copy of brief hereafter set out.

The Fifth sub-division of the above rule includes many requirements and each will be considered separately and in the order in which they are stated in the rule. Each is imperative. (A) "A concise statement of so much of the record as fully presents every error and exception relied on, referring to the pages and lines of the transcript." 

It is uniformly held that if a party fails to comply with the requirements of the rules of court, or any of

WHAT APPELLANT'S BRIEF SHALL CONTAIN 161 

them, he waives the error, if any was committed, Tisdale v. State, 167 Ind. 85, 78 N. E. 325. The statement of the evidence must precede the propositions and points and the authorities cited to support them. Thomas v. Hennes, 78 App. 275, 135 N. E. 392. The line and page of the transcript showing the asserted error must be referred to. Chicago and Co. v. Lawrence, 169 Ind. 319, 79 N. E. 

363; McCrocklin v. State, 189 Ind. 254, 126 N. E. 678.

The statement of the record, except where the rule authorizes it to be set forth in narrative form, must be a quotation from the record. M. Rumley Co. v. Mayor, 64 App. 41, 115 N. E. 337. But it has been said that if the substance of the record is set forth that will be sufficient. Terryton v. Wilson, 160 Ind. 435, 67 N. E. 183; and in American, etc. v. Halstead, 165 Ind. 633, 76 N. E. 

251, this is said: "Appellant has assigned the rulings on demurrer as error. Its brief, however, contains no statement of the substance of the answers demurred to, further than this: That the fourth and sixth paragraphs are respectively based on claims of set-off for commissions on goods sold, that the fifth paragraph is a set off for work and labor performed and that the seventh paragraph is in the nature of an accord and satisfaction. 

Appellant's points in his brief contain no specific objection to any of said answers. It is merely stated therein that said paragraphs are insufficient. In the particulars mentioned, said brief in no wise complies with rule twentytwo, of this court. The brief is defective both in the omission to make a statement of the record sufficient to present the exception relied on and also in the failure to point out any definite objection to said answers. To state what the record is, is not to state what the opinion of counsel is concerning the record. Dillon v. State, 48 

App. 495, 96 N. E. 171; Jeffersonville v. School City, 50 

App. 178-82, 96 N. E. 662. 

It is evident the safe rule is to quote so much of the record as will present the error and exception relied on. 

The only part of the record which may not be quoted is that part which may be set forth in narrative form and

WHAT APPELLANT'S BRIEF SHALL CONTAIN 

the title of the case and signature of attorneys. If any assigned error is properly presented in the brief, it will be considered though other such errors are not presented in such a way that they will be considered. Perry and Co. v. Stone Co., 160 Ind. 435, 67 N. E. 183. Objections to the admission or exclusion of evidence will not be considered on appeal unless the question, objection, offer to prove, ruling of the court, exception thereto and answer are set forth in the brief with reference to the line and page of the record, where the same may be found. Kokomo, etc. v. Kokomo, etc., 193 Ind. 219, 137 N. E. 763; and so are objections to overruling a demurrer to a plea in abatement where neither the plea nor the demurrer are set out. 

Getz v. Clough, 82 App. 501, 133 N. E. 884; and so in refusing to strike out all interrogatories and answers thereto, if the complaint is not set out. Thread Mills 

Co. v. Hubbard, 85 App. 5, 152 N. E. 294; and so in overruling a motion for a new trial if the motion is not set out. United Paper Board Co. v. Muncie Steel, etc., 84 

App. 333, 151 N. E. 365; and so a motion to strike out a pleading, if the motion is not set out. Getz v. Clough, 82 App. 501, 133 N. E. 884; and so as to the sufficiency of an answer to a cross-complaint, if the answer is not set out. Hawkins v. First Nat. Bank, 81 App. 478, 143

N. E. 709; and so in overruling a demurrer to a reply and the demurrer and the reply are not set out. Allen v. 

Allen, 83 App. 20, 147 N. E. 310; and so a motion for continuance, if the motion is not set out. Miles v. Miller, 82 App. 665, 147 N. E. 281; and so a ruling on a demurrer if the memorandum is not set out. Doriot v. Haskell, etc., 79 App. 535, 137 N. E. 283; and so the law of another state, if it is not set out. Wilson v. Wilson, 83 App. 690, 149 

N. E. 732; and so as to damages where no part of the evidence relating to damages is set out. Jackson v. Atwood, 194 Ind. 56, 140 N. E. 549; and so as to any error in the admission of testimony if the testimony is not set out. Thurston v. Carter, 87 App. 518, 155 N. E. 834. (B) "If the insufficiency of the evidence to sustain the verdict or finding in fact or law is assigned, the statement

WHAT APPELLANT'S BRIEF SHALL CONTAIN 163 

shall contain a condensed recital of the evidence in narrative form, so as to present the substance clearly and concisely."

This portion of the rule does not require that the page and line of the transcript where the evidence is found, must be referred to, but there is every reason to think that such reference is necessary and that if no such reference is made the brief will be fatally defective, so far as presenting any question arising on the sufficiency of the same to sustain the verdict or finding in fact or law is concerned. It is believed the reference should be to the page showing the name of the witness, whether the examination is direct, cross, or re-examination and to the line and page of every material thing he testifies to, and if the evidence is documentary it should be to the line and page where the document begins. The reference should be so frequent that the court will not have any trouble in readily finding the particular testimony relied on. It is not necessary to set out all the evidence but only that part which bears upon the point which it is claimed is not sufficient to sustain the verdict or finding and if there is no such evidence it cannot be set out and it is sufficient in the brief to say so, and the court will take such a statement as true, unless the appellee shows it is not true, by quotations from the record. Simplex, etc. v. Western, etc., 173 Ind. 1, 88 N. E. 682. (C) "The statement will be taken to be accurate and sufficient for a full understanding of the questions for decision unless the opposite party in his brief shall make necessary corrections or additions."

The above portion of the rule seems to be clear and to place on the "opposite party" the duty to set forth in his brief the necessary corrections, but the decisions of the court have changed the meaning of the rule. It is held to be sufficient if a "good faith effort" is made to comply with the rule. Stamets v. Mitchenor, 165 Ind. 672, 75 

N. E. 579; and it seems that to be a good faith effort,

WHAT APPELLANT'S BRIEF SHALL CONTAIN 

there must be a substantial compliance with the rule. 

Howard v. Atkins, 167 Ind. 184, 78 N. E. 665; Swing v. 

Hill, 165 Ind. 411, 75 N. E. 658. If the brief shows there has not been such a compliance, it is only necessary for the "opposite party" to call attention to the fact, Dix v. 

Wilfred Coal Co., 76 App. 511, 132 N. E. 595; Town of 

Jasonville v. Humphreys, 170 Ind. 583, 84 N. E. 340. "The brief states as much of the pleadings as is necessary to advise us of their character, and to indicate clearly the basis and ground of the court's action with respect to the rulings of which appellant complains. This is sufficient." Houpt v. Dutton, 170 Ind. 69, 83 N. E. 634.

It is only necessary to state in the brief the material allegations of the pleading necessary to a full understanding of the question presented. Schreiber v. Woun, 164 Ind. 7-10, 72 N. E. 852; State v. Roberts, 166 Ind. 

585-9, 77 N. E. 1093; but where error is assigned on overruling a demurrer to the complaint, it is not sufficient in the brief to merely point out the pages of the transcript where the complaint may be found. Schreiber v. Woun, 164 Ind. 7, 72 N. E. 852. Appellant by failing to make necessary recitals of evidence in his brief, cannot compel the appellee to set out such recitals, and if the appellant asserts he has set out all the evidence on a certain point, but the appellee denies this and sets out some of it in his brief and makes reference to the page and line of the transcript where more may be found, the appellant's question depending on the evidence will not be considered. 

Crawfordsville, etc. v. Burke, 92 App. 558, 157 N. E. 6.

If the appellee in his brief supplies the defect in the appellant's brief, the question raised by the appellant will be considered. Parker v. Boyle, 178 Ind. 560, 99 

N. E. 986. If the appellee makes corrections or additions to the appellee's statement of the evidence, they will be considered as correct unless the appellant in his reply brief shows by the record they are not correct. 

Simplex v. Belting Co., 173 Ind. 1, 88 N. E. 682.

WHAT APPELLANT'S BRIEF SHALL CONTAIN 165 

2 (D) "When error is predicated on the giving or refusal of instructions the statement must recite the instructions given."

All the instructions given must be recited-quotedin the brief. Stone v. Travelers, etc., 84 App. 243, 149

N. E. 454; and the line and page of the transcript where each instruction is found must be stated. City of Indianapolis v. Barthel, 194 Ind. 273, 141 N. E. 339. To show that a refused instruction should have been given it is only necessary to set out in the brief some evidence in the record to which it is applicable. Indianapolis Tr. 

Co. v. Wiles, 174 Ind. 236-41, 91 N. E. 161; to show that it should not have been given assert there was no evidence to which it was applicable, and this statement will be taken to be true unless the appellee quotes from the record enough of the same to show the assertion is not true. Simplex, etc. v. Western, etc., 173 Ind. 1, 88 N. E. 682. 

The sixth subdivision of the above rule has been added to the last revision and as it contains several specific requirements, they will be considered separately. 

A. "The briefs (of the appellant) shall contain under the heading 'Propositions and Authorities' a copy of each assigned error relied on stating its number as designated in the original assignment of error, and in the case the error assigned is the overruling of the motion for a new trial, then the cause relied upon numbered as in the motion." 

If any assignment of error is not presented in the brief under "Propositions and Authorities" it will not be considered, Judy v. Woods, 51 App. 325, 99 N. E. 792; but if, under this head, the brief is sufficient as to some assignments and not as to others, the former will be considered and the latter will not, Perry v. Stone, 160 Ind. 435, 67 

N. E. 183. 

B. "Each of the foregoing assignments shall be separately considered by separately numbered propositions concisely stated without argument, supported by separately

WHAT APPELLANT'S BRIEF SHALL CONTAIN 

numbered or lettered points and authorities. Assigned errors not treated as herein directed shall be deemed as waived. No alleged error or point not contained in the statement of errors or points, shall be raised afterwards either by reply brief, or oral or printed argument, or by a petition for a re-hearing." 

If the brief does not contain any points and authorities upon the questions named, such questions will not be considered, Stewart v. Stewart, 175 Ind. 412, 94 N. E. 564; but if under the head of Points and Authorities it is stated no authority in point can be found, this is a compliance with the rule, Wilson v. National Fowler Bank, 47 App. 689, 95 N. E. 269. Propositions of law must be applied to the errors assigned, Crawfordsville Trust Co. v. Burke, 92 App. 558, 157 N. E. 6; and no question not presented under Points and Authorities in appellant's brief can be presented in argument, Locomotive, etc. v. 

Higgs, 79 App. 427, 135 N. E. 353; nor in reply brief, Rudolph v. Ayde, 84 App. 202, 149 N. E. 734. Any ground for a new trial stated in the motion for the same and not stated in the brief under Propositions and Points will not be considered, Doering v. Walters, 80 App. 194, 140 N. E. 

74; nor will the assignment of error that the court erred in its conclusions of law, if it is not supported in the brief by propositions and points, State v. Amer. Co., 79 App. 

88, 137 N. E. 338; nor objections to the admission or exclusion of evidence, if they are not so supported, Mansfield v. Hinkle, 81 App. 6, 139 N. E. 700. C. "In citing cases the names of the parties must be given, with the book.and page where reported. Indiana cases officially reported shall be cited from the book and page of the official report."

APPELLEE'S BRIEF, ETC. 

167 !

APPELLEE'S BRIEF, ETC. 

Rule 22. "Appellee, in his answer brief, may point out any omissions or inaccuracies in appellant's statement of the record. Where the appellee relies upon facts appearing in the record which are not recited in appellant's brief, to render harmless an alleged error committed by the trial court, he shall set them out in his brief. If the making of such corrections requires the appellee to set out parts of the record omitted by the appellant, which these rules require him to set out, the court may tax against appellant any costs incurred by appellee on account of such omission."

In considering the above see what is said under "C," supra.

If the appellee supplies the omissions in appellant's brief, he cures the defect, though he disclaims an intention so to do. Liberty Canning Co. v. Lippencott Co., 80 App. 184, 137 N. E. 283; but the appellant cannot fail to make necessary recitals of the evidence, or anything else necessary for him to make in his brief, and by so doing compel the appellee in his brief to supply the omission, and to show that the appellant has not set forth the record required by the rules, it is only necessary for the appellee to set forth in his brief some of the omitted record and refer to the line and page of the record where the other omitted parts may be found and by so doing he does not supply the omission and cure it, Crawfordsville Trust Co. v. Burke, 92 App. 558, 157 N. E. 6. If the appellee fails to file a brief this is a confession of error, if the appellant has shown prima facie reversible error. 

Pittsburg & Co. v. Linder, 195 Ind. 569, 145 N. E. 885.

If a brief assumes to set out the evidence and such statement is not controverted by opposing litigant, such statement is accepted as true. Bryan v. Reiff, 192 Ind. 264, 135 N. E. 886; but if the record shows the statements in the brief are not true they will not be accepted as true, 1

APPELLEE'S BRIEF, ETC. 

though not controverted, and the cause will not be reversed on that statement, State v. Whitsel, 197 Ind. 278, 149 N. E. 369. "Following such statement, if any, the brief shall contain a short and clear statement of the propositions by which counsel seek to meet the alleged errors, and to sustain the judgment, or decree, or by which such errors are obviated, and specifically apply them to the propositions presented by the appellant in the same order that they were presented by appellant. Under each of such propositions shall be grouped the points of law and authorities ruled on to support it, in like manner as required in the appellant's brief." 

This new requirement is specifically stated and must be observed. The pointing out of omissions and inaccuracies in appellant's brief, and setting out parts of the record is the first thing the appellee is required to do in his brief. Then he shall consider each proposition stated in the appellant's brief and refer to it by the number given it by appellant, and under it state such proposition, or propositions, followed by points and authorities, as he urges to meet such alleged errors and such propositions as he thus states must be numbered or lettered. Reply briefs must be prepared substantially as answer briefs-that is the propositions in the answer brief must be referred to by their appropriate number and be considered by separately numbered propositions, followed by points and authorities. Any such proposition presented by appellant, not considered by the appellee, probably will be regarded as conceded or waived, Indianapolis, etc. v. Hensley, 186 Ind. 479, 115 N. E. 934.

The brief, answer brief and reply briefs on cross-errors shall be prepared as the briefs of appellant and appellee in the main action, and reply briefs shall be prepared substantially as answer briefs. Rule 22. Thompson v. 

Hennes, 78 App. 275, 135 N. E. 392. The names of counsel must be affixed to all briefs filed by them, and may be typewritten or printed. The brief may be supported by argument which shall be distinct from the brief, but

RULES CONCERNING PREPARATION OF BRIEFS 169 

bound with it and be confined to a discussion of the points in the brief. Rule 23.

The above rules concerning the preparation of briefs as stated and judicially interpreted are so clear that if any attorney fails to prepare his brief in accordance with them, and for that reason the Court fails to pass upon the questions he desires to present to it, he will have no one to blame but himself. Every attorney who prepares a brief must remember that the rules of Court have the force and effect of law binding alike on litigant and on the Court and cannot be disregarded in particular instances. Epstein v. State, 190 Ind. 693, 127 N. E. 441; 

Griffith v. Feltz, 52 Ind. App. 268, 99 N. E. 432; Magnuson v. Billings, 152 Ind. 177, 52 N. E. 803.

RULES CONCERNING PREPARATION OF BRIEFS. 

The following principles are invariably applicable to the preparation of briefs: 

1. On appeal the cause is tried by the record. Mitchell v. Stinson, 80 Ind. 324. 

2. If the court has jurisdiction of the parties and the subject matter, no cause will be reversed unless the record shows an exception by the appealing party. 

Robinson v. State, 185 Ind. 119, 113 N. E. 306. 

3. A cause will not be reversed for the purpose of assessing nominal damages. Smith v. Parker, 148 

Ind. 127, 45 N. E. 770. 

4. The court presumes there is no error in the record and before the cause will be reversed the appealing party must by the record show there is error in the

RULES CONCERNING PREPARATION OF BRIEFS 

1 proceedings which injured him. Jackson v. State, 194 Ind. 130, 142 N. E. 1. 

5. The court will not search the record to reverse a case but will search the record to affirm it. McCowen v. Short, 69 App. 466. 

6. Rules of court are the law concerning the matters to which they apply and are binding on both litigants and the court. Griffith v. Felts, 52 App. 268, 99 

N. E. 432. 

7. Briefs must be prepared to conform to rules of court. Robinson v. State, 185 Ind. 119, 113 N. E. 306. 

8. There must be set forth in the briefs all of that part of the record which shows the error complained of and in the order and manner prescribed in those rules. Robinson v. State, 185 Ind. 119, 113 N. E. 306. 

9. The briefs must be prepared in such a way that the court will not have to refer to the record to understand the questions at issue and to decide the same, but where the record is set forth the page and line of the record must be referred to so that the court can readily refer to the record. Bedford Quarries 

Co. v. Chicago, etc. Co., 175 Ind. 303, 94 N. E. 326; 

Robinson v. State, 185 Ind. 119, 113 N. E. 306. 

10. No alleged error or point not contained in the statement of errors or points shall be raised afterwards either by reply brief or oral or printed argument, or by a petition for a rehearing. Rule 21, subd. 6.

The following is a copy of a brief filed in the Court on appeal in behalf of appellant which met the criticism of opposing counsel and received the approval of the court, under the rules of court then in force and before the rules in force November 15th, 1933, and which it is believed complies with the rules now in force. It may assist in getting a correct understanding of the rules.

RECORD. 

HISTORY OF THE CASE. 

This suit was commenced in the Monroe Circuit Court. The venue was changed to the Owen Circuit Court. There the cause was tried by a jury and a verdict returned for the plaintiff in the sum of Six Hundred Fifty Dollars ($650.00). A motion for a new trial was sustained. A change of venue was taken from the judge. A special judge was appointed, the cause was again tried and the jury returned a verdict for the plaintiff in the sum of One Thousand Dollars ($1,000.00). A motion for a new trial was sustained and the judge who tried the case the second time declined to try it again. Another judge was selected and the cause was tried a third time.

STATEMENT REQUIRED BY RULE 22. 

First. The plaintiff brought this suit to recover from the defendant pay for service she alleged she rendered for the defendant, with its knowledge and consent and at its request. 

Second. The complaint was in one paragraph. To this, the defendant filed a motion asking that the same be made more specific, and also filed a demurrer for want of facts, and an answer in three paragraphs, the first of which was a general denial; the second, a plea of payment; and the third, a plea of the Six Year Statute of Limitations. To the second and third paragraphs of answer the plaintiff filed a reply in general denial.

APPELLANT'S BRIEF 

1 

Third. The motion to make the complaint more specific was overruled, with exception. The demurrer to the complaint was overruled, and exception taken. The jury returned a verdict for the plaintiff in the sum of Two Thousand, Two Hundred 

Twenty Dollars ($2,220.00), and judgment was rendered on this verdict. A motion for a new trial was filed by the defendant and overruled. 

Fourth. The errors assigned in this court are: (1) The court erred in overruling the defendant's motion to make the complaint more specific. (2) The court erred in overruling the defendant's demurrer to the complaint. (3) The court erred in overruling the defendant's motion for a new trial.

The last two of these assigned errors are relied upon for reversal.

PROPOSITIONS, POINTS AND AUTHORITIES. 

ASSIGNED ERROR NO. 2 RELIED ON. 

The Court erred in overruling the defendant's demurrer to the complaint. (B. p. 3)

PROPOSITION A. 

In suing on an account for labor performed at the instance and request of another, the complaint must state that the amount sued for is due and unpaid. If it does not so state, the complaint is bad, and if the defect is pointed out by the demurrer and the memorandum attached thereto, and the Court overrules the demurrer and an exception is taken to the ruling of the Court, the cause must be reversed on appeal, if the ruling of the Court and the demurrer is properly presented. The error is not cured by verdict or amendment.

POINTS AND AUTHORITIES. 

(1) In suing on an account for labor performed at the instance and request of another, to make the complaint good it must be alleged therein that the amount sued for is due, and also that it is unpaid. 

Goodman v. Gordon, 87 Ind. 126–128. 

Johnson v. Tyler, 1 App. 387, 388. 

Jaqua v. Showalter, 10 App. 234. (2) The appellant filed a demurrer to the complaint. That demurrer is found on page 5 of this brief. It stated that the "Complaint does not state facts sufficient to constitute a cause of action against the defendant."

APPELLANT'S BRIEF 

A memorandum was filed with it. The second specification of that memorandum is, "The complaint does not show that there is any amount due and owing by the defendant to the plaintiff.”’ 

The fifth specification is, "The complaint does not show that there is any amount unpaid." 

The record shows, "The Court now overrules said demurrer, to which ruling of the Court the Defendant at the time excepts." 

(3) Nowhere is there found in the complaint in this case the statement either that the amount sued for is due or that the same is unpaid. (B. p. 4) (B. p. 3) (4) The ruling of the Court on the demurrer is properly assigned as error in this case. (5) The error in overruling the demurrer to the complaint is not cured by the verdict or by amendment. 

Sheffer v. Hines, 149 Ind. 413. 

Mansur v. Streight, 103 Ind. 358. 

Johnson v. Breedlove, 72 Ind. 368–374.

May v. The State Bank, 9 Ind. 233. 

For the reasons herein given, this cause must be reversed. 

ASSIGNED ERROR NO. 3 relied on.

The Court erred in overruling the defendant's motion for a new trial. (B. p. 3) 

PROPOSITION A. 

The complaint lacked two essential averments. It was neither alleged in the complaint the money for the services alleged to have been rendered, was due or that it was unpaid. As the complaint was fatally defective because of the absence of these averments, it was error for the Court to tell the jury the plaintiff should recover if the material averments of the complaint were proven by a preponderance of the evidence. The Court did so tell the jury in three instructions given by the Court at 773

PROPOSITIONS, POINTS AND AUTHORITIES 

183 the plaintiff's request, and to the giving of each of which instructions the defendant did at the time except.

POINTS AND AUTHORITIES. 

(1) To authorize a recovery by the plaintiff he must state a cause of action in his complaint and if he does not do so, there should be no recovery by him although every material averment in the complaint is proven. Burns 1914, Sec. 344, Sub. 

Div. 5; Palmer v. Hayes, 112 Ind. 289. (2) In Instruction No. 1 given by the Court at the request of the plaintiff, (p. 26 of this brief), the Court told the jury in substance that the plaintiff was entitled to recover if she proved all the material allegations of her complaint by a fair preponderance of the evidence; and in Instruction No. 7 given by the 

Court at the request of the plaintiff (found on p. 29 of this brief), the Court told the jury: "If you find from a preponderance of the evidence there was such an implied contract as I have defined, then the plaintiff will have a right to expect such compensation. In the absence of an agreement as to the amount, she will be entitled to recover the reasonable value of such services so performed." 

And the Court said substantially the same thing in Instruction No. 5, p. 28 of this brief.

PROPOSITION B. 

(Here state proposition, followed by

PROPOSITION C. 

points and authorities.) (Here state proposition, followed by points and authorities.) Argument.

(Here insert argument.) 

R. C. & R. 

Attorneys for Appellant

The appellee should identify each proposition set forth by appellant in support of his assignment of error, either by referring to the number of it or by setting forth the number and proposition in his, the appellee's brief. 

Under each of such propositions the appellee shall state and designate, by number the propositions on which he relies to meet the appellant's proposition and under each of said propositions so set forth by the appellee he shall state and designate by number or letter the legal points and with them state the authorities upon which he relies to meet the contention of the appellant. 

Each assignment of error considered by the appellant in his brief shall in the order in which it is considered by the appellant be so considered, as above stated by the appellee in his brief.

Any assignment of error or proposition stated by the appellant and not so met by the appellee will be regarded as waived by the appellee. This brief shall be signed by counsel for the appellee.

Appellant's reply brief shall contain the title of the cause, etc., as set forth in his original brief.

APPELLANT'S REPLY BRIEF 

stated by the appellee under each assignment of error considered by the appellee and in the order in which it is so considered, referring to it by the number or letter it bears and the page of the appellee's brief where it will be found. Under each of these propositions he shall state propositions upon which he relies to meet the appellee's propositions, numbering such proposition and under each such proposition the appellant shall set forth the legal points numbering or lettering them, upon which he relies to support the proposition and following such points shall be stated the authorities relied upon. 

The propositions stated in appellee's brief which are controverted by the appellant shall be stated in the reply brief in the order in which they are stated and numbered in appellee's brief.

Any proposition stated by the appellee and not so considered by the appellant is regarded as waived. This reply brief shall be signed by counsel for the appellant. 

Though the above brief prepared by appellant was prepared before the present rules were in force, it may be referred to in preparing briefs under the present rules and it is hoped it will assist and not hinder those who prepare briefs under the rules now in force. 

It is also hoped that the statement of the manner in which the answer brief and the reply brief may be prepared, is clear and when considered in the light of what has heretofore been said concerning the preparation of briefs and the way said appellant's brief was prepared will assist any careful lawyer in preparing his brief in harmony with the rules of Court on that subject.

AMENDMENT OF BRIEFS-REHEARING

AMENDMENT OF BRIEFS. 

"Upon notice to the adverse party and on presentation of a verified application setting forth the proposed amendment and the reasons on which it is based, the court in its discretion, may grant leave to amend briefs upon such terms as it shall prescribe. Rule 25."

Errors presented for the first time in an amended brief, will not be considered and all errors not challenged by appellant's original brief are waived and cannot be presented by an amended brief. Chicago, etc. Co. v. Walas, 192 Ind. 369.

PETITION FOR LEAVE TO AMEND BRIEFS.

And the reason why he asks leave to make such amendment is that in preparing it he did not comply with Rule of this court, or any other reason. 

A. B. Appellant by his Attys. C. D.

REHEARING. 

"Application for a rehearing of any cause shall be made by petition, separate from the briefs, signed by counsel,

REHEARING 

filed with the clerk within sixty days from the rendition of the judgment, stating concisely the cause for which the judgment is supposed to be erroneous which application shall be supported by briefs only, with argument set out therein, if desired. Ten copies of the brief must be filed at the same time the petition is filed." Rule 29; S. 1362; 

4-220-Bald, 1383.

A petition for a rehearing will be denied, if the question sought to be raised by it was not before presented to the court for consideration. Postal v. Postal, 192 Ind. 

376, 134 N. E. 882. A ground for a rehearing unsupported by argument or authority is waived. City of Bedford v. Neal, 143 Ind. 425, 41 N. E. 1029; and if the time for filing a petition therefor has expired the time will not be extended except where the filing was prevented by fraud of the opposite party, or unavoidable accident and the petitioner is without fault. Gilchrist v. Cotton, 83 App. 415, 148 N. E. 435. A petition for rehearing will be stricken from the files if it is not supported by brief, but if the statute and rules of court are complied with, the court is bound to review the same. Harmon v. 

Smitch, 86 App. 527, 157 N. E. 284. A petition for a rehearing must point out specifically the errors relied on and notice of the petition need not be given to the adverse party. Hanley v. Mason, 42 App. 312, 85 N. E. 

381. A second application cannot be filed. Marion Light 

Co. v. Vermillion, 51 App. 677, 99 N. E. 55. If the court on appeal fails to decide any question presented, the remedy is by petition for a rehearing. Stevens v. Templeton, 174 Ind. 129, 91 N. E. 563. Though the court is not bound to consider questions presented for the first time, even if by an appellee, it may do so to affirm a judgment. 

Alsman v. Walters, 184 Ind. 565, 106 N. E. 879. The record in a cause cannot be amended on a petition for a rehearing. Smith v. Gustin, 169 Ind. 42, 80 N. E. 959.

The petition must be filed within the sixty days, Ft. 

Wayne, etc. v. Scott, 75 App. 266, 130 N. E. 440; and should relate only to points that were properly presented

REHEARING-PETITION FOR 

191 for decision at the first hearing and were overlooked, or were not properly decided. Kilgallen v. State, 192 Ind. 

531, 132 N. E. 682; nor can the petitioner present any question which he has waived. Shera v. Woody, 82 App. 

367, 143 N. E. 607; no notice of the same need be given to the adverse party. Hardy v. Mason, 42 App. 312, 85 

N. E. 381; nor can a new point be presented, Cleveland, C., C. & St. L. R. Co. v. Lynn, 171 Ind. 589, 85 N. E. 999, 86 N. E. 1017. 

The following is a proper form:

Caption 

Petition for a Rehearing

In the above entitled cause the appellant (or appellee) petitions the Court for a rehearing of the same for each of the following reasons: 

1. (Here set out the reason, by stating the decision of the court and wherein it is wrong.) 

2. (Here set out the reason as above.) 

3. (Here set out the reason as above.) 

A. B. 

Atty. for Petitioner.

There is no law or rule of court authorizing the party not filing the petition to file an answer to it in any way. 

The petition for a rehearing must state specifically the reasons why the rehearing should be granted and each should be numbered. These really are an assignment of errors and the brief in support of them should be prepared as the brief in support of the assignment of errors made on the transcript on appeal.

PETITION TO TRANSFER TO SUPREME COURT 

444 

PETITION TO TRANSFER TO SUPREME COURT. 

An application to transfer a cause from the Appellate Court to the Supreme Court, after a petition for rehearing has been overruled, shall set forth (1) that the Appellate Court has decided the case, or some material part thereof, against the party who asks that it be transferred, (2) that a written opinion setting forth the reasons on which such judgment was based was filed by the Appellate Court, giving the date when it was filed, (3) that the Appellate Court overruled a petition for rehearing duly presented by such party within the time allowed, stating on what date it was overruled; and having thus shown the jurisdiction, the petition (4) shall particularly and specifically and without argument point out what there is in the opinion of the Appellate Court which contravenes a ruling precedent of the Supreme Court, or which erroneously decides a new question of law, indicating the ruling precedent, or concisely stating the new question of law, referred to. Such petition (5) shall be signed by the petitioner or by his attorney. (6) If briefs are filed, they shall be separate from and filed with the petition; but they shall not be necessary to invoke the action of the court on the petition. Parties opposing a transfer may file briefs within 40 days after the ruling on the petition for rehearing. Burns 1933, 4-215; Burns 

1926, 1357; Baldwin, 1359, Rule 2912.

In passing upon an application to transfer, the petition must state the jurisdictional facts which are (1) that the Appellate Court has decided the case, or some material part thereof, against the party who asks that the case be transferred, and has overruled a petition for a rehearing duly presented by such party; (2) that a written opinion was filed by the Appellate Court setting forth the reasons on which its judgment is based; (3) that such written opinion contravenes a ruling precedent of this

PETITION TO TRANSFER TO SUPREME COURT 193 

(the Supreme Court), or erroneously decides a new question of law. Either or both of such questions if desired to be presented must be particularly pointed out, and (4) that the petition for a rehearing and the application to transfer were each seasonably presented in accordance with the statutory direction. The application must be filed within thirty days after the petition for a rehearing is overruled and in deciding whether or not the case will be considered the court cannot look to the record but looks only to the petition. In re Aurora Gas Light, etc., Co., 186 Ind. 690, 115 N. E. 673. In considering the merits of the application the court can look only to the written opinion of the Appellate Court. City of Huntington v. Lusch, 163 Ind. 266, 71 N. E. 647; and if there is no such opinion there will be no transfer, Craig v. Bennett, 158 Ind. 9, 62 N. E. 273; nor can a case commenced in the Appellate Court be transferred, State v. Deupree, 169 Ind. 279, 82 N. E. 452. If a transfer is sought because the decision of the Appellate Court contravenes a ruling precedent of the Supreme Court, or that a new question of law is directly involved, and was decided erroneously, the precedent disregarded must be stated, or such new principle of law must be particularly set out, American Quarries Co. v. Lay, 166 Ind. 234, 76 N. E. 517.

There is no law or rule of court which requires the petitioner to file a brief in support of this petition but he may do so if it is filed within the time allowed for filing the petition. Nor is there any law or rule of Court requiring the party opposing the petition to file a brief in answer to the petition but such may be done if done within 40 days from the time of overruling the petition for a rehearing. Nor is there any law or rule of Court which requires or authorizes the party petitioning to file a reply brief, but it is presumed he may do so and if he does it will be considered. If the petition fails to show any essential thing required by the statute or rule of Court, it will not be considered.

PETITION TO TRANSFER TO SUPREME COURT 

The reasons stated why the case should be transferred, really constitute an assignment of errors and any briefs filed in support of or in opposition to such petition should be prepared as briefs are in support of or in opposition to the assignment of errors on appeal. 

See Acts 1933 page 800-A $50.00 deposit required with petition to transfer.

PETITION TO TRANSFER. 

The following is a copy of a petition by the appellant to transfer a case from the Appellate Court to the Supreme Court because the decision of the Appellate Court contravened ruling precedents of the Supreme Court, and also presented a new question of law. The case was transferred.

In the Supreme Court of the State of Indiana, November 

Term 1931. 

A, B, C, D, E & F, V.

G, Appellants, Appellee.

Petition by appellants to transfer said cause, numbered in the Appellate Court 14420 to this court and to reverse the same and brief in support thereof. 

The appellants in the above entitled cause show the court (1) that the judgment of the Superior Court of Marion County, Indiana, in this cause was affirmed by said Appellate Court 

Oct. 29, 1931; (2) that a written opinion of said court was then

PETITION TO TRANSFER TO SUPREME COURT 195 

filed, setting forth the reasons on which its judgment is based; (3) that a petition for a rehearing was filed by the appellants in said cause, December 26, 1931; (4) that the appellants were the losing parties in both said Superior and said Appellate Court; (5) that said petition was overruled by said Appellate 

Court on the 25th day of February, 1932; (6) that said opinion contravenes ruling precedents of this Court in each of the following respects:

I. 

The opinion shows that the appellee filed her complaint against the appellants in two paragraphs--one to quiet her title to three-eighths (%) of the real estate in controversy, and the other to partition it. To this the appellants filed a general denial, and the cause was tried on the issue thus made; and the 

Court rendered a judgment and decree quieting appellee's title to three-eighths (%) of said real estate; also that the real estate could not be partitioned and ordered the same sold. 

The opinion also shows that the appellee and H were married September 15, 1923, and they lived together as husband and wife until he died March 24, 1929, and that a guardian was appointed for him June 24, 1924; also that the appellants on the trial of said cause offered to prove that said H was insane at the time of the marriage. This the Court, over objection of counsel for appellee refused to allow, unless it was or would be shown that H had been judicially declared insane before the marriage. There was no such showing. To this ruling of the 

Court the appellants excepted. 

This ruling of the trial court the Appellate Court in its opinion affirms and decides to be correct, and such opinion contravenes a ruling precedent of the Supreme Court as stated in the opinions in the following cases: 

Freed v. Brown, 55 Ind. 310; Northwestern, etc., v. Blankenship, 94 Ind. 535; Heavenridge v. Monday, 34 Ind. 28; Jeffersonville Water Sup. Co. v. Ritter, 146 Ind. 521; Gwinnup v. Shies, 161 Ind. 500; East v. Peden, 108 Ind. 92; Graham v. Graham, 55 Ind. 23; Chicago, etc., v. Grantham, 165 Ind. 279; Kaufman v. Preston, 158 Ind. 361.

PETITION TO TRANSFER TO SUPREME COURT 

II. 

The opinion also says, in the second paragraph of affirmative answer it was alleged: "That A was the father of H, deceased, and that the other appellants were his brothers and sisters; that he died intestate and that they were his only heirs at law; that said deceased H was insane and while insane, the appellee took him from a hospital where he was being treated for his mental disease, and took him to Greenfield, Hancock County, Indiana, where she procured a marriage license to be issued to her and to him and caused a marriage ceremony to be performed; that she ever afterward claimed to be the wife of said H and that her claimed interest in said real estate depended solely upon her being the lawful wife of said decedent; that she had no interest in said real estate and that they were the owners of the same" and it asked that the said pretended marriage be declared void. To this paragraph of answer a demurrer was sustained and exception taken by the appellants. 

With reference to this paragraph, the opinion also says: "There is an absence of sufficient allegations in it that would amount to fraud upon the part of the appellee in the matter of the marriage between herself and the deceased. The evidence shows the marriage was consummated according to the forms of law, a license was procured and a minister performed the marriage ceremony. In such case, the presumption in favor of its validity is one of the strongest known to the law." Also: "If we hold the second paragraph of said affirmative answer constitutes a direct attack (which we do not decide), yet if it be a direct attack it is wholly insufficient to withstand the demurrer filed to it. This affirmative paragraph of counterclaim was evidently filed in an attempt to bring the case within the language used in Bruns v. Cope, 182 Ind. 289–95, wherein it is said: 'It is not claimed that either paragraph under consideration was drawn on the theory of an equitable proceeding to procure a decree declaring a marriage void ab initio.' We have examined the pleading in question in this case and we conclude that it lacks many of the elements necessary to withstand the demurrer addressed to it. If we are correct in holding that this 772

PETITION TO TRANSFER TO SUPREME COURT 197 

second paragraph of affirmative answer, considered as a pleading to have the said marriage declared void ab initio, is insufficient to withstand the demurrer addressed to it, then the case falls within the rule announced in the Bruns v. Cope case, supra. 

In that case it was held that the validity of a marriage may not be collaterally attacked in a statutory action by heirs for the partition of land of an alleged insane decedent, but that any relief must result from a direct attack in an appropriate equitable proceeding."

The opinion of said Division of said Appellate Court, as hereinabove set out, contravenes a ruling precedent of the Supreme Court in each of the following respects, as stated in the opinions in the cases following each of them: (1) The opinion holds and decides that said second paragraph of affirmative answer is a collateral attack upon the marriage. This holding and decision is erroneous and such opinion contravenes a ruling precedent of the Supreme Court, as stated in the opinions in each of the following cases: 

Freed v. Brown, 55 Ind. 310; Castor v. Davis, 120 Ind. 231; 

Bruns v. Cope, 182 Ind. 289; Wiley v. Wiley, 75 Ind. App. 456; 

Henneger v. Lomas, 145 Ind. 287; Union Trust Co. v. Curtis, 182 Ind. 61. (2) The opinion above set forth holds and decides that said second paragraph of affirmative answer is insufficient because there is no allegation in it that would amount to fraud upon the part of the appellee in the matter of the marriage between herself and the decedent, and the presumption in favor of its validity is one of the strongest known to the law. This holding and decision is erroneous and such opinion contravenes ruling precedent of the Supreme Court as stated in the opinions in each of the following cases: 

Bank v. Coquillard, 6 Ind. 232; Cain v. Goda, 84 Ind. 209; 

City of Indianapolis v. Wann, 144 Ind. 175; Pittsburgh v. 

Montgomery, 52 Ind. 1-20-21; Sare v. Butcher, 141 Ind. 146-47; 

Board v. Co., 88 Ind. 199; Sinclair v. Sunzenhauser, 179 Ind. 

78, 138. (3) The opinion as above set forth holds and decides that said second paragraph of affirmative answer constitutes a collateral attack on the marriage by heirs for the partition of land

PETITION TO TRANSFER TO SUPREME COURT 

of an insane decedent and that it is not a direct attack in an appropriate equitable proceeding to have the marriage declared void. This holding and decision is erroneous, and such opinion contravenes ruling precedent of the Supreme Court, as found in each of the following cases: 

See authorities under (1) and (2) above.

III. 

The opinion of the Appellate Court also says: "We believe the Court correctly overruled the demurrers to both affirmative paragraphs of answer and that no error was made in the rulings on the evidence. We find no reversible error. 

The statutes pertinent to this appeal are sections 9859, 9862, and 9864 of Burns Ann. St. 1926. They are as follows: Burns 

1933, 44-101, Burns 1926, 9859, Baldwin, 5618 Supra: "Marriage is declared to be a civil contract, into which males of the age of eighteen and females of the age of sixteen, not nearer of kin than second cousins, or not having a husband or wife living, are capable of entering." 

Burns 1933, 44-104, Burns 1926, 9862, Baldwin, 5619 Supra: "The following marriages are declared void:

First: When either party had a wife or husband living at the time of such marriage; second, when one of the parties is a white person and the other possessed of one-eighth or more negro blood; third, when either party is insane or idiotic at the time of such marriage." 

Burns 1933, 44-105; Burns 1926, 9863; Baldwin, 897 Supra: "All marriages prohibited by law, on account of consanguinity, affinity, difference of color, or where either party has a former wife or husband living, if solemnized within this state, shall be absolutely void without any legal proceedings." 

Burns 1933, 44-106, Burns 1926, 9864, Baldwin, 898 Supra: "When either of the parties to a marriage shall be incapable from want of age or understanding, of contracting such marriage, the same may be declared void on application of the incapable party by any court having jurisdiction to decree divorces; but the children of such marriage, begotten before the same is annulled, shall be legitimate; and in such cases the same

PETITION TO TRANSFER TO SUPREME COURT 199 

proceedings shall be had as provided in applications for divorce."

The decision and opinion as above set out holds that said 

Burns 1933, 43-105, Burns 1926, 9862, Baldwin, 10540, which declares the marriage of an insane person at the time of such marriage void, is governed by said sections, Burns 1926, 9863, Baldwin, 897, Burns 1933, 44-106, Burns 1926, 9864,

Baldwin, 898, and that the marriage of a person insane at the time of the marriage cannot be shown in a judicial proceeding to have the marriage declared void, unless it is also shown that the insane person had been judicially declared insane before the time of the marriage. Such opinion contravenes a ruling precedent of the Supreme Court, as stated in the cases heretofore cited in this petition, and especially in the case of Wiley v. Wiley, 75 Ind. App. 456, in which these sections were fully considered by the Appellate Court and decided adversely to the decision of the Appellate Court as found in the opinion above set forth, and which decision and opinion of the Appellate Court this Court affirmed by refusing to transfer it, and reverse the cause.

IV. 

The opinion of the Appellate Court as above set out presents a new question of law, and the same is duly discussed in briefs of appellants filed in said cause; which new question of law is this: If a woman marries a man insane at the time of the marriage, can the validity of that marriage be raised in any judicial proceeding after his death unless it is shown that he was judicially declared insane before the time of the marriage? Such question has never been decided in an appeal taken to the Supreme Court in this state in which it was presented and in which an opinion of the Supreme Court was written. Such question of law was decided erroneously by said Division of said Court in this, that it decided that such question could not be so raised unless it was shown that he had been judicially declared insane before the time of the marriage. This decision is contrary to the following authorities: 

Burns 1933, 44-104, Burns 1926, 9862, Baldwin, 5619; Bank v. Coquillard, 6 Ind. 232; Cain v. Coda, 84 Ind. 209; City of 

Indianapolis v. Wann, 144 Ind. 175; Pittsburgh v. Montgomery,

t 

CERTIORARI 

52 Ind. 1-20-21; Sare v. Butcher, 141 Ind. 146-47; Cass County 

Board Com'rs v. Logansport & R. C. G. R. Co., 88 Ind. 199; Sinclair v. Gunzenhauser, 179 Ind. 78-138; Wiley v. Wiley, 75 

Ind. App. 456.

We respectfully ask that this case be transferred and reversed. 

Attys. for Petitioner.

CERTIORARI. 

If after the transcript has been filed in the office of the Clerk of the Supreme Court, it is found to be incorrect, either because it does not contain what should be there, or because it contains that which should not be there, or because it has been mutilated, on petition by either appellant, or appellee, to the Court in which the cause is pending stating such defect and asking that court to order the Clerk of the court from which the appeal is taken to correct the transcript so that it will be a true copy of the record made in that case, such an order will be made. 

The following form of petition may be used:

In the Supreme Court (or the Appellate Court) State of Indiana, May (or November) Term 1933. 

A. Appellant, V. 

B. Appellee. No.

In the above entitled cause the appellant makes known to the Court that judgment was rendered against him in favor of the

CERTIORARI 

201 appellee in the trial Court on the 10th day of March 1933 in the sum of $1,000.00; that said judgment was rendered after a motion for a new trial was overruled on said day; that on rendering such judgment the appellant prayed an appeal to the Supreme Court of the State of Indiana; that the Court then fixed the amount of the bond that should be given by the appellant and the surety on the same and the time within which it should be filed; that said bond was executed and filed as directed by the Court and the transcript in said cause was filed in this court within 10 days after the filing of said bond, and the appellant also makes known to the Court that said transcript in said cause as filed in the office of the Clerk of said Court is not correct in each of the following respects, viz: 

1. On page 12 of said transcript, beginning in line 10 are found the following words (here set them out). Said words are not in that portion of the record which the transcript of that page of the same sets out. 

2. On page 15 of said transcript there is not found the following words (here set out the omitted words). Said words are found in the record of which the transcript in said cause purports to be a correct copy and they should be set out in it and follow the word (here name it) found on said page in line 20. 

3. On page 20 of said transcript beginning in line 10 following the word "defendant" certain portions of the same followed by the word "decedent" have been mutilated so they cannot be read. 

4. The record in said cause has been corrected by a nunc pro tunc entry and no part of such correction appears in said transcript.

Wherefore the appellant asks this court to direct the clerk of the Court making said transcript, to correct it in each of said respects and properly to certify such correction to this court. 

Respectfully requested, A. B. Attorney for Petitioner. 

Subscribed and sworn to this the 

Rules 10, 11, 12. day of May 

C. D., Notary Public.

ERROR-HOW WAIVED 

If the petition is filed before submission notice to the opposite party is not necessary, but if filed after submission 10 days' notice in writing must be given him of the time when the petition will be presented and the notice must be accompanied by a copy of the petition and of the petitioner's brief thereon and proof of the service of such notice and copy and brief shall be filed in the clerk's office within ten days. The party upon whom the notice is served has ten days unless otherwise ordered by the court, from the date of service of the notice in which to file such papers and briefs as he may desire. 

The petition will be decided by the court on the papers and briefs filed. Rule 13.

A certiorari is not necessary if improper matters are included in the transcript and this is shown by a mere inspection of the record. The duty of obtaining a certiorari rests upon counsel, and not upon the court, though the court may on its own motion order a correction of the transcript, but counsel has no right to ask or rely on the court in the matter. Nor will the court of its own motion order a certiorari for the purpose of reversing a case but may do so for the purpose of affirming it. 

Kesler v. Myers, 41 Ind. 543.

ERROR-HOW WAIVED. 

1. As to objections. By not objecting to the thing asked to be done before the Court decides. Falley v. 

Gibling, 128 Ind. 110–11, 20 N. E. 784; and by not stating specifically the objection that shows the thing asked should not be done.

ERROR-HOW WAIVED 

203 

2. As to exceptions. By not reserving an exception to the decision of the Court at the time it is made. Burns 

1933, 2—3105; Burns 1926, 685; Baldwin, 455. If, however, the proceedings are ex parte, it is sufficient if the objections and exceptions are taken at the earliest opportunity. Rooker v. Fidelity Trust Co., 193 Ind. 450, 141 

N. E. 4. 

3. As to time. By not doing what is attempted to be done within the time prescribed by law. State ex rel. v. 

Cox, 194 Ind. 380, 442 N. E. 862. 

4. As to request. By asking the Court to do the thing objected to. Dawson v. Shirk, 102 Ind. 184, 1 N. E. 292. 

5. As to reasons. By failing to give proper reason for objecting to the matter for decision. Decker v. 

Mahoney, 64 App. 500, 116 N. E. 57. 

6. As to amendment. By amending the pleading. 

Earp v. Board, 36 Ind. 470. 

7. As to statutory requirements. By not fully complying with the statutory requirement authorizing a thing to be done. Walker v. Johnson, 6 App. 600, 33 N. E. 267. 

8. As to defects in a pleading. By failing to point out in a memorandum to a demurrer to a pleading the defect in it, Burns 1933, 2-1007; Burns 1926, 362; Baldwin, 111-6th. Burns 1933, 2-1014; Burns 1926, 369; Baldwin, 118. Harrold v. Whistler, 60 App. 504. Burns 1933, 2-1026; Burns 1926, 381; Baldwin, 130; Pittsburgh, etc. v. Home Ins. Co., 183 Ind. 355, 108 N. E. 525. 

9. As to evidence. By introducing the same kind of evidence as that objected to. Gaff v. Greer, 88 Ind. 128. 

Hill v. State, 194 Ind. 688, 141 N. E. 639. 

10. As to pleadings. By failing to demur. Burns 

1933, 2—1011; Burns 1926, 366; Baldwin, 115. 

11. As to process. By entering a general appearance when the process is defective. N. A. & C. Co. v. Combs, 13 Ind. 490, but a special appearance for the purpose of questioning the process does not. 

12. As to new trial. By failing to present in a motion for a new trial that which the statute says must be in it,

ERROR-HOW WAIVED 

and to state the particular ruling complained of. Reese v. Caffee, 133 Ind. 14-16, 32 N. E. 720; Kenwood Tire Co. v. Speckman, 92 App. 419, 176 N. E. 29. 

13. As to demurrer. By filing an answer while a demurrer is pending. State v. Harrington, 174 Ind. 730, 93 N. E. 1. 

14. As to assignment of errors. By not making a specific assignment of any error relied on. Burns 1933, 2-3225; Burns 1926, 719; Baldwin, 502; Baldwin v. 

Sutton, 148 Ind. 591, 47 N. E. 629; Mesker v. Bishop, 56 App. 455, 103 N. E. 492. 

15. As to witness. By failing to object to competency. 

Dime, etc. v. Jones, 84 App. 508, 151 N. E. 701. 

16. As to evidence. By failing to object to competency of the same. Hull v. Butler, 7 Ind. 267. 

17. As to verdict. By failing to object to form-and by failing to move for a venire de novo. Hutson v. 

Brookshire, 80 App. 273, 139 N. E. 190. 

18. As to carrying a demurrer back to the complaint. 

By failing to demur to the complaint, the defendant cannot have a demurrer to his answer carried back to the complaint. 79 App. 119, 137 N. E. 562. 

19. As to motion. By failing to make the motion several, and to make the pleading more specific. St. Vincent's Hospital v. Stine, 195 Ind. 350, 144 N. E. 537. 

20. As to amendment. By amending the pleading. 

The Wabash, etc. v. Morgan, 132 Ind. 430, 31 N. E. 661. 

21. As to defect of parties. By not naming the parties. Rich v. Fry, 196 Ind. 303, 146 N. E. 393. 

22. As to new trial for new evidence. By not showing diligence. Shick v. Blakesly, 80 App. 253, 134 N. E. 498. 

23. As to judgment. By failing to file motion to modify. Berkey v. Hascall, 123 Ind. 502.

MANDATE 

205 

MANDATE. 

The final direction of the court on appeal is the mandate, Bouvier. When a cause is reversed in whole or in part the court reversing shall remand it to the court below with instructions for a new trial, when the justice of the case requires it, but if no new trial is required, with particular instructions relative to the judgment to be rendered and all modifications of the same. Burns 1933, 2-3234; Burns 1926, 728; Baldwin, 507. When any cause is determined on appeal, the clerk of that court must certify the decision and instruction of the court to the court below. Burns 1933, 2-3226; Burns 1926, 730; 

Baldwin, 509.

Either party may file within sixty days from the time of the decision a motion to modify the mandate. Pittsburgh, etc. Co. v. Mahoney, 148 Ind. 196, 46 N. E. 917; and the motion may be separate and no part of the petition for a rehearing, or it may be a part of such petition. 

Kastoff v. Meyer Kiser Bank, 201 Ind. 396. If the evidence is all written, or if there is an agreed statement of facts, the court will decide what judgment shall be entered. Nichols v. Glover, 41 Ind. 24; City v. Steam Ferry 

Boat, 35 Ind. 19. If a special finding of facts is made and conclusions of law stated and the Appellate Court deems that justice requires a new trial, it will be so ordered. 

Buchanan v. Milligan, 108 Ind. 433, 9 N. E. 385; but if the special finding of facts is so complete that nothing remains but to pronounce and apply the law, the mandate will be that judgment be rendered on the special findings in accordance with the judgment of the court and that a new trial be not ordered. McAffee v. Reynolds, 130 Ind. 

33, 20 N. E. 423. If answers to interrogatories are in irreconcilable conflict with the general verdict and show the appellant is entitled to judgment the Appellate Court may direct that judgment be so rendered. Brown v. 

Ohio, etc. Co., 138 Ind. 648, 37 N. E. 717; but the court is not bound to direct judgment on the answers though they show such judgment may be directed. If the court thinks

PETITION TO CHANGE MANDATE 

justice requires a new trial it will be ordered though the answers of the jury to interrogatories are in irreconcilable conflict with the general verdict. Cleveland, etc. Co. v. Wolf, 189 Ind. 585, 128 N. E. 38. The mandate must be obeyed by the lower court, though it is stated in general and not specific terms. Cutsinger v. Nebeker, 58 Ind. 

401. The court on appeal has the right to direct specifically the procedure of the lower court. Board v. Douch, 6 App. 337, 33 N. E. 663; and in certain cases the court has the right to order a new trial though none was asked. 

Plaskett v. Benton-Warren, etc., 45 App. 358, 89 N. E. 968. 

The motion to change the mandate should state specif. ically the changes that should be made, give the reason why and be supported by a brief filed in the case and signed by counsel for the party making it. Caption 

PETITION TO CHANGE MANDATE. 

In the above entitled cause the appellant moves the court to modify the mandate in the same, in each of the following respects: 

1. This is said in the mandate (here set it out). This should be changed so as to read as follows (here set it out), for each of the following reasons: a. State the reasons. b. State the reasons. 

2. This is said in the mandate (here set it out). This should be omitted for each of the following reasons: a. State the reasons. b. State the reasons.

This motion is supported by a brief. 

A. B. 

Attorney for Appellant.

There may be occasion for other forms but the forms herein given will be sufficient to enable the attorney to prepare such forms.

CONCLUDING STATEMENT 

207 

CONCLUDING STATEMENT TO THE READER: 

My purpose in preparing this book is found in the introductory statement.

No attempt has been made to state the many ways in which a case may be tried and the record prepared so as not to present for decision on appeal the decision of the trial court.

If there is one certain way to try a case, prepare the record and present on appeal any desired question, that is enough. Any other way probably is a wrong way. 

If this book points out to the student and to the inexperienced as well as to the experienced lawyer, a way by which to try a case, make the record and present to the court on appeal, any decision of the trial court, believed to be erroneous, and to do this in such a way that the court on appeal will decide that question, a knowledge of that fact will be my chief compensation for preparing it. 

The Bobbs-Merrill Company kindly consented for me to quote from Elliott on Appellate Procedure.

I have quoted from Buskirk's Practice without anybody's consent for me so to do. I do not know whom to ask for such consent. The quotation made from this work is so valuable I do not think I would be justified in not making such quotation. 

Indianapolis, Indiana 

September 15, 1934

ISAAC CARTER

Conclusions of Law made in writing, following the finding at the time it is made and when filed in the case they become a part of the record, 60. do not have to be made at the time the finding of facts is made, nor on the same paper, nor then be signed by the judge, nor before a motion for a new trial is made or passed on, 61. if the record shows the conclusions of law and that they are signed by the judge, that is sufficient, 61. an exception must be taken when stated, or as soon as possible thereafter and before any other step is taken by the party excepting, 61.

Demurrer to the Evidence the common law and not the statute provides for such a demurrer, 62. if any material point is not proved, the demurrer must be sustained, 62. if the case is on trial by a jury, the defendant at close of plaintiff's evidence in chief, may demur to it, 62. the demurrer must be in writing, signed by the defendant, or his counsel and filed as a paper in the case, 162. it is not necessary for the opposite party to join in the demurrer, but it must contain all the evidence given by the plaintiff, 62. the party offering the evidence may object to the demurrer because it does not fully and correctly set forth the evidence, stating wherein such is the case and it is the duty of the court to see that it fully and correctly sets forth all the evidence and no other, before it is passed on, 62. the demurrer takes the case from the jury, 62. if the demurrer is sustained, the case is at an end and there is judgment, for the defendant; if overruled, the damages are assessed either by the jury called to try the case, or by another jury afterwards called to assess them, 62. if the demurrer is sustained the party offering the evidence should except, if overruled, the party demurring should except, 62. if the party offering the evidence joins in the demurrer, he admits the evidence is correctly set out, 62. in passing on the demurrer no advantage can be taken of any defect in the pleading, 62. the demurrer admits the facts of which there is any evidence and all conclusions which can fairly be deduced from them, 62. the demurrer, with the evidence, the ruling of the court thereon and the exception thereto are a part of the record and to bring them before the court on appeal it is only necessary to assign as error that the court erred in sustaining, or overruling, the demurrer, 63. the party upon whom the burden of proof rests cannot demur, 63. no evidence offered by the demurring party can be considered, 63. if the demurring party does not ask to have the damages assessed by the jury he waives that right and the court assesses them, 63. if he proceeds and offers evidence without objection, he waives the exception, 63. if the other party objects to his offering evidence, it will be error for the court to permit it to be given, 63. if he does not object he waives the error, and the cause proceeds as if there had not been a demurrer, 63.

INDEX Demurrer to the Evidence-Continued at any time the plaintiff has rested in chief, the defendant may demur to the evidence, 63. also if the defendant admits the plaintiff's action, the plaintiff may demur to the evidence offered by the defendant to sustain his defense, 63. the same rules apply to set-off and counterclaim, 63. form for demurrer to evidence of plaintiff, 63. 

Directed Verdict if there is no evidence to sustain the action, the defendant may at any time after the plaintiff has rested in chief, move that the court direct a verdict for the defendant, and the court of its own motion should so direct, 64. if the motion is overruled and the defendant introduces evidence, he waives the ruling on his motion but may renew it at the close of all the evidence, 64. such a motion asserts the evidence is not sufficient to sustain a verdict for the plaintiff, 64. what must the court consider in passing on it? 64. the motion need not be in writing, 65. if made by the defendant at close of plaintiff's evidence and is overruled, he may ask to introduce evidence, and if not permitted to do so, it is error, 65. if both parties ask for a directed verdict, the cause is taken from the jury and submitted to the court for decision, 65.

Co-parties-How made parties to the appeal, 151. if some co-parties appeal and others do not, it is not necessary, in a term time appeal, to make those not appealing parties, or to name them in the assignment of errors, and they are bound by the judgment, 151. co-parties means parties to the judgment, and are all those in whose favor a judgment is rendered or against whom it is rendered, 151. this applies only to term time appeals and if such is prayed and not perfected it does not apply to it, 151. nor does it apply to those who do not join in a term time appeal but afterwards when it is perfected they assign errors, 151. in a vacation appeal a part of several co-parties may appeal but they must serve written notice of the appeal upon all other co-parties, or their attorneys of record and file proof of the same with the clerk of the court, 151. if any of them are non-residents, provision is made for service on them and they will be bound by the decision, and unless they appear and decline to join, they are regarded as having joined, are liable for costs, their names may be stricken out, shall not take an appeal afterwards and shall derive no benefit from the appeal unless they are under disabilities, 152. parties who disclaim any interest in the matter are not coparties, 152. parties who make default must be made parties to the appeal and so must parties to the record if their interests will be affected, 152,

Trial what is, 116. how authenticated, 118. how obtained, 116. what is it? 39. upon what does an issue of law arise? 39. upon what does an issue of fact arise? 39. all issues of law are triable by the court, 39. all issues of fact which prior to June 18, 1852, were of exclusive equitable jurisdiction, 39. all other issues are triable by a jury, unless it is waived, 39. if the two causes are joined in the same suit, the one is triable by the court, the other by a jury, 39. both may be tried at the same time, 39. if any essential part of a cause is of equitable jurisdiction, all is triable by the court, 39. if money alone is sought, the action is at law, if a specific decree, the action is at equity, 39. for causes triable by the court, see 39. if some paragraphs are triable by the court and some by a jury, what is a proper motion? 40. in all statutory actions, the trial is by the court, unless the right for jury trial is given, 40. the same is true as to appeals, 40. a general demand for a jury must be denied if both kinds of actions are joined in the same complaint, 40. if a cause is triable by a jury, and no objection is made to trial by the court, the jury is waived, 40.

Verdict the verdict is the decision of the jury, in writing, signed by the foreman and delivered to the judge in open court, 80. must be shown by the record, and the amount of money recovered must be stated, 80. if for the recovery of specific personal property, the value of the same and damages must be stated, 80. on return of the verdict, either party may poll it, by the clerk asking each juror, is this your verdict? 80. if any juror dissents, the jury is sent out to deliberate, 80. any juror in a criminal case has the right to dissent before the jury is discharged, 81. the jury in a civil case may be instructed to return a verdict if five-sixths or more agree, 81. by agreement of the parties, the verdict may be sealed up, delivered to the foreman and delivered in open court when the jurors reassemble, 81. if the verdict is defective, the jury may be ordered to correct it, 81. may be received on Sunday, but not out of court without consent of parties nor in court in the absence of the parties or their attorneys unless after notice they do not appear, 81. and so as to re-instructing the jury, 81. must be received by the judge who presided at the trial, 81. surplusages will be disregarded, and are good though informal, and will be disregarded only from necessity, 82. if no objections are made to defects, they are waived, 82. a chance verdict should be set aside but such fact cannot be shown by a juror, 82.
